How to find Richard the dog in Ocarina of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

How to identify Richard and complete the quest

Finding Richard is a side quest in The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake. You might have seen Richard, the adorable dog, during your first visit to Hyrule Castle Town: a white energetic dog that you couldn’t pet because its owner would accuse you of trying to steal her dog. If you visit the town again later, you will learn that Richard has disappeared.

Luckily, you can help the distressed owner to find her precious dog. Below, we explain how to find Richard in the Ocarina of Time remake.

To find Richard, you must visit Hyrule Castle Town during the night and identify it among the running dogs. To know which one is Ricard, you must look for a white dog that will twirl and emit a high-pitched bark when you interact with it. All the other dogs have a deeper bark and briefly stand on their hind legs instead of spinning like Richard does. Since you can only look for Richard at night, consider unlocking the Sun's Song to change the time.

While it is possible to find Richard through trial and error, you don't need to. First look for the white dogs, then interact with them to see how they react. Since there aren't many white dogs, checking each one does not take much time. When I found Richard, he was on the street on the left side of the map, but I can't guarantee that it would always be in the same place for every player.

Once you have interacted with and identified Richard, he will follow you. Head to the street that leads to the entrance of the city to find its owner, then talk to her. After a detailed analysis, she will confirm that that dog is, indeed, Richard, and she will reward you with a Piece of Heart.

If you want to start this quest but Richard hasn't disappeared yet, be sure to deliver Zelda's letter to the guard in Kakariko Village after completing the "Princess of Hyrule Castle" quest. Progressing through the campaign until this point will trigger a change in Hyrule Castle Town and unlock the Richard quest.

Blue Origin's New Glenn launch delay will continue into 2027

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Repairs have been slow after an explosion earlier this year.

Blue Origin made waves earlier this year after a New Glenn rocket exploded on its launchpad in May 2026. This issue during a hot-test caused notable damage to the company's infrastructure in Florida that has delayed it from further activity. Shortly after the incident, Blue Origin's CEO claimed that the repairs would be finished quickly and that the next launch would occur before the end of the year. According to an internal memo from CEO Dave Limp, however, that timeline has been pushed back. The next rocket launch is now not expected until January 2027. Financial Times first reported on this development.

The memo suggests that the launchpad's Vehicle Access Tower has been repaired and restacked, and the launch bridge has been restored. Limp credited weather issues for the fixes happening slower than hoped. "Your collective work on our return to flight has been nothing short of remarkable," he wrote. "In just six months, Team Blue has accomplished what many thought would take years."

The company, owned by Jeff Bezos, is on the verge of notably expanding its footprint in space once it is cleared for takeoff again. Blue Origin won a contract from NASA in September to set up a telecommunications network for Mars. Its New Glenn rockets are also slated to bring more gear into space to support Amazon's Leo satellite broadband service.

Inside the NYC Members' Club Where Republicans and Democrats Party Together

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A half-dozen of us are squeezed into a small service elevator, our various colognes clashing with the signature fish-and-rubber scent of Lower Manhattan. The elevator operator blares Chinese ballads, and the young man next to me bobs his head in appreciation. An older man shifts uncomfortably. “Could you actually make it louder?” he snarks. Over the course of one excruciatingly slow ride to the sixth floor, we’re trapped in our private discomfort or pleasure. But we’re all headed to the same place: a midterms watch party at The Bench, a members’ club for people who are into politics but feel like political outsiders. It’s a place, according to its website, where “restless organizers, creatives, misfits, sneakerheads, and policy nerds collide.”

Since its founding two years ago, The Bench has hosted insiders ranging from CBS News editor-in-chief Bari Weiss to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani. Monthly dues, “just to keep the lights on,” run as little as $50. The space is regularly used for events unaffiliated with The Bench: readings by literary magazines, climate week pop-ups, splashy debates. Michka Bengio, the social club’s owner and co-founder, is an earnest believer in dialogue and, if his programming is any indication, a little bit of a chaos agent. He likes to produce scenarios that underscore political tensions, like when he had Weiss’s The Free Press, co-host a watch party for Mamdani’s 2025 election. According to those who attended, it was a somber evening for the pro-Cuomo crowd, though some of them nevertheless returned to The Bench for this week’s midterms viewing.

On the night of the midterm elections, partygoers had the option of viewing the election results in a spacious, orderly room displaying CNN’s broadcast or in a lounge-y, narrower space screening Fox News’s coverage. In a cozy hallway between the two screens, guests could buy cocktails and popcorn or engage with people whose political views differ from their own—arguably The Bench’s most exclusive offering.

“There’s just a whole splatter of different opinions that come in here,” says David Azoulay, who works with the content agency Fixer Media. “The Free Press, the Manhattan Institute, and then I also get to meet a lot of people who are working in the Democratic space, on the Biden or Kamala Harris campaigns.” Azoulay helped produce events for an “America 250” tour in collaboration with Young America’s Foundation, a conservative youth organization, and has worked with provocateur Ben Shapiro and former Prime Minister Boris Johnson. Though he used to be a registered Democrat, he “grew up” and now identifies as a proud Republican. While Azoulay engaged in a playful back-and-forth with a progressive 21-year-old over what he considers to be the fallacy of socialism, Democratic councilmember Christopher Marte milled about wearing an “I Voted” sticker.

Over the sound of people screaming about the James Talarico-Ken Paxton race, Marte told me he met Bengio 10 years ago through Arena, a New York-based political organization that took off in the wake of the first Trump presidency. “It was all these young people trying to figure out how to get involved in politics. A.O.C. was on the couch, Alessandra Biaggi, all these people who were just curious, post-Trump election,” he says. At the time, Bengio was working as a campaign manager for Michael Basch, a techno-optimistic Democratic NYC mayoral candidate, a gig he secured by asking the only person he knew in politics to introduce them. He’s also worked as a consultant for NBA Africa and managed downtown restaurants like Regina’s Grocery and Miss Lily’s. The Bench is one of four Bengio ventures; he also co-founded the supper club Shtick with his wife, Jacqueline Lobel, and partners with the Hancock Foundation and the Hancock Cafe. On any given event night, he can be found buzzing around the room, excitedly facilitating introductions for his guests.

The son of a Moroccan father and Algerian mother, Bengio grew up in Strasbourg, France. When I ask what motivated him to move to New York in 2001, he deadpans: “Ever heard of the American dream?” More specifically, though, he was jealous of a friend who moved to the States for six months and returned speaking fluent English. “He was the coolest kid,” Bengio says. “And I was like, ‘I want to go to America, too.’ I love basketball,” he says, reorganizing a pile of books so that the edges aligned just so.

Bengio, a first-time voter, is coy about his own politics. Instead, he’s driven by the promise of America “that you can coexist,” he says, and a desire to nurture civil, democratic debate. “I want to be part of the project.”

“We need to really recommit and reaffirm what we mean when we talk about free speech—expression that is unencumbered by political interference in any way, shape, or form,” says Josef Palermo, a former curator at the Kennedy Center who drove up from the nation’s capital to attend this party. Earlier this year, Palermo wrote about the politicization of the Kennedy Center by both the Biden and Trump administrations in an article for The Atlantic. “We need spaces like this in D.C. Everybody’s in their own camps and certain venues are definitely coded one way or the other. I think having something like this would be huge in our nation’s capital.”

Then again, it’s partly New York’s distance from the D.C. swamp that makes talking about politics at The Bench feel cool. The space is decorated with Bengio’s enviable collection of NBA and political memorabilia. (He jokes that he stays up late scrolling vintage sites and bidding on Americana rarities.) Alongside Michael Jordan Wheaties boxes and a slew of rare basketball sneakers, there’s a framed copy of Emile Zola’s 1897 letter to the French youth during the Dreyfus affair, a portable Florida voting booth from the Bush vs. Gore election, a framed paper shift dress from Richard Nixon’s 1968 presidential campaign, and nearly every issue of John F. Kennedy Jr’s George magazine. The one with a Donald Trump cover gets taken down from the wall often, Bengio says, but he always puts it back up.

On Tuesday night, there appeared to be other collectors of rare political merch. Vintage Obama-era crop tops caught my eye, and a speechwriter for a New York City council member waxed poetic about his favorite vinyl record: a collection of John F. Kennedy speeches. Two guys wore t-shirts they designed themselves that cheekily quoted Michigan turncoat Haley Stevens: “Israel cums to me in my dreams.” (One of them requested anonymity, fearful of being cancelled by the Canary Mission, an anonymous organization that tracks critics of Israel.)

A good number of attendees who were professionally invested in the election outcomes couldn’t stop checking their phones. Anthony DiMieri, the man behind Mamdani’s winning social media strategy, told me he’d just come from Brad Lander’s party. Max Altman, the brother of the OpenAI founder Sam, also stopped by. He’s excited about a recent move to New York from Austin, Texas, and if he hadn’t forgotten to request an absentee ballot, he says, he would’ve voted for James Talarico.

As the results turned progressively blue, the room began to clear out. Before leaving, I chatted with Azoulay’s “libertarian friend” Hunt Beaty. In the hallway between broadcasts, Beaty tells me he’s not terribly moved by the midterms. “Everything interesting that is happening in the world right now, to me, is happening outside of politics,” he says. He rejects the libertarian designation and lives by the credo “free minds and free markets,” citing Reason magazine. And yet, Beaty finds that The Bench still has something to offer him.

“It’s fun to be in a room filled with buzzy, interesting people who at least are able to try and talk about ideas, while also being like, ‘Maybe we’re doing coke in the bathroom, too.’ I want there to be a New York City where those things could coexist.”

AWS Kiro aims to solve identity crisis by adding another identity: bot orchestrator

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The cloud giant refits its agentic dev tools for agent orchestration

AWS Kiro, the cloud giant's AI-oriented integrated development environment, isn't the most popular code editor.

That honor goes to Microsoft's Visual Studio Code, at least as measured by Stack Overflow's 2026 Developer Survey.

Kiro might rank 27th among the listed IDEs, but then it's not really an IDE anymore. Having shrugged off reports that it played a role in outages last year, Kiro now styles itself "an agentic development environment" after a brief stint as "an agentic IDE."

It's also the inheritor of Amazon Q Developer coding assistant users, who have been directed to migrate to Kiro.

It's also "an AI-powered development environment that helps you build software from prototype to production." It's "available as a desktop IDE, a command-line interface, a web app, a mobile app, and a personal AI agent (Crew)."

Except that Kiro Crew is also "the persistent, open source development workspace." It's more agent infrastructure – an environment – that supports Kiro Web, IDE, and CLI.

To help get Kiro through its identity crisis and perhaps make it more beloved, AWS is expanding Kiro's remit still further. The cloud giant is launching Kiro Crew bots, recalling similar efforts like OpenAI dots and Grok Bot, as well as Claude Duet and Meta Muse.

Bots in Kiro Crew are software agents that attempt to carry out long-running tasks.

"A bot is a new way to structure and drive agentic work," a Kiro blog post explains.

"Instead of working in isolated sessions, each bot is a named, long-running agent that you define and customize for a specific part of your work. It has its own memory and runs its own sessions so you stop tracking them one by one. Kiro Crew already learns from how you work. Bots change where that learning lives."

There's some appealing anthropomorphism to giving each agent a name and its own tools, permissions, and memories. You're suddenly surrounded by friends.

The possible downside is how that gets billed – there's potential for a lot of duplicated context if your many agents process overlapping data without some form of shared cache.

Given Amazon's concerns about internal token usage and developer complaints about runaway costs in Kiro, it seems other organizations might be curious about the potential expense of keeping bot memory isolated.

In response to our inquiry about the cost of keeping memory separate, an AWS spokesperson said, “All of the Kiro bots bill together through a developer’s existing Kiro plan. Each bot in Kiro Crew has its own memory and sessions, and their learned context is intentionally kept separate so that what one bot learns doesn’t bleed into another’s work. However, all bots operate within the same Kiro Crew workspace, use the same harness, and have access to the same codebase, project files, MCP, skills, and tools—so they don’t need to independently rediscover your project structure.”

In any event, figuring out how Kiro Crew agents are spending tokens looks likely to require analytics of some sort, because AWS Kiro subscriptions are sold as "credits" that get converted to tokens at different rates, depending on the underlying model.

As a thought experiment, AWS suggests a developer might deploy an intake bot for reading trouble or job tickets, labeling them, flagging duplicates, and drafting reproduction steps for bugs.

Then there might be the build-health bot for checking the CI pipeline periodically and surfacing test failures that require the attention made scarce by having to deal with a bunch of AI agents.

A code review bot might also be warranted for reviewing pull requests and other delegated responsibilities. And top that off with a release-readiness bot, for confirming that merged changes have tests and for seeking release sign-off.

For those smitten by the idea of a single surface through which multiple agents can be configured, routed, and governed, Kiro Crew has your backend. ®

Want to Smell Chic and Rich? Celebrities Like Sofia Richie Grainge and Kate Middleton Love Jo Malone London

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

If there’s one perfume brand that virtually every chic celebrity wears, it’s Jo Malone London. It was the scent of the royal wedding between Kate Middleton and Prince William, for crying out loud! But royals (including Meghan Markle) aren’t the only ones obsessed with the brand’s take on quiet luxury perfumes. Hudson Williams dropped that it’s his go-to, as did Sofia Richie Grainge.

The best part about Jo Malone London fragrances is that they're timeless yet unique scents that are made for everybody and designed to be worn alone or layered for a personalized scent pairing profile. And even though the scents are pricey (we're talkin' $155 for 100 ml), you get a great amount of quality product that will last all day long—which totally justifies it, amiright?

It’s got all this A-list love—but which scents are best? I, along with the Cosmo beauty team, have been testing the iconic British perfume line for more than five years. And there are a handful of standout winners.

Now after that sneak peek, keep scrolling for the full list of my favorite Jo Malone London scents—that include fresh, floral, citrus, and woody profiles—so you can find the right fragrance for you below.

"Like taking a walk by the ocean on a cloudy day while wearing a beautiful cream cable knit sweater. Jo Malone London has so many beautiful fragrances, but I have to say, Wood Sage & Sea Salt may just be my favorite. It's so fun to experience a completely fresh take on a beach scent."—JAYNE LEMIRE, EXECUTIVE ASSISTANT

"When I first spritzed this perfume, I expected it to be overly fruity and sweet, but it actually dries down into a powdery and misty scent that I love. Even though its fruit-forward I love wearing it year-round for a fresh and clean signature scent."—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

“If you're a fruity perfume lover, this fresh cologne from Jo Malone London might just be the signature scent you were looking for. It's fresh and earthy, making it an ideal fall fragrance, and the fruitiness leans more juicy than sweet, so it's not overly gourmand. I also find that many fruit scents have an overpowering vanilla base, but this one let's the beetroot and patchouli do the heavy lifting.”—JH

“The name of this fragrance is quite apt, considering it really does smell like gingerbread. It’s spicy, with a punch of ginger and cinnamon. But it’s not necessarily sweet, which I think a lot of people who are sick of gourmands will love. It’s really unlike anything else I have in my fragrance collection.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

“I am chomping at the bit for a lover who will douse themself in this fragrance, then cuddle next to me (while watching The Holiday, perhaps!). A girl can dream! This fragrance is so simple yet so sexy. The vanilla and whiskey pair perfectly together, combining spice and sweetness for a really well-rounded scent. For now, I’ll wear it myself and pretend I’m the LOML.”—BG

“Jo Malone London does florals, and they do florals well—so much so that this perfume has become something of a bridal favorite. The peony is soft and romantic, while the suede feels sophisticated and grown-up. Who wouldn't want to associate this perfume with the happiest day of their life?!”—LIA MAPPOURA, BEAUTY WRITER

“I thought I hated floral perfumes, but then I tried Wild Bluebell a few years ago. Since then, it’s been my favorite floral. It’s so fresh and bright (thanks to persimmon and peach). It smells like running through a field in a Dôen dress, no cares in the world.”—BG

“Think about those fruity scents you wore in your youth: Sweet, citrusy, and, erm, headache-inducing? Yeah, this fruity Jo Malone London scent is anything but. Once you spritz this on, you'll be immediately hit with the smell of fresh pears, wrapped in a floral blanket, and mellowed out with a hint of patchouli. It's basically the most elegant scent profile ever.”—SIENA GAGLIANO, FORMER ASSOCIATE EDITOR

“News flash: Jo Malone London’s Orange Blossom was the signature scent of Kate Middleton and Prince William’s wedding, so if that's not enticing enough to make you wanna try it, maybe its gorge notes are: Clementine flower, white lilac, and vetiver come together to create the freshest and floral perfume that’s truly unique—and, ahem, fit for royalty.”—SG

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

Siena Gagliano is an associate editor at Cosmopolitan who writes beauty and has three years of experience writing about beauty, fashion, and lifestyle news.

Salon is shutting down after 31 years

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Salon says its publishing model is ‘no longer sustainable.’

Salon says its publishing model is ‘no longer sustainable.’

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Salon launched 31 years ago as one of the first online-only news magazine outlets, and it announced today in an unsigned post that it’s ceasing publication.

The company said that the “business realities of independent digital journalism have changed profoundly over the years,” and it decided that its publishing model is “no longer sustainable.” It didn’t go into detail about why, but many websites are grappling with “Google Zero” and the impact of AI tools. When reached for comment, Salon general manager Amanda Wolfe pointed The Verge to today’s announcement post.

“Thirty-one years is a remarkable run, but what matters most is what happened during those years: the journalism, the ideas, the voices, the conversations and the community that grew around them,” Salon said in its post. “Salon helped demonstrate what digital journalism could be and could become. We hope that legacy will continue to matter long after today.”

On Bluesky, Salon chief content officer Erin Keane says that the editorial archive will “stay live for now.”

Current Salon owner Find.co acquired Salon in 2023 and describes itself as a “Performance Marketing Ecosystem transforming crypto marketing with performance and precision.”

Hyperliquid Strategies files to become CFTC-registered FCM, posts $13.3 million in quaterly revenue

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The filing would let the HYPE treasury firm carry U.S. client accounts for HIP-3 perpetual futures markets, pending regulatory approval.

Nasdaq-listed Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR) reported $13.3 million in revenue for the quarter ended Sept. 30, exceeding analyst estimates of $10.6 million to $12 million, depending on the data provider.

The company posted $620.6 million in net income, largely driven by $803 million in unrealized gains on its Hyperliquid (HYPE) token holdings, partially offset by a $172.3 million income tax provision.

Perhaps most notably, Hyperliquid Strategies filed with the CFTC to become a futures commission merchant (FCM), which would allow the firm to carry U.S. client accounts for HIP-3 markets.

"Hyperliquid recently announced its intention to add additional deployer functionalities under the name HIP-3," the firm said in a release. "These optional additional features would allow deployers to build and launch permissioned markets on Hyperliquid's onchain infrastructure. Independent operators would run their own market in a compliant manner, while the chain underneath would stay a neutral infrastructure layer."

The FCM, which is subject to regulatory approval, would allow Hyperliquid Strategies to "play a role" in bringing perpetual futures to U.S. customers within a regulated framework.

"There can be no assurance as to the timing or outcome of the application or regulatory approval of permissioned HIP-3 markets," the firm said.

This comes as Hyperliquid's underlying infrastructure moves closer to the U.S. market. NYSE parent Intercontinental Exchange agreed last week to partner with TradeXYZ to bring Hyperliquid-based perp futures markets to U.S. traders. ICE would operate the exchange and clearing infrastructure, with TradeXYZ designing the markets.

Earlier this year, President Donald Trump said his administration was working to bring Hyperliquid into the U.S. "in a fully compliant and legal fashion."

Simkhai Pre-Fall 2027 Menswear

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As a designer mainly based in Los Angeles, Jonathan Simkhai can’t help but be influenced by the California coast: It’s in his fashion DNA. But if you think that means board shorts and flip-flops, think again. His pre-fall menswear fuses a free-spirited West Coast sensibility with a little more refinement—alternating between structure and ease. “We started with our great wardrobe heroes, and really played with color and fit,” he said.

Simkhai has been steadily building his menswear business these past few seasons, and his new assortment continued its focus on easy sportswear. Classic red polo sweaters were given sailor collars, and paired with his sleek pleated “Elvis” trousers. His blue-and-white striped polos were updated in cashmere-cotton blends that were soft to the touch. More technical windbreakers, a trending piece, were offered in subtle black and white or punchy red—the perfect thing to wear if you own a sailboat, and want to spend a day at sea. (One can dream.)

If things started to feel too coastal, Simkhai roughed them up with a bit of edge. The standout outerwear was a black funnel-neck leather jacket, boxy and with a sheen finish. Simkhai also offered luxurious suede button-up shirts that are great for those not-too-hot, not-too-cold days. His take on a classic denim jacket, meanwhile, was cropped and given a more nipped waistline.

How We Pulled It Off: An East-Meets-West Wedding in Istanbul

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Jasmin Karibzhanova and Sam Sutcliffe are a multicultural pair whose families and upbringings have spanned the globe. Jasmin was born and raised in the city of Almaty, Kazakhstan, and now lives in Toronto with Sam, a Canadian of Chinese-English descent (who grew up partly in Hong Kong). Friends introduced them in 2022 and the couple were engaged in Turks and Caicos in 2024. Quickly, they figured out that anywhere they married would require a long-haul flight for many if not most of their guests, so why not give everyone an excuse to go somewhere thrilling?

It was Jasmin’s father who suggested they wed in Istanbul for a few reasons: Turkey doesn’t require visas for Canadian or Kazakh citizens, there are direct flights to Istanbul from both Toronto and Almaty, and the city seemed like a more compelling place than typical European destination wedding locales like the countryside of France or Italy. Plus, given the bride and groom’s cultural backgrounds, an east-meets-west theme fit nicely with Istanbul’s location and history. Ahead, the couple shares how they planned their June 2026 wedding for 160 guests in Istanbul, a celebration that included a boat ride to a secret destination, a ceremony along the strait, and copious amounts of Turkish delight.

Living far away, the couple knew they’d need a lot of help plotting their destination celebration. After deciding on Istanbul, they first reached out to Turkish planners, but then struggled with the cultural divide. “Western wedding traditions are a bit different than what they were used to, and we had some communication issues with language,” Jasmin says.

They expanded their search to planners elsewhere in Europe with whom they could speak more clearly, and who were well-versed in producing larger-scale celebrations. They landed on Matthew Oliver Weddings, a London-based firm that specializes in destination weddings across Europe and the Middle East. Coincidentally, the team there had recently connected with Aylinly Events, a Turkish planner who’d directly expressed an interest in collaborating on Turkish destination celebrations. Given the serendipity, working with both firms seemed like the best way to pull off the kind of highly-produced event Jasmin and Sam wanted.

From there, the wedding was plotted via weekly Zoom meetings with the Matthew Oliver team, particularly their lead planners Bertie and Georgia, while the Turkish team tended to be most comfortable over WhatsApp. “If you're sending a WhatsApp, they'll respond in 20 minutes at 4:00 a.m. their time,” Jasmin says. “The Turkish approach to hospitality is next level. They’re never off. They work the hardest of any creative team I’ve ever met.”

The couple visited Istanbul twice during their engagement, with the first trip to tour venues about one year out from the wedding date they eventually set. They sought a space that could handle their substantial guest list, and quickly fell for the Peninsula Istanbul, which spans four buildings set right on the Bosphorus in the historic neighborhood of Karaköy.

“It was integrated into the fabric of the city,” Jasmin says. “You could just walk out into the street and enjoy Istanbul, which we really loved, and it had fantastic views of the Golden Horn.” The brand was a special touchstone for Sam and his family too, as the Peninsula has roots in Hong Kong.

Given the hotel’s size, its staff was adept at events and offered plenty of standard packages, though they were happy to work outside of the typical framework. “There was nothing they would say no to,” Jasmin says. “They had a fantastic understanding of vendors in the city and issues to consider, with amazing recommendations. Their whole approach to hospitality and wedding planning was that there is no rejection of any idea. They would just figure it out.”

When reaching out to friends and family, the couple was pleasantly surprised to find how many wanted to fly to Turkey. “I sort of assumed that because it was so far, especially for the Canadians, that we’d have a smaller guest list than we did,” Jasmin says. “But people really wanted to go because it was a destination that a lot hadn’t been to before. It was a great excuse to make their way over there.”

So they felt a duty to properly entertain their loved ones. “My philosophy around the event was that I wanted it to be completely guest-facing. I wanted it to be easy for people to come and enjoy the weekend,” says the bride, who admits she went totally “overboard” in this respect. Aside from creating a wedding website with all the event info, they also offered travel tips and suggestions, fashioned suggested itineraries, and pre-booked a local tour of Istanbul for folks who arrived early.

Another reason they opted for the Peninsula was its location near many other hotels with varying price points and appeals. “Our friends stayed closer to bars and nightlife, and older guests stayed near museums and such,” Jasmin says, estimating that about a third of attendees checked into the Peninsula.

Both Jasmin and Sam have creative backgrounds—they work together on a film production company, Jasmin studied theater and acted, and Sam’s parents are celebrated architects—and approached the aesthetics of their wedding like an artistic pursuit. “We knew that a lot of our guests would be from the creative world, and we were particular about how we wanted things to look,” Jasmin says. They suggested high-level elements like the color scheme (dusty blue and rich mulberry), and then their planning teams, along with the creative studio Event Lab, filled in the gaps with more ideas.

One key element in ensuring the wedding’s cohesion was the furniture production, for which they worked with AND Event & Design Studio to create items like modular custom chairs and a platform drenched in a velvety maroon for the ceremony space. Another was the florals, designed and arranged by Fluida Design. For the eye-popping ceremony, the flowers were lush and architectural, almost alien-like, while for dinner the arrangements were tighter, paler, and more romantic.

“I wanted it to function like a play: There’s a rising energy, then a reveal, then a resolution,” says Jasmin of the aesthetic journey. In Fluida Design, they found a creative partner that pushed them in all the right directions, outside a typical wedding comfort zone. “The initial proposition was a lot more neutral colors, just whites and greens, and then our floral team was like, ‘Well, why don't we just do color because that's just more interesting?’” Jasmin recalls. And as much as the couple cared about the look, they were happy to let the experts be the experts. “We were like, ‘Well, we would like for you also to just have fun with it,” she says. “Do whatever you want.”

On Thursday of their wedding weekend, the couple hosted an intimate but more casual rehearsal dinner for their family and closest friends at the hotel. Friday brought the welcome party for all of their guests, who boarded boats bound for an unknown location at sunset from the Peninsula: it turned out to be the Rahmi M. Koç Museum, a funky transportation museum space along the Bosphorus.

When the couple toured venues, they found that many options were great but a little too similar in energy to the Peninsula. “The last place we went was this museum, which is a ridiculous, magical place,” Jasmin says. “It’s a private collection of vintage cars, boats, submarines, and planes, all cramped together in this hangar-like building on the Bosphorus. It was very kitschy but a great contrast to our wedding venue.”

The Silk Road-themed party packed in loads of Turkish details from the music to the food: carts offering grilled meatballs (or köfte), ⁠döner, and stuffed bulgur balls (or içli köfte) made for a bazaar energy, while a live dessert station formed traditional lokma, freshly fried dough balls drizzled with syrup. “Floral” arrangements were built around local vegetables, like tall piles of peppers strung on garlands, and evil-eye pendants were handed out as party favors.

Sam and Jasmin’s wedding day started out a bit drizzly, a rarity for early summer in Istanbul. One of the reasons they chose the Peninsula in the first place was for its large terrace set against the strait, with the hope of getting married at sunset along the water with boats streaming by in the background. The vision was so beloved that they refused to come up with a rain plan, even for the platform built across the space.

“Our team was like, ‘Maybe we look into other things?’ And I can be very impulsive,” Jasmin admits. “‘No, we're just going to commit to this. If it rains, it rains. I don't want to hide the city from our guests, even if it's wet.’” They delayed the ceremony for a few minutes but got their dream of holding it outside without soaking their families and friends.

Waiting for that area was worth it. “It was a very special space because normally that space is just a herb garden on top of the Peninsula, but we took it over for the wedding,” Sam says. “You both got spectacular views across the entire city and felt in it, while being slightly removed from the craziness.”

The Muslim call to prayer rang out amid a ceremony officiated by family friend and noted Canadian philanthropist Nancy Lockhart, and boats slowed down in interest. “It didn't feel invasive at all,” Jasmin says. “We wanted to be integrated into the city, and that's what we got.”

Following the ceremony, guests enjoyed cocktails steps away in a part of the courtyard wrapped in a romantic mauve pink that colored the curtains, custom loungers, and drink tables. In keeping with the ideas of travel and adventure, the cocktail hour had a hotel theme (an idea that came from the Peninsula’s staff), with staff dressed as bellhops and a wall of ornate golden keys with pink tassels serving as the escort cards.

The wall of keys also functioned to block off the dinner area as it was set up, “so when they were ushered into the next space,” Sam says, “it was a surprising and beautiful new twist for everyone.” Next to the ceremony and cocktails spaces, the dinner “room” was formed by walls of blue curtains and the soaring, upside-down umbrellas that sit permanently on that courtyard. The tables were arranged in long, wavy lines that provided open views to the water.

And while this could never have been planned—even by the considerable talents of the creative couple and their multiple event teams—the risk of rain was rewarded by an incredible sight. “We had a spectacular double rainbow from the Asian side to the European side of the Bosphorus," says Sam. “The rainbow is something I will never forget for the rest of my life.’

While the couple’s welcome dinner was packed with cultural touchpoints, the wedding day itself was more traditionally Western, albeit with some Turkish details. Sam took his groomsmen to a hamam during the day for spa treatments, and the wedding dinner was based around the best of the local cuisine. Canapes included mussel “dolma’’ with black-ink bread, a lemon commape, a bite of Ezine cheese with quince, and a simit bread crumble, while the main meal began with a mezze platter before a first course of duck or mushroom baklava.

After dinner and speeches from loved ones, the party took to the dance floor, where the Uninvited Jazz Band got the party going. The couple served a classic wedding cake in two flavors: one dark chocolate and one raspberry and white chocolate. A series of smaller sweet bites from the Peninsula’s famous high tea were also passed around the tables.

A local city ordinance that event noises die down after 10 p.m. inspired a move off the terrace and into the nearby ballroom for more dancing. The after-party space was blanketed in scarlet, with a black and white checkerboard floor. From there, DJ Live from Élan Artists spun into the wee hours, and the newlyweds poured out a Champagne tower. A poem Sam had written was displayed above the bar: “Silk Road’s starting point / union atop ancient roots / celebratory love.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel won't fill the shoes of GTA 6 as the next white whale, says analyst

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Our special GTA 6 newsletter, with breaking news, insider info, and rumor analysis from the award-winning GTA 6 O'clock experts.

From the creators of Edge: A weekly videogame industry newsletter with analysis from expert writers, guidance from professionals, and insight into what's on the horizon.

This November has been a terrific month for the number six, with GTA 6 approaching and The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel finally out in the open. Once GTA 6 is in our hands at last, we'll have to reckon with a strange new world where we're no longer constantly waiting for the next Grand Theft Auto – the kind of alien sensation that Hollow Knight: Silksong players, who practically grieved yearning after so much buildup, are intimately familiar with.

Will The Elder Scrolls 6 step up to the plate as the next [clears throat] Massive Sequel Over a Decade in the Making That Also Has the Number Six in It? Is it going to fill Rockstar's enormous shoes as gaming's white whale?

"No, I don't think it is," says Circana games analyst Mat Piscatella when I mention this idea in passing. "It's a huge game, obviously, but things like Grand Theft Auto, Call of Duty, NBA 2K, these are games that are huge, mass-market appeal titles."

This reflects part of what makes GTA 6 so interesting, not as a game to be played but as an outlier to be observed. Similar to Nintendo, Rockstar can basically do whatever it wants because its loyal audience is big enough. More uniquely, Grand Theft Auto is the type of game that appeals to a huge range of players, from the enthusiasts playing dozens of the best games of 2026 to casual fans who mostly stick to the sports and shooter series that Piscatella mentions.

"They're their own thing," he says. "It's a different set of rules, a different set of expectations. They're gonna do their thing, and they've earned every bit of that, just based on what they've done. Nintendo is its own thing, too. They are unique, and I know people hate that and go, 'They're competing just like everyone else.' They are, but with the IP and their history and how they've developed how they go to market, it is a unique proposition. That's why they can do things other companies can't, and they have a relationship with their players that other platforms don't. These are companies or developers that are doing their own thing, blazing their own trail, and a lot of other people try to follow."

As AAA game development faces cost crises, Rockstar is making the biggest bet of all, and with the most favorable odds imaginable. Nobody else could make a game like GTA 6. For anyone but Rockstar, it would be too big to succeed. "If you want to talk about a big bet, they don't come any bigger than this, and the appetite for big bets like this doesn't exist in many places," says Piscatella.

Even The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel, follow-up to one of the most-played RPGs of all time, and handily the most-modded, is in a different weight class. (The Witcher 3 and Skyrim have both sold 65 million copies.)

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

"Whether you're talking about an Elder Scrolls, a Witcher, a God of War, those are big games but they're nowhere near the scale of an NBA 2K, Call of Duty, or Grand Theft Auto," adds Piscatella. "No shade to them. They're all great games and perform very well. But we're talking about a secondary tier to those real mass-market hit drivers."

It certainly doesn't help that The Elder Scrolls 6 was announced eight years ago, and re-announced this week with a subtitle, yet we still don't even have a screenshot. The game is reportedly looking at a 2029 launch, which would put it 18 years post-Skyrim, so I'm going to need Bethesda to wake me up for the third time when, and only when, it's got some heat.

Bethesda wants you to "show off your excitement" for The Elder Scrolls 6: Sentinel by spending $35 on a T-shirt

Corey Gamble shared a loving Instagram tribute about Kris Jenner, whom he fondly described as his “lil wife.”

Did Midterm Elections Cause Changes in News World Order?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

And the real winner of the U.S. 2026 midterms is — Steve Kornacki?

More viewers of traditional TV elected to watch NBC News coverage of the voting during primetime, according to data from Nielsen, but the figures suggest some shifts in overall viewership patterns — some expected, and some not. NBC widened the number of hours it devoted to the evening, and launched a stand-alone “Kornacki Cam” that let viewers watch the data analyst on a special streaming outlet in addition to his appearances alongside colleagues Savannah Guthrie, Tom Llamas, Kristen Welker, Hallie Jackson and Ryan Nobles. Related Stories Guillermo del Toro Tears Up as He Says ‘We’re Handing Down a World in Ruins’: ‘It Pains Me Deeply to See Three Generations Fail the Next’ Japan IP Market to Return to Cannes in 2027 as Tokyo Partnership Continues

NBC News’ primetime coverage of the nation voting to give Democrats control over both the U.S. House and Senate won an average of 6.74 million viewers overall, compared with 5.19 million for ABC News and 4.315 million for Fox News Channel. MS NOW, which was expected to see a surge of audience as the nation tilted to the left, had fewer overall viewers than any other mainstream outlet except for CBS News.

In recent years, ABC News and Fox News Channel have typically emerged from critical Election Nights with the greatest command of overall viewership. The conservative Fox News crowd likely didn’t thrill to seeing a “blue wave” by Democrats oust Republicans from their control of Congress, but there’s no obvious answer for the decline at the Disney news outlet. Even so, a special edition of ABC’s “World News Tonight” captured 7.4 million viewers between 6:30 p.m. and 7 p.m. eastern and the majority of ABC stations chose to air local programming at 7 p.m. — before the network’s primetime election coverage began. And the Democratic surge was not mirrored in the viewership statistics for MS NOW.

CNN won bragging rights for leading cable in the critical audience between 25 and 54, the demographic favored most by advertisers in news programming. CNN attracted 1.512 million viewers in that category, compared with 771,000 for Fox News Channel and 665,000 for MS NOW. NBC and ABC captured the biggest and second-largest demo crowd, 2.794 million and 1.672 million.

An estimated 31.2 million people in all watched primetime coverage of the 2026 U.S. midterms across 13 linear TV networks, according to Nielsen, compared with 25.4 million across 12 networks in 2022.

Of the 31.2 million people that tuned in, 57% watched coverage on broadcast networks, Nielsen said, while 43% watched coverage on cable.

TV networks in 2026 vied with a host of new voices tapping digital platforms to connect with audience niches. While both Kornacki, MS NOW’s Ali Velshi and Fox News Channel’s Ruthless podcast team were spotted in these digital channels, a wide range of upstarts and former TV faces — people like Piers Morgan, Chuck Todd, Katie Couric, Jim Acosta, Don Lemon, Scott MacFarlane and Katie Phang — also made a play for the news crowd.

Media executives may sift through the data for all kinds of reasons. At Skydance, recently created through the merger of Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery, management might be tempted to compare results at CNN and CBS News, both of which are now under the company’s umbrella. CNN’s coverage, led by Jake Tapper, John King, Dana Bash, Kaitlan Collins and a host of other familiar personalities, won the demo in cable and lured the third-largest audience. Though CBS News won more people between 25 and 54 than Fox News and MS NOW, it had the smallest overall crowd (though its viewership was up from 2022).

Some Fox News audience members are believed to be grappling with “viewer fatigue,” according to Fox Corp. CEO Lachlan Murdoch, who said as much during a recent call with investors. While Murdoch didn’t say as much, Fox viewers may not want to tune in at a moment in the news cycle when Democrats are on the rise. On Election Night, Fox News gave time to opinion hosts like Sean Hannity and Laura Ingraham, who nodded to some of the problems President Trump’s policies had caused Americans, even as they tried to support his vision.

Major shifts in the political tone of the nation have in the recent past sent TV-news outlets scrambling to match the tenor of the times. Not soon after President Trump was elected to his first White House term, NBC News lured Megyn Kelly from Fox News Channel in the belief more viewers wanted a conservative viewpoint. That pairing proved ill-fated, but if results and ratings from Tuesday night are any indication, there will be room for other experiments in the not too distant future.

At the Races: After the deluge

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

By Mary Ellen McIntire, Daniela Altimari and Niels Lesniewski

Tuesday’s rout by Democrats has led one group dedicated to electing more Republican women to Congress to warn of a “crisis-level decline” of female GOP members on Capitol Hill.

Danielle Barrow, the president of Women for Women Action Fund, drew parallels between this week’s results and the 2018 blue wave.

“After 2018, when Republicans lost 40 seats and elected only one new Republican woman, the party made a real commitment to recruiting and investing in women – and it worked,” Barrow said in a statement Wednesday, adding that women accounted for 11 of the 15 House seats the GOP picked up in the 2020 election. But she cautioned that the party needs to sustain that focus across election cycles.

“Republicans cannot only make this investment after a wipeout election and abandon it when things improve,” she said. “It is time for party leaders to treat this as a permanent strategy, not a crisis response.”

The ranks of House Republican women won’t plummet to as low as after the 2018 elections, when the caucus had just 13 female members. At least 18 Republican women have won House races so far, according to The Associated Press, and two others are leading as of Thursday afternoon. The conference is likely to see two new female members: Florida’s Sydney Gruters, the wife of RNC Chairman Joe Gruters, and Washington state’s Amanda McKinney, who holds a comfortable lead as votes continue to be counted.

Still, those figures are worlds apart from House Democrats, whose caucus will have more than 100 women next year.

“We have always known that when women run, women win,” Florida Rep. Lois Frankel, chair of the member-led Elect Democratic Women PAC, said in a statement Wednesday.

Those incoming new members span the ideological range of the party, including progressives from New York and California who scored big primary wins this summer, to more moderate Democrats who flipped seats in Virginia and Pennsylvania.

House Democrats had set a public goal this cycle to push their female membership past 100. Former Speaker Nancy Pelosi, who is retiring at the end of the year, said electing Democratic women had been her “passion” since she joined the House in 1987.

“We made a decision to make more room for women at the tables of power – and generations of candidates, organizers and supporters have carried that work forward,” she said.

Blue breakthrough: Democrats flipped the House and Senate this week, giving the party one of its biggest nights in decades. On the Senate side, they wrested seats from Republicans in Texas, South Carolina, Kansas, Iowa, North Carolina and Ohio, while finally flipping Maine. Meanwhile, on the House side, Democrats were on track to not just win every race rated a Toss-up by Inside Elections but also pick up seats in deep-red territory that weren’t considered competitive until late in the cycle. Mary Ellen and Daniela summed up their initial election night takeaways, including how Democratic moderates and progressives both cheered the results and why many of Trump’s picks flopped at the ballot box.

Not going anywhere: The top Senate Democrat, Charles E. Schumer of New York, is confident he will be elected majority leader by his caucus, especially after the party’s resounding midterm wins. Several of his incoming members have promised to support others, our colleague Savannah Behrmann reports, but the reality is there’s no real plausible alternative. Schumer’s own seat is up in 2028.

Redistricting rout: Republicans’ redistricting gambit hasn’t paid off as they’d hoped, the latest election results show, with the GOP appearing poised to flip only a handful of redrawn seats. Democratic incumbents who sought reelection from their reconfigured seats appeared poised to all win, although the race for Rep. Darren Soto’s Central Florida seat remained uncalled as of Thursday afternoon.

Taking the chair: Among the bruising defeats that North Carolina Republicans suffered this week was National Republican Congressional Committee Chairman Richard Hudson’s loss to Democrat Richard Ojeda, a former West Virginia state senator. Hudson is now the second party campaign chief to lose reelection this decade after New York’s Sean Patrick Maloney , who lost in 2022 while serving as the DCCC chair.

New senators: The incoming Senate Democratic class includes a returning senator, a former governor, two lieutenant governors and a congressman, a notable amount of governing experience. Meanwhile, five Republican House members are moving across the Rotunda after winning open Senate seats.

Member-to-governor pipeline grows: Five newly elected governors will be departing Capitol Hill to become the top executive of their state or territory, Savannah reports. Additionally, two former members who also served in the Biden administration were elected governor, though a trio of House Republicans fell short in their gubernatorial bids.

Comeback season: Roll Call’s Nick Eskow reports on the former members who are poised to return to the House or Senate next year, with at least seven winning comeback bids Tuesday.

The firsts: Several candidates made history this week with their elections to Congress or the governorships of their state. Roll Call’s Andrew Menezes rounds up the milestone wins.

D.C. in the House: Robert White, D.C.’s newly elected nonvoting delegate, told our colleague Nina Heller that he plans to lobby fellow Democrats next year to support D.C. statehood. A potential selling point: The party could tackle affordability concerns better with two Democratic senators from the deep-blue District, he said.

Keeping count: As of 4:30 p.m. Thursday, Democrats had flipped 42 seats in the House and seven in the Senate, according to the AP. Republicans have so far picked up two House seats, both through redistricting. Twenty-eight House contests and two Senate races (Alaska and Mississippi) remain uncalled as of publication time. Check out Roll Call’s results tracker for updates as the races are called.

Indie losses: Independent Senate hopefuls running in deep-red states did not find success on Tuesday, even in races without a Democrat on the ballot. In Nebraska, former union leader Dan Osborn came up short in his second Senate bid. In Idaho, former state legislator Todd Achilles failed to dislodge Sen. Jim Risch, while Navy veteran Brian Bengs couldn’t topple South Dakota Sen. Mike Rounds. In Montana, where the Democratic nominee did not drop out, former university president Seth Bodnar lost to Republican Kurt Alme. On the House side, Alaska GOP Rep. Nick Begich comfortably leads commercial fisherman Bill Hill, though below the 50 percent threshold needed to avoid a ranked-choice runoff. With votes still being counted, the AP had yet to call the race at publication time.

Not just Congress: The blue wave that broke Republican majorities on Capitol Hill also washed over state legislatures. Democrats are poised to hold new majorities in 12 legislative chambers, including the North Carolina House and Texas House, according to the Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee, although some results aren’t official yet. The party also won governing trifectas in Michigan, Minnesota, Nevada, Pennsylvania and Wisconsin. Meanwhile, in blue states, Democrats tightened their grip on government: In Rhode Island, Republicans are on track to hold just 10 seats in the entire 113-member legislature, while next door in Connecticut, the 36-seat Senate could have as few as three GOP members.

Blame game: As Republicans take stock of what went wrong for them in the midterms, Vice President JD Vance was quick to point to the Iran conflict as an insurmountable albatross. “The reason we lost is the war,” Vance said in a lengthy X post. “If you’d like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so. … But everyone knows why we lost.”

But the tariffs: A pair of Republican former senators, Arizona’s Jeff Flake and Pennsylvania’s Patrick J. Toomey, both also urged their party to focus on the impact of Trump’s tariff policies. “Arbitrary and capricious tariffs directly raised costs for all American consumers,” Toomey wrote Wednesday on X. “Many voted for Democrats yesterday.”

Staying put: Pennsylvania Rep. Brendan F. Boyle says he will remain in the House, where he’s poised to become the next Budget Committee chair, rather than launch a Democratic primary challenge to Sen. John Fetterman next cycle.

Bayh Bayh Bayh: A third generation of Indiana’s famed Bayh family won political office this week, with Beau Bayh’s election as secretary of state. Bayh, a Marine Corps veteran, follows in the footsteps of his father, Evan Bayh, who won the same post 40 years ago before going on to serve as governor and senator, and his late grandfather, Birch Bayh, who won three terms in the Senate. Indiana Democrats had a breakthrough election this week, defeating GOP Rep. Victoria Spartz, winning a second statewide office, unseating the speaker and breaking the GOP supermajority in the state House.

A win and a loss: Nebraska Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts won reelection, but his wife, Democrat Susanne Shore, lost a special election for a seat on University of Nebraska Board of Regents, which governs the university system. Shore, who lost to a fellow Democrat in the officially nonpartisan race, was making her first bid for elective office.

Bans on the ballot: Voters in Arizona, Nebraska and Washington state have approved new measures barring transgender athletes from participating in girls sports. A similar proposal in Colorado also appears likely to pass, although the AP had yet to call it as of publication time.

Roll Call elections analyst Nathan L. Gonzales of Inside Elections shares 15 initial lessons from the midterm elections, from the president’s “political mortality” to candidate quality and the power of personal brands. And, Nathan writes, as bad a night as it was for Republicans, it could have been even worse.

As Maine goes: The New York Times caught up with with Republican Sen. Susan Collins, who lost her bid for a sixth term in Maine. Collins, who had survived tough cycles before, spoke of getting “caught up in the Trump tsunami” and how she finally had her “Margaret Chase Smith moment.”

“These are extinction-level numbers”: Texas Monthly sat down for an interview with former state House Speaker Dade Phelan, who anticipated a down-ballot GOP rout but not the full extent of it. “MAGA needs to be dead,’’ said the Republican, who led the push to impeach state Attorney General Ken Paxton in 2023 and then was targeted for defeat by Trump. He opted against reelection this year.

Corruption a top issue: The Associated Press reported that its 2026 voter poll found corruption at the top of the list of concerns for voters who helped sweep Democrats into power on Capitol Hill.

Tar Heeled: Among the states where Republicans suffered their biggest defeats Tuesday, perhaps none was more stunning than North Carolina, where they’ve so far lost five U.S. House seats and are trailing in one another and lost control of the state House. The (Raleigh) News & Observer looks at how maps “designed to deliver strong Republican majorities [failed] so spectacularly at that goal.”

Support and discord: Michigan Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed’s sharply critical views toward Israel roiled the Jewish community in Michigan. But exit polling suggests most Jewish voters backed him, albeit in smaller numbers than they might have had a different Democrat run, Religion News Service reports.

That’s how many House districts won by Trump in 2024 have been called for Democratic candidates by the AP as of 4:30 p.m. Eastern time on Thursday.

Twenty-eight additional Trump-won districts remain uncalled at publication time, and Democrats are currently leading in eight of those races.

The figures stand in sharp contrast to the start of the cycle, when there were just 13 House Democrats in districts that backed Trump in 2024.

Since then, several Republican-led states redrew their congressional maps in an effort to increase the number of Trump-won districts and improve their party’s chances of holding the House.

In large part, this effort appears to have backfired in 2026, with the Democratic wave cresting high enough to wash over dozens of Trump-won districts. Twenty-one of the 52 Democratic wins so far have come in states that redrew their congressional lines: Texas (six Trump-won seats), North Carolina (six), Ohio (five), Florida (three) and Alabama (one).

Congressional Republicans are holding leadership elections next week as they transition from the majority to the minority party. Speaker Mike Johnson is not seeking his conference’s top post, setting up a clash to be the next House minority leader. On the Senate side, South Dakota’s John Thune hopes to continue leading his party in the chamber.

American Airlines Adds New App Option When Flights Go Wrong—Try For An Earlier Flight Without Giving Up Your Confirmed Seat

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

American Airlines is making it easier to try for an earlier flight when your travel falls apart. Its app and website will now offer standby options when you’re dealing with a travel disruption while keeping your new confirmed flight. You don’t need an agent for this – it’s part of their self-service tool. The change to list yourself for free standby on an earlier flight went into effect this week. During a disruption, the flight you can confirm may be hours later than one you could actually get on if a seat opens up. You no longer need to deal with customer service to get on the standby list. . How The New Standby Option Works American calls its tool Dynamic Rebooking. Here’s the sequence described in an internal company memo: Open your disrupted trip on aa.com or in the American Airlines app. Start with Flight Options or Rebook Trip in the disruption banner. If your flight was canceled and you don’t have a replacement, confirm one first. Choose a flight with availability, then return to Flight Options to look for standby. Look for earlier nonstop flights. Up to three standby options may appear when American can’t confirm the earlier rebooking. Pick one. You can join one standby list at a time through this tool. You can remove yourself or switch to another option without contacting an employee. Keep your confirmed flight while you wait. Your itinerary changes only if you clear onto the standby flight. If you’re confirmed for an 8 p.m. flight and the app can’t confirm you on the 5 p.m., you can standby for it while remaining confirmed on that 8 p.m.. If you don’t clear, your confirmed itinerary remains intact. Employees are told, Do not cancel the customer’s confirmed flight because the customer joined standby If you do clear standby you lose the confirmation on your original flight so if you can’t make it you need to remove yourself from standby. What American Is Actually Adding For Passengers American has offered same day changes and standby through its app since 2017. And keeping your original booking while standing by for an earlier flight was already part of published policy. The improvement is putting standby inside the flight disruption rebooking process. Here’s one example of where this matters: an Executive Platinum member facing a seven hour Dallas–Charlotte delay could see confirmed alternatives in the disruption tool, but couldn’t find a way to request earlier standby. Even accepting a later confirmed flight didn’t solve it. An agent eventually added them to standby and they cleared. I covered American’s new disruption banners in January, alongside rebooking, baggage tracking, and digital hotel and meal vouchers. Standby was one of the additions they said was coming. American also described the feature in its October 26 iPhone app release notes. American is trying to offer more tools for managing disruptions. That’s both a cost savings and also empowering to the passenger. A customer who feels in control of their options is going to have a better view of the airline than one who feels at the mercy of broader forces. It’s about raising net promoter scores when things go wrong, which is part of customers being willing to be repeat purchasers, purchasers of premium products, and passengers willing to pay more to fly American. That’s the strategy anyway. In contrast, in 2024, American restricted most passengers to digital standby even when its systems couldn’t handle their requests. They restored agent assistance in July 2025. Improving digital tools and offering agent help is ultimately the right direction to be moving. The Limits Customers Should Know Nothing is being taken away here, but the new tool has limits: Three displayed choices means one standby request. You aren’t joining three lists at once, and the memo only describes nonstop options. That’s not every possible way to get where you’re going. This doesn’t fix ordinary same day confirmed changes. I’ve written about American’s restrictive availability and routing rules. This update is an improvement for recovery from disruptions, but ordinary standby and confirmed flight changes are still broken at American. United introduced a broader version of this in September: disrupted customers can select up to three eligible earlier flights, and United monitors for seats and texts when one opens. Customers choose whether to switch while retaining their confirmed reservation until they decide. American’s memo allows one standby listing and says the confirmed itinerary changes when standby clears. This isn’t going to change the need for me to work with an agent, but it’s going to be helpful to a lot of people. Plus, American’s Executive Platinum members and higher can have more than one flight in their itinerary confirmed at a time during irregular operations (for instance, I book a backup in case I misconnect when my first segment is delayed – rather than having to make the call early to make the switch and give up my shot at making it, I can lock in the option of flying later while still running for my connection). And bear in mind that you can still ask for a partner flight (such as on Alaska Airlines) if your confirmed option is the next day as well. And bear in mind that you can still ask for a partner flight (such as on Alaska Airlines) if your confirmed option is the next day as well. A customer who feels in control of their options is going to have a better view of the airline than one who feels at the mercy of broader forces. It’s about raising net promoter scores when things go wrong, which is part of customers being willing to be repeat purchasers, purchasers of premium products, and passengers willing to pay more to fly American. That’s the strategy anyway.

In contrast, in 2024, American restricted most passengers to digital standby even when its systems couldn’t handle their requests. They restored agent assistance in July 2025. Improving digital tools and offering agent help is ultimately the right direction to be moving.

Nothing is being taken away here, but the new tool has limits: Three displayed choices means one standby request. You aren’t joining three lists at once, and the memo only describes nonstop options. That’s not every possible way to get where you’re going. This doesn’t fix ordinary same day confirmed changes. I’ve written about American’s restrictive availability and routing rules. This update is an improvement for recovery from disruptions, but ordinary standby and confirmed flight changes are still broken at American. United introduced a broader version of this in September: disrupted customers can select up to three eligible earlier flights, and United monitors for seats and texts when one opens. Customers choose whether to switch while retaining their confirmed reservation until they decide. American’s memo allows one standby listing and says the confirmed itinerary changes when standby clears. This isn’t going to change the need for me to work with an agent, but it’s going to be helpful to a lot of people. Plus, American’s Executive Platinum members and higher can have more than one flight in their itinerary confirmed at a time during irregular operations (for instance, I book a backup in case I misconnect when my first segment is delayed – rather than having to make the call early to make the switch and give up my shot at making it, I can lock in the option of flying later while still running for my connection). And bear in mind that you can still ask for a partner flight (such as on Alaska Airlines) if your confirmed option is the next day as well. Maya Hawke is the first actor to join the cast of Hasbro's upcoming Magic: The Gathering movie.

Per Deadline, the Stranger Things star has been cast in an undisclosed role, though it's "expected to be a key one in the project." The producers also reportedly "made it a priority" to give Hawke the first offer, with the rest of the ensemble cast going through the audition process.

The movie, based on the tabletop game of the same name, was announced in 2025 as the beginning of a cinematic universe of sorts. The movie is meant to be just one of several movies, streaming shows, and "other content" that will tie in the MTG mythos across its 33-year history.

‘In moments of pain, football comes together’: Spain says goodbye to Jesús de Miguel

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Only 3,587 people live in his family’s village but the streets of Navahermosa were fuller yet quieter to mourn the Tenerife striker at his funeral

The narrow streets of Navahermosa in La Mancha were fuller than they have ever been on Thursday afternoon and quieter too. Silence accompanied the funeral cortege as its made way from the funeral home to the parish church of San Miguel Arcángel carrying Jesús de Miguel Alameda, “Demi”, the Tenerife striker who died after an on-field collision with the Andorra goalkeeper Jesús Owono three days earlier. Only 3,587 people live in his family’s village but now they were joined by the game he loved. “We wanted to be close to his parents, his wife and daughter. In moments of pain, football comes together more than ever,” said Luis de la Fuente.

The Spain manager did not say much else; there was not much else he could say, not much anyone could. These have been dark, desperate days, days when it was impossible to assimilate what has happened, and there will be more. “We will get through this together but we will need help, professional help,” the Tenerife president, Felipe Miñambres, said. The club’s majority shareholder, Rayco García, had cried as they left the building where De Miguel’s body had laid in Santa Cruz de Tenerife and he asked the striker’s teammates to be close to his widow Maria, when he had vowed they would for ever take care of his daughter, Clara.

Maria was in the stands with 16-month-old Clara in her arms when, in the 96th minute of Monday’s second division game, she saw her partner chase a ball up the pitch to the centre circle where Owono crashed into him. De Miguel’s father was watching it from the hotel Alameda in Navahermosa, where he goes for most games. His son’s football career had mostly been played not far from there and from the Leganés home where he had grown up – at Fuenlabrada, Inter Madrid, Unión Adarve and Navalcarnero, then Salamanca and Castellón – but now he was 1,000 miles away.

Medical staff rushed on to the pitch. Players stood, terrified, while for six minutes De Miguel was treated there. He was then taken on a stretcher to the ambulance waiting in the corner where, the club reported, they were able to re-establish a pulse. He was transferred to the Hospital Universitario de Nuestra Señora de Candelaria in Santa Cruz, where medics succeeded in getting him to breathe again. He remained in a critical condition but, with his body temperature lowered, he was stable, they said.

With De Miguel’s parents hurrying to find a flight to Santa Cruz, where García collected them, doctors told Tenerife there would be no news until the morning. But at 3am, there was another call to say his condition had deteriorated neurologically. At 8am, he passed away; Tenerife announced his passing at 8.31am. He was 30. The preliminary autopsy pointed to a trauma of the thorax: commotio cordis which can only occur upon impact within a 40-millisecond window in the cardiac cycle. A routine investigation was opened, the focus not just on Owono but the medical protocol applied.

Football is supposed to be a safe space, a refuge, an escape. All those lines about giving your life to the game are not supposed to be literal. This didn’t make sense. The images and the outcome shocked; the contradictory combination of the improbability and the plausibility of it all made it harder still to come to terms with: this couldn’t happen yet it could have happened to anyone. “I am the same age,” Raphinha, Barcelona’s captain, said. There is an everyman quality about De Miguel. He was also a partner, a father. “How we loved him,” García told Cadena Ser. “He was so humble, his feet on the ground.”

“He was a worker, a footballing labourer,” teammate Javi Alonso said. “He was in the AFE, the players’ union, and helped me a lot. He was a great teammate and a good person, wonderful. He was always willing to help.”

He had begun in the academy at Leganés, a modest start to a career at modest clubs; real football. It hadn’t been easy and he had endured injuries too. He met Maria when he was playing for Navalcarnero in the third tier: she lived in one of the houses in front of the small, municipal ground with its astroturf pitch. It was not until he was 27 that he played “professionally”, always understood in Spain as referring to the first two divisions. He took his teams there too, twice winning promotion.

He never played in the top flight and played 40 games in the second division: he was not a star and was not rich. García vowed that his daughter would not want for anything. Fran Sol, a former Tenerife player, started a crowdfunding page for his family. Almería said that their gate receipts when they play Tenerife would go to the family. During the funeral one of the eulogies noted that playing in segunda for Tenerife was his dream, finally fulfilled at 30: this wasn’t the end of his career; it was the start. When they won promotion, he told reporters: “When I started playing as a little kid, I never thought I would live all this.”

An interview he did with Ser Tenerife resurfaced this week in which he said: “nothing in life is easy. There will be good moments and bad ones too; it is a path you have to walk. Making fans happy is what makes you proud; seeing people celebrate goals is the loveliest thing in football.”

Now they mourned him, desperately sad. Outside gate No 9 at the Heliodoro Rodríguez López, fans brought flowers and left candles. So too did the squad, standing in silence. The Spanish Federation cancelled all Copa del Rey games. The Champions League went ahead; Como’s Nico Paz, whose father played for Tenerife, held up a T-shirt in his honour. Messages came in from across the game; minutes’ silences were held. Las Palmas announced that they will wear their third kit this weekend, because it is blue and white, the same colour as their rivals’ shirt.

They would prefer not to play at all. The players’ union De Miguel was part of said it wanted to stop, that it had canvassed footballers and they agreed: they were not ready, their heads not right. But La Liga decided all games will go ahead except those involving Tenerife, Andorra and De Miguel’s former club Castellón – a decision they did not finally confirm until Thursday afternoon.

By then, De Miguel’s funeral had finished. His body was flown to the mainland on Wednesday, the decision made to hold the funeral in his parents’ village, although there will be an event at Tenerife’s stadium on Saturday, open to everyone. Tenerife’s players and staff boarded a charter at 7am on Thursday and then went to Navahermosa by bus, all of them. The presidents of the AFE, the federation and the league came; the presidents of Real Madrid and Atlético Madrid, too. Representatives from clubs across Spain. Andorra’s owner, Gerard Piqué; president, Ferran Vilaseca, and general manager, Jaume Nogués.

The minster of sport, Milagros Tolón Jaime. Pablo Blanco, ambassador of Sevilla, the club that lost Antonio Puerta and Jose Antonio Reyes. Their midfielder Giorgi Kochorashvili, a former teammate, who had asked for leave; the head coach, Luis García, said: “We’re people above all else.”

Before the cortege set off, Manuel Hernandez, De Miguel’s agent, said: “In those difficult moments, we want to thank everyone from the bottom of our hearts for the words and gestures, all the support we have received. We’re very grateful; to feel so much affection towards Jesús and see the impact he made on people is something we will always take with us. Jesus leaves a void that is impossible to fill but also leaves everyone who was fortunate enough to share their life with him great memories.”

Wreaths of flowers from all his clubs covered the hearse as it edged towards the church, following the cross-bearer there, slowly and in silence. There was not enough room in the church for all those there. When they came out on to the plaza again, Tenerife’s players lifted the coffin on to their shoulders and carried their teammate back through the crowd to his final resting place in Navahermosa.

The Best Airbnbs in Asheville, From a Fairy-Tale Tree House to a Victorian Cottage

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As someone raised in the mountains of North Carolina, I've always loved visiting Asheville. I have made the winding two-hour drive from my hometown of Boone countless times, and the city's vibrant energy never ceases to excite me. An eclectic little gem located in the heart of the Blue Ridge Mountains, Asheville’s bustling art and restaurant culture attracts visitors from all over. It's also home to famous historic sites like Biltmore Estate, which is the largest privately owned home in the United States.

What truly makes Asheville so captivating is the city's wide range of attractions, activities, and gorgeous places to stay. Unbelievable mountain views, hiking trails, and other outdoor activities are minutes from the city center, and there are dozens of gorgeous Airbnbs to hole up in no matter what area of the city you choose. The River Arts District is a hub of creativity and expression, home to hundreds of artists, galleries, and expansive outdoor murals along the French Broad River. Downtown is my favorite neighborhood, and I like to take an afternoon to mosey around, with a non-negotiable pit stop at Tupelo Honey for lunch. Wherever you stay, there's so much to do—you can spend your days soaking in a refreshing mountain spring and evenings dining at an award-winning restaurant or craft brewery, then catch live music at one of Asheville’s charming theaters. You will never run out of activities, with something to discover around each corner. Below, find 11 Airbnbs that capture every bit of Asheville’s quirky and whimsical appeal.

We've selected these listings based on Superhost and Guest Favorite status, ratings, amenities, location, decor, editor stays, and previous guest reviews.

With on-site parking, a king-size bed, a full bathroom, and a kitchenette for whipping up light meals, a stay here will feel nothing like traditional camping. Situated about 20 minutes from downtown Asheville in Fairview, this property is an ideal hideaway to unplug while still offering access to Asheville’s unbeatable dining and activity options.
