Seattle startup OneCourt brings tactile tablets for blind and low-vision fans to NHL games

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 5, 2026 at 2:01 pmNovember 5, 2026 at 2:01 pm

Seattle startup OneCourt is bringing its haptic devices for blind and low-vision sports fans to hockey, with the NHL and Ticketmaster making the tablets available free at the league’s marquee events this season and at all home games for four teams.

The device turns real-time game data into vibrations that users follow with their fingertips. For hockey, OneCourt draws on the NHL’s EDGE puck and player tracking data.

The tablets debuted at the Heritage Classic on Oct. 25, and the Washington Capitals, Colorado Avalanche, Chicago Blackhawks and Detroit Red Wings are the first clubs to offer them at every home game.

“Our community has been asking us for hockey since day one, and launching it with Ticketmaster and the NHL is a milestone for OneCourt,” CEO Jerred Mace said in a news release.

The hockey launch builds on OneCourt’s work in other leagues, starting with the NBA’s Portland Trail Blazers. Since 2025, Ticketmaster has offered the tablets at every home game for nine NBA teams, and it backed an NFL pilot during the 2025 season with four clubs, including the Seattle Seahawks, along with Super Bowl LX.

Mace started OneCourt as a University of Washington student studying industrial and product design. His co-founders are three fellow UW grads: COO Antyush Bollini, CTO Andrew Buckingham and CIO Nick Durand.

Time named OneCourt’s tablet to its Best Inventions of 2026 list last month, in the accessibility category. It was one of several Seattle-area innovations on the list.

To The Hospital — John Muir Trail Days 7 and 8

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After a night in the Motel 6, I got up, took the car keys Sam left me, and went to the Northern Inyo Hospital.  It was a sunny day in Bishop, California.  My stomach was still full of barbecue from the night before.

My hospital! (Photo credit to India M. Clamp)

The people at the hospital were really nice.  They checked me in.  I thought I packed my insurance card but could not find it.  Fortunately, I had my insurance card on my phone and was able to email that to them.  The nurse took my temperature and other vital signs.

A moment later the doctor came in, and I told her about my health, and the John Muir Trail, and how I wondered whether my hike was finished.  Then she put her stethoscope on my chest for about five seconds.  I was puzzled, but she said, “Look, we’re going to take a chest x-ray to rule out pneumonia.  But regardless of the outcome of that x-ray, we’re going to put you on antibiotics.  The x-ray will tell us which particular antibiotic, but that’s about it.”

I agreed (what else was I going to do?) and they wheeled me off for the x-ray.  Then, after the chest x-ray, nothing happened for a long time. I waited in my little cubby in the Emergency Room for about an hour and a half.  I began to wonder what was going on.

In the meantime, I went to the bathroom.  I began to feel cold and asked them for a blanket.  Finally, the doctor returned and said my chest x-ray looked okay.  She said, “We are going to give you Z-pak.”

According to Google AI: “Z-Pak (azithromycin) is a common brand-name prescription antibiotic (Zithromax) taken over a 5-day course to treat bacterial infections like bronchitis, pneumonia, and ear or throat infections.”  Sounded about right.

She said, “Go to Duane’s Pharmacy in town.  You will get six tablets.  Take two today and then one a day for the following four days.”  She also prescribed some codeine cough medication as well as cough pearls.

Then I said, “Well, doctor, you know what my next question is going to be, right?”

She paused for a few seconds and then replied, “You want to know whether you can go back out on the trail.”

I nodded and she said, “I’ll tell you what.  Take off today and take off tomorrow.  See how you respond to the antibiotic.  If you’re feeling a lot better, I think you can go back on the trail.”

Sam and I checked out of the Motel 6 and drove south, stopping for lunch back at the Copper Top BBQ.  We each had the same order:  Tri-Tip hamburgers and chocolate cake.  Oh, it was so good!

Right at that corner in Big Pine was a sign for the Ancient Bristlecone Pine Forest turnoff.  Having the free time, we drove 45 minutes up into the White Mountains of Inyo County on the east side of the valley (the Sierras are on the west side).  I had never heard of “ancient bristlecone pines” but these trees are apparently the oldest living things on earth.

Bristlecone Pine (thanks to Wal Saravia and Pexels)

Some of these trees are over 4,000 years old.  They live at high altitude in rocky soil under arid conditions.  These factors make them incredibly hardy.  Some are able to continue growing even with just a tiny strip of bark left on them.

Sadly, the location of the oldest tree of all, “Methuselah,” is kept secret to prevent damage from vandals.  Who would do something like that?  But it is not a hypothetical fear.  According to Wikipedia:  “On September 4, 2008, an arsonist set fire to the Schulman Grove Visitor Center and several bristlecone pines. The building and all the exhibits within were destroyed.”  Appalling.

On our way back down, we tried searching for fossils.  We did not have much luck, but we did find a beautiful yellow flowering plant.

Can anyone identify this? Beautiful in any case!

Back in Independence, we checked into the Eastern Sierra Motor Lodge, where we had stayed before setting off the previous week.  It felt like it had been a much longer interval than that.  Sam’s room was for one night; my room was for two nights.

I started to think about unpacking and seeing what I had in the sealed resupply bucket I left in Sam’s car.  No surprise, I had too much food, though not as much as I feared.  The Mount Williamson Motel (of Lauralyn fame) was across the road and had a hiker box for excess food.  So, it would not necessarily go to waste.

That night we went south into Lone Pine and had a steak dinner at Seasons Restaurant.  They ran out of filet mignon, so I had to suffer through a sirloin steak.  What a sacrifice!  I stayed away from the wine for health reasons.  Instead, Sam and I each had a couple of Heineken Zeros.

Sleeping through the night was going to be the big test of the antibiotics.  I had codeine cough medicine and pearls but decided I would not take any – at least at first — because I had taken my two Zithromax at lunch and I wanted to see how I was responding.

Yes, I woke up several times in the night to pee.  But each time I did, I didn’t cough more than two or three times before falling back asleep.  Basically, I slept through the night.

Sam planned to leave for the Las Vegas Airport around mid-morning and asked me to set my alarm so we could go for breakfast together.  When it went off, I immediately asked myself, “How did the night go?”

The answer was promising.  Yes, I felt weak, but I produced much less mucus and coughed much less.  The improvement was dramatic.

Sam and I drove down to Lone Pine to eat at the Alabama Hills Café and Bakery again.  This time we had eggs Benedict with bacon and avocado.  Yes, delicious.  Then we took a drive through the namesake Alabama Hills, famous as the location site for many old Western movies and TV shows.  Many of the boulders are named for images they (ostensibly) suggest and one or two are actually painted.  You can learn more about this area (with pictures) here.

A sample of Alabama Hills (thanks to Allie Feeley and Pexels)

Then we drove back to Independence and soon thereafter, Sam left.  He was headed home.  I was on my own.  Despite my illness, I really enjoyed our time together and appreciated his kindness during my ordeal.  At the same time, I am very used to hiking solo, so there was no anxiety surrounding the change.

After that I loafed about trying to decide how I was feeling, how much impact the antibiotics were having.  I asked myself, “Should I go back on trail tomorrow, or try to take one more day before going back?”  It was a balance between being cautious with my health and being brave enough to get back out there.

I sat outside my room at the motel pondering my options.

The Eastern Sierra Motor Lodge with my socks drying in the foreground and the High Sierra in the background.

Before deciding, I elected to take a walk through town.

First, I dropped off my excess food and gear at the Mount Williamson Motel.  They told me they would not provide transportation to the trailhead for non-guests, but they gave me a name and number for “Mark.”   They said hikers often hired him for that purpose.

I continued down the road, past a stand selling “miel” (honey), and I fell. My first fall on the trail!  It was kind of weird.

The town’s configuration at street crossings entails bright yellow indicators painted on the ground to show where the crosswalks are.  These are at right angles (depending on which road you are crossing at the intersection) and, in between the two, there is a raised concrete block right on the corner.  That block has no marking on it at all, making it kind of invisible, particularly with sunglasses.  I didn’t see that there was a step.  Suddenly I was just  flying onto the ground in the bright sunshine on the hard concrete sidewalk and trying to protect my head to avoid hurting myself severely.

Although it was a dramatic fall, and somebody came running over right away from the miel-selling place, I was actually okay.  Maybe I bruised my elbow just a little bit.  But how funny that my first fall on the John Muir Trail should occur while walking without a pack in the bright sunshine of downtown Independence, California.

There are two places that advertise Mexican food in Independence, but the first one I visited was only open Monday to Friday and this was a Sunday.  The other was a taco truck pop-up next to the Shell station.  I had two carnitas, one asada, and one chorizo there, all really good tacos with a Jarritos de Mandarina (a Mexican tangerine-flavored soft drink) to wash them down.  It was wonderful.

Since I was still close to my motel, I ordered dinner and breakfast to go.  I got a dinner burrito of carnitas asadas, and a breakfast burrito of chorizo for the morning.  I put those in my hotel room and then headed out to the Eastern Sierra Ice Cream Company.

I swear the pure lime they sell there is absolutely the best.  I tried the orange one, which is supposed to be similar, but the lime was just better, so very tart.  This flavor was especially welcome because it was hot.

The temperature was “only” in the low to mid-90s, but the sun was just blasting down.  No one else was foolish enough to walk around town like I was.

Still, I enjoyed the chance to sight-see and I took some pictures of the town.  It was still the Inyo County seat, attesting to a former dynamism.  My picture of that distinguished building — the Inyo County Courthouse — tops this blog post.  But now the town was just dead.  It was weird.

It felt like a ghost town.  Here we were, in peak tourist season too, so it was very strange.  Though I did encounter an attractive young woman and almost persuaded her to come back to my room with me.  At least she let me take a photo of the two of us together which you can see below.

Back in my room (alone) I took stock.  No, I was not ALL better yet.  But I was much better than I had been and was finally trending toward recovery.  I had taken two ‘Zero’ days and would be falling out of alignment with my John Muir Trail permit if I took any more.  With some trepidation, I called Mark and arranged for a pickup at the motel the next morning at 6 a.m.

“Here I come,” I thought.  “I’m going back on the trail tomorrow.  At least, I’m going to try.  I have a 2,700-foot pass to climb right off the bat, so that will be a good test.  And if I can only make 2,500 feet of that, I can still turn around.  I can still come back down.”

I realized also that once I got over the pass, and headed into the High Sierra, I would be kind of committed to keeping on.  The “bail-out” points were few and challenging.  A couple of days earlier I did not expect to be on my way back to Kearsarge Pass, that was for sure.  But now I was going to try.

There was a lot to do before turning in.  I ate the dinner burrito.  It was enormous.  I guessed my hiker hunger was abating, because I couldn’t even finish it.

In assembling my gear, I could not find one of my canteens.  I looked everywhere.  Maybe it was in Sam’s rental car?  Sam gave me his Smartwater bottle, so I still had two water containers.  But I wanted to keep that canteen as it was easier to fill with a filtering squeeze bottle.

What else?  I took a shower, texted, sent emails, and called a bunch of people:  wife, son, friends.

I trimmed my nails a little bit.  They weren’t too long, but the shower presented an opportunity to trim them now.  Who knows whether or when I would be able to do it again the rest of the trip?

The hair tie holding my ponytail I did not even try to remove.  I accepted that my hair was already a complete rat’s nest and it was only going to get worse.

In my supply bucket was an alternate finger splint.  I wore a splint religiously on trail, trying to recover from a mallet finger sustained on my longest and hardest training hike.  In Independence I threw out the old splint and replaced it with the new one.

I packed my backpack.  That was challenging.  I love my backpack, but it was actually just a little too small for what I was carrying on this trip.  Adding the bear cannister crossed the tipping point.  Somehow, I was still making it work.

I tested my umbrella.  I thought, “If I can get that sun umbrella to work, it could actually help me a lot.”

With my new-and-untested 20,000 mAh power bank charged I had taken care of my electronics (except for the “untested” part).

I even spent a little time memorizing lines for my role in “Almost, Maine.”  Lee and I were slated to perform in that play after my return.

All this activity was exhausting me.  So, I completed these trail notes, set my alarm, and hit the mattress.  As I dozed off, I thought, “I hope my next set of trail notes convey a successful first day back on the trail…”

She and I had similar political views.  Indeed… “Make Pupusas, Not War”

Hi, I’m Papa George! In my 69 years I’ve done a lot of cool things – I’ve crossed the Atlantic in a 40-foot sailboat, bushwacked 100 miles across the north slope of the Alaska Range, hiked for 26 days in Western Nepal crossing five passes over 5,000 meters, and climbed Mt. Kilimanjaro, to name a few. It was my honor to serve my country as a diplomat around the world for 34 years. I have been active in retirement, and I hiked the Wonderland Trail in 2024 and the Long Trail in 2025. This year I'm trying... the John Muir Trail!

'We got it done!' Spacewalking astronauts finish installing next-gen ISS solar arrays

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The first iROSA arrays were deployed in 2021.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Just as NASA is preparing to demonstrate "Max Power" on the ground, two astronauts on a spacewalk have provided the International Space Station (ISS) with the same.

Expedition 75 flight engineers Anil Menon of NASA and Joshua Kutryk of the Canadian Space Agency installed and deployed the eighth and final ISS Roll-Out Solar Array (iROSA) on Thursday (Nov. 5), augmenting the space station's legacy solar array wings to produce as much as 30% more electricity.

"We got it done!" said Menon at the end of the 6-hour-and-59-minute spacewalk. The EVA (extravehicular activity) began at 8:36 a.m. EST and ended at 3:35 p.m. EST (1336 to 2035 GMT).

Repeating many of the same tasks as they did on an Oct. 29 spacewalk that added the penultimate iROSA, Menon and Kutryk worked this time on the starboard side of the station's backbone truss on the 3B power channel. The two retrieved the 750-pound (340 kilograms) iROSA assembly from a temporary stowage platform, carried it to the worksite by way of the Canadarm2 robotic arm and mounted it to its previously assembled bracket kit.

Menon then attached the cables to tie the iROSA into the station's systems but ran into a problem.

"We are seeing half of the expected shunt current that we want," radioed Nichole Stilwell, a NASA astronaut serving as the spacewalk officer in Mission Control. "In this current config, we are not necessarily going to deploy."

To address the issue, the connectors needed to be inspected. Out of safety concerns — given that the arrays generate electricity whenever lit by the sun — the astronauts needed to wait for the space station to enter the shadow of Earth to see what could be done. In the interim, they spent the time cleaning up their worksite and returning the Canadarm2 to its park site as they would have done after the deployment.

Fortunately, they were able to clear the issue.

"We are seeing good shunt currents on the ground, so we are go for deploy," said Stilwell.

After releasing two bolts, Kutryk and Menon watched as the array started to unfurl to its full 63-foot (19 meters) length. No motor was needed; the iROSA, as designed, used only the potential energy stored by its rolled-up carbon composite booms to extend itself. The entire process took about 10 minutes.

"Today marks the completion of not just one solar array but, over 15 years after its development, eight arrays," said Menon at the close of the spacewalk. "These solar arrays are incredible. Half the size of the old ones and bringing 40% more power."

Each of the eight iROSAs and their mounting assemblies were delivered to the space station by SpaceX Dragon cargo spacecraft. Beginning in June 2021, four teams of two astronauts did as was done on Thursday to deploy each array. In addition to Kutryk and Menon's work on the 2A and 3B power channels, the spacewalkers included Shane Kimbrough of NASA and Thomas Pesquet of the European Space Agency for the 2B and 4B channels; Josh Cassada and Frank Rubio, both with NASA, on 3A and 4A in December 2022; and NASA's Steve Bowen and Woody Hoburg on 1A and 1B in June 2023.

The need for the iROSA augmentations was discovered by flight controllers when the space station's eight original arrays began to show degraded power output as they exceeded their 15-year design life. The iROSAs provide the additional electricity needed to support ISS operations through its retirement and controlled deorbit, which are currently scheduled to occur in late 2030.

"Congratulations to you all on your stellar work to finish the last of the roll-out solar arrays and the upgrades to the ISS power system that will take it to the end of its journey," said Stilwell, addressing Menon and Kutryk. "It is fitting that we just surpassed 26 years of constant human presence on the International Space Station, and you all showed us what the best of humanity looks like executing one of the most difficult and dangerous [things] humans do and making it look easy. Congratulations!"

Menon has now logged 33 hours and 30 minutes on four spacewalks, while Kutryk has tailed 14 hours and 10 minutes on two EVAs. Thursday's spacewalk was the 101st U.S. EVA at the International Space Station without a space shuttle being present and the 286th overall in support of ISS assembly, maintenance and upgrades. It was the ninth spacewalk of 2026 and the sixth conducted by the station's 75th resident crew.

Robert Pearlman is a space historian, journalist and the founder and editor of collectSPACE.com, a daily news publication and community devoted to space history with a particular focus on how and where space exploration intersects with pop culture. Pearlman is also a contributing writer for Space.com and co-author of "Space Stations: The Art, Science, and Reality of Working in Space” published by Smithsonian Books in 2018.In 2009, he was inducted into the U.S. Space Camp Hall of Fame in Huntsville, Alabama. In 2021, he was honored by the American Astronautical Society with the Ordway Award for Sustained Excellence in Spaceflight History. In 2023, the National Space Club Florida Committee recognized Pearlman with the Kolcum News and Communications Award for excellence in telling the space story along the Space Coast and throughout the world.

It’s Great To Be A Cow In World Of Warcraft: Forever

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

World of WarcraftWorld of Warcraft: Forever

Ah, Mulgore. The enormous, bright blue skies. The crunch of prairie grass under my hooves. The glorious sunrises and sunsets. The shimmer of Stonebull Lake and the awesome sight of Thunder Bluff’s great plateaus looming in the distance. It’s great to be a tauren!

Every race in World of Warcraft gets a couple of special abilities unique to their people. What these abilities are has varied over the years as the game was rebalanced in various ways. One racial ability that, for years, was never used, was called Plainsrunning. It was an ability unique to the tauren, a race of humanoids with cow-like features. In the original beta version of WoW, tauren were the one race that did not receive mounts to ride on at level 40, likely because they were kind of big and bulky characters that didn’t fit nicely on a horse. Instead, they received a quest to learn plainsrunning, which let them run real fast, equivalent to how fast a mount would move. At some point, it was decided that this wasn’t an amazing idea, the Plainsrunning racial was removed, and tauren were given sick-ass kodos to ride instead.

Plainsrunning was eventually forgotten, becoming nothing more than an interesting historical fragment of data, until very, very recently, when it was reintroduced in WoW: Forever‘s beta. This new version of Plainsrunning worked a little bit differently, and let tauren slowly build up running speed over time as they moved, as long as they didn’t stop or engage in combat. So while not making much difference over short distances, it could theoretically build up and eventually give tauren a real nice top speed for getting around the world, particularly at lower levels.

But then, after the beta shut down and in the weeks before the game actually launched, Blizzard changed something. It freakin’Â buffed Plainsrunning, bringing it to a cap of 60-percent movement speed increase.

Guys, that’sÂ so fast.Â That’s as fast as an apprentice-level mount, which you don’t get until level 40. This is available for tauren at level 1. Tauren right now are zooming across Mulgore. You just see one go by, wa-pow! There and gone in a flash.

Now, granted, it takes some time to build up to 60 percent. You get a stack of Plainsrunning every few seconds, and each stack is worth a 2-percent increase, to a cap of 30 stacks. So you gotta run for a while, unimpeded, without fighting or stopping to pick a Peacebloom, in order to get that sweet, sweet 60 percent. But let me tell you something. Classic WoW? Lotta running. Lotta running long, long distances, across entire zones and continents. In fact, one of the things people love about older versions of World of WarcraftÂ is the slower pace. But Plainsrunning creeps up to max speed so gradually that it gives you the fun of moving quick without detracting from that in any way. Even with all the new fast-travel additions in Forever, you’re still going to be doing a lot of hoofing it, literally if you’re a tauren. So being able to slam that Num Lock down, kick back, and (eventually) go fast? What a dream.

I get asked some version of the same question all the time: what’s the best travel credit card to actually keep in your wallet?

For me, the answer has changed over time. As my travel habits have evolved, so has the mix of cards I rely on. Some help me earn points quickly, others offset travel costs with built-in credits, and a few simply make traveling more comfortable.

While getting outsize value from a travel credit card often means paying an annual fee, I've found that the right perks can more than justify the cost. And one of the cards I'm looking to add to my wallet next doesn't charge an annual fee at all.

These are the travel credit cards I keep coming back to — and in some cases, the ones I'm planning to add next — based on their earning potential, flexibility and benefits I actually find useful.

I chose these cards based on the value they can provide travelers. In particular, I considered:

I've also included cards for different types of travelers, from beginners and those seeking premium perks to airline and hotel loyalists and travelers who don't want to pay an annual fee.

Purchases that qualify for the $300 annual travel credit will not earn points.

*Welcome offer value is an estimated value based on TPG's November 2026 valuations and is not provided by the issuer.

The newest premium travel card in my wallet is the Chase Sapphire Reserve, and after just a few months, it's already become one of my favorites.

While the $795 annual fee is steep, I've been getting plenty of use out of the card's travel perks. One of my favorite benefits so far has been the up to $500 in annual credits for The Edit, Chase’s curated hotel booking platform (issued as two credits of up to $250 per calendar year for stays of two nights or more).

I recently used one of those credits for a luxury stay at At Six in Stockholm, where I also enjoyed some of the other perks when you book with The Edit, such as complimentary breakfast for two and a $100 property credit. The hotel's impressive breakfast spread was a particular highlight, and I was able to use the property credit to cover a couple of dine-in meals.

Lounge access has also been a major benefit. I frequently fly through Dulles International Airport (IAD) when visiting family on the East Coast, and the Etihad Lounge, which is part of the Chase Sapphire Lounge network, has become one of my go-to spots to relax before a flight.

I've also booked other hotel stays through Chase Travel, where the card earns 8 points per dollar spent on eligible purchases. Between the hotel benefits, lounge access and $300 annual travel credit, I've found plenty of ways to put the Reserve to work in a short period of time.

To learn more, read our full review of the Chase Sapphire Reserve.

Related: Chase Sapphire Reserve: Earn 100,000 valuable points

The Sapphire Preferred was the first travel card in my wallet — and that goes for a lot of us here at TPG. It’s also the card I recommend most often to beginners or casual travelers who want flexible points without a sky-high annual fee.

With beginner-friendly perks like a $100 annual hotel credit and strong travel protections, it delivers real value without overwhelming complexity.

The card also makes it easy to get quick, upfront value. After earning the welcome offer and maximizing bonus categories — like 3 points per dollar spent on dining and 2 points per dollar spent on travel purchased outside the Chase Travel portal — I was able to build a sizable stash of points within a few months.

I ultimately redeemed those points for a trip through four European countries, which felt like a pretty great return for my first year with the card.

I'm also particularly excited about the card's newer earning category for vacation rentals. As someone who's stayed in more than 60 Airbnbs, I love that I can now earn 3 points per dollar spent on eligible vacation home rentals, including Airbnb bookings.

It’s the travel card I recommend over and over again to friends and family because of its ease of use, strong earning potential and outsize value relative to its modest $95 annual fee.

To learn more, read our full review of the Chase Sapphire Preferred.

Related: 6 ways to maximize the Chase Sapphire Preferred as a beginner

Best for: Straightforward premium rewards

Another top contender in the premium space — and one that’s firmly earned a place in my wallet — is the Capital One Venture X Rewards Card. For me, its biggest selling point is how easy it is to get value from the card without having to overthink my spending.

Part of the reason I added the card was to diversify my points and miles beyond Chase Ultimate Rewards. Up until recently, I’d been almost exclusively earning Chase points, but as I’ve gotten deeper into the points and miles world, I’ve realized how valuable it is to have access to multiple transferable currencies and transfer partners.

At the same time, the Venture X fits naturally into my everyday spending habits. Cardholders earn a flat 2 miles per dollar on nearly every purchase, which removes the need to track bonus categories or optimize every swipe. It’s become my go-to catch-all card and the one I reach for whenever a purchase doesn’t neatly fit into a bonus category on another card.

I've also gotten great use out of its travel benefits. I recently used the card's $300 annual Capital One Travel credit toward a three-night stay at the DoubleTree by Hilton London Marble Arch, bringing the cost down from $1,096 to $796. I also appreciate being able to relax in Capital One Lounges and participating Priority Pass lounges when I travel.

For me, the Venture X works because it combines straightforward everyday earning with travel benefits I actually use, making its $395 annual fee easier to justify.

To learn more, read our full review of the Capital One Venture X.

Related: 5 reasons why I’m applying for the Capital One Venture X

For travelers who regularly fly a single airline — especially those based near one of that carrier’s hubs — a cobranded airline card can make a lot of sense.

That’s been true for me with United, but I think the same general strategy applies to frequent Delta and American Airlines flyers as well. If you consistently fly one carrier, the right airline card can make your travel experience cheaper, smoother and more comfortable through perks like travel credits, free checked bags and priority boarding.

Despite its $350 annual fee, the United Quest offers several easy-to-use benefits that help offset the cost. The $200 annual United travel credit, for instance, has helped offset flights home to visit family. And the two free checked bags for me and a travel companion have already saved me a significant amount in baggage fees.

The card also offers award flight discounts, anniversary bonus miles, priority boarding and improved award availability, which make the card feel like a keeper for the long term.

That said, I do wish it included lounge access — especially at this price point.

Still, the Quest has made flying United more affordable and convenient for me, which is exactly what I want from an airline credit card.

To learn more, read our full review of the United Quest.

Related: I just changed my airline loyalty — here's how the United Quest Card sealed the switch

Not every worthwhile travel rewards card needs to charge an annual fee. The Freedom Unlimited is one I'm interested in adding to my wallet because it could fill an important gap in my Chase strategy without adding another annual fee.

On its own, the Freedom Unlimited earns cash-back rewards. But when you pair it with an eligible Ultimate Rewards-earning card like the Sapphire Preferred or Sapphire Reserve, you can combine your rewards and potentially get more value by redeeming them for travel or transferring them to Chase's airline and hotel partners.

That's particularly appealing to me because I'm working toward building the Chase Trifecta, a strategy that combines multiple Chase cards to maximize earnings across different types of purchases.

With no annual fee, the Freedom Unlimited could give me another way to earn rewards on everyday spending while letting my Sapphire cards handle travel purchases and unlock more valuable redemption options.

To learn more, read our full review of the Chase Freedom Unlimited.

Related: The best beginner Chase card combo for travel rewards

Best for: Hilton perks without the premium price tag

The Hilton Surpass is the newest addition to my wallet — and my first hotel credit card.

For years, I've prioritized flexible rewards over hotel-specific points because I didn't want to commit to a single hotel program. But as I've started traveling more and spending more nights in hotels, I realized I was leaving some valuable perks on the table.

After comparing several hotel cards, I ultimately chose the Hilton Surpass because it struck the right balance between cost and benefits. One of the biggest draws for me is automatic Hilton Honors Gold status, which gives me meaningful hotel perks without requiring me to earn status through stays.

I'm also a fan of the card's up to $200 in Hilton statement credits each calendar year, issued as up to $50 each quarter. And Hilton's large footprint means I'm much more likely to find a property where I can actually put those benefits to use when I travel.

For me, the Surpass is a relatively approachable way to test-drive hotel loyalty while getting useful benefits from the start. As I spend more nights with Hilton, I'll be able to see whether a hotel card deserves a permanent place in my travel strategy.

To learn more, read our full review of the Hilton Surpass.

Related: How I chose my first hotel credit card — and the 4 cards I considered

Choosing the right travel credit card ultimately comes down to how you travel, where you spend your money and which perks you'll actually use.

For beginners, the Chase Sapphire Preferred remains one of the strongest entry points thanks to its balance of value, flexibility and simplicity. Frequent travelers looking for a more premium experience — especially when it comes to lounge access and travel perks — may get more value from the Chase Sapphire Reserve.

Meanwhile, the Capital One Venture X is a great fit for travelers who want premium benefits without overcomplicating their rewards strategy, and the United Quest offers a strong value proposition for frequent United flyers who prioritize convenience and United-specific perks.

For travelers who don't want to add another annual fee, the Chase Freedom Unlimited can be a useful complement to a Sapphire card, particularly if you're building a Chase trifecta. And for travelers who want hotel-specific perks, the Hilton Surpass offers a relatively accessible entry point to hotel loyalty with automatic Hilton Gold status and useful Hilton credits.

You certainly don't need all six cards on this list to get good value from travel rewards. But whether you're looking for your first travel card or considering adding another to your wallet, I'd start by thinking about which benefits you'd use most often — and whether they're worth the annual fee.

Polygon eyes hard fork to recover $6.5 mln in stolen POL – But at what cost?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

In a surprising development, Polygon has proposed a major network upgrade to recover approximately 70.49 million POL tokens, worth around $6.5 million. These were those tokens that were stolen during two separate security incidents involving Polymarket and Binance in October 2026.

The proposal aims to move the stolen tokens from five attacker-controlled wallets into recovery wallets designated by the compromised companies.

5 attacker-controlled addresses hold about 70.49M POL: about 23.86M POL (4 addresses) from the Polymarket incident and about 46.63M POL (1 address) from the Binance incident. The funds cannot move, but nothing in the current setup lets them be returned either.

Polygon’s block producers have already added these addresses to their transaction blocklists. This they did to prevent the attackers from moving the funds further. However, this measure only freezes the assets, and it does not return them to their actual owners.

The existing system also requires block producers to restart their infrastructure whenever blocklist changes are made, making it difficult to maintain these restrictions over time.

Hence, to address this issue, Polygon has proposed a hard fork, which is a major network upgrade that changes the blockchain’s underlying rules.

Under the proposal, at a predetermined block, the POL balances of the five identified wallets would be reduced to zero. The same amounts would then be credited to recovery addresses verified and designated by Polymarket and Binance.

However, for this plan to succeed, the proposal also requires both companies to prove ownership of their designated recovery addresses before the upgrade. If approved, developers will first implement and test the changes on Polygon’s Amoy testnet before deploying them on mainnet.

At the same time, validators, exchanges, and node operators will also need to upgrade their software before activation. However, the proposal has raised important questions about decentralization and user ownership.

Normally, blockchain transactions cannot be reversed or wallet balances moved without the owner’s private keys. But Polygon’s proposed intervention would override this principle through a consensus-level change.

It is an extreme action that should not be reproduced commonly.

This comes as the Polygon’s MATIC was trading at $0.098 after a hike of 2.08% in the past 24 hours.

But with 21Shares recently withdrawing approximately $11 million worth of POL from Coinbase over the past 30 days, including 45.7 million POL worth $4.08 million on 28th October, concerns remain.

What Is Slow Beauty and Why Gen Z Is Ditching the 10-Step Routine for It

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The 10-step routine wore you out, and you are not alone. After years of chasing trending serums and TikTok hacks, the people who turned skincare into a lifestyle are stepping back, and the beauty industry is finally catching up.

Gen Z and Gen Alpha together control spending power projected to top $17 trillion by the end of the decade, and they are redirecting it toward slow beauty, a pared-back approach that trades viral micro-trends for fewer products, mindful rituals and ingredients that actually work. That shift is already reshaping how brands launch, package and price what they sell.

Slow beauty is a whole-person approach to skincare that encourages slowing down, developing self-care and evaluating how beauty products impact the planet and people, according to Elle.

In practice, it looks like minimalist, recovery-focused routines across skin, body and hair. Products get chosen for their mood-modulating, nervous-system-supportive effects, and launches follow the seasons and a narrative rather than the algorithm. Forecasting firm WGSN describes the movement as a return to reduce, reuse and recycle, with consumers investing in higher-quality actives, simple eco-friendly packaging and a simplified routine overall.

The point is not deprivation. Slow beauty prioritizes the essentials that work for your specific skin type over an exhausting, expensive stack of serums you rarely finish.

Mintel’s 2026 Global Beauty and Personal Care Predictions names slow beauty a defining macro trend, with consumers craving “mindful, unhurried rituals” over trend chasing. The Global Wellness Summit has flagged a parallel surge in nervous system regulation content on TikTok, where the hashtag has collected more than 260,000 posts as younger users fold emotional well-being into daily routines.

That timing tracks with a wider cultural fatigue. Buying 10 products to perform one routine, then swapping them out every few weeks for the next viral launch, is expensive and draining. Slow beauty offers a reset.

The spending numbers explain why brands are paying attention.

Those are digitally native consumers who are also digitally depleted. Brands designing for slower, more deliberate experiences stand to capture them.

One brand already built on that bet is Kindred Black, founded in 2014 by Alice Kindred Wells and Jennifer Black Francis. It runs on limited batches and seasonal scarcity, working directly with farm partners for hand-extracted ingredients like lilac enfleurage oil sourced from seasonal blossoms. When the season ends, the product is gone until next year. Francis told Beauty Matter that a venture capitalist would call that a major shortcoming, but she and her co-founder call it their business model.

The practice is less about products than about pace. Common rituals include exfoliation, dry brushing and facial massage. Others reach for gua sha, ear seeding or mindful meditation.

Solana developers propose major overhaul to address hidden risk that could disrupt its network

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Anza CEO Brennan Watt wants a common rulebook for validator software as Agave and Firedancer operate independently, potentially reducing dangerous disagreements over blockchain rules.

Solana developers are considering a new protocol rulebook designed to make independently developed validator software more reliable and easier to maintain.

The proposal, published Oct. 30, comes from Brennan Watt, CEO of Anza, the blockchain engineering company behind Agave, one of the main software programs used to operate the Solana network.

Watt wants Solana to establish a single, authoritative specification defining how its blockchain processes transactions, validates blocks and reaches consensus, rather than relying on rules scattered across existing software and separate technical proposals.

The change could address a growing challenge as Solana expands beyond its original validator software to include independently developed alternatives such as Firedancer, built by trading firm Jump Crypto.

Validators are computers that verify transactions and help secure the blockchain. Although different developers can create the software running these computers, every implementation must follow the same underlying rules to maintain agreement across the network.

Disagreements over those rules could cause validators to process identical transactions differently, potentially creating conflicting views of the blockchain and threatening network reliability.

Watt argues that Solana's existing development process makes it unnecessarily difficult for independent teams to determine exactly how the network is supposed to behave.

Under the current system, protocol changes are proposed through Solana Improvement Documents, known as SIMDs. These documents describe individual upgrades, but the network's complete operating rules are not consolidated into one authoritative reference.

As a result, developers sometimes have to examine existing validator code or reconstruct previous upgrade decisions to understand which rules are active.

Watt said this approach can produce repeated or inconsistent descriptions of existing behavior while making it harder to identify every part of the protocol affected by a proposed upgrade.

Under the proposal, developers would continue discussing potential upgrades through the existing SIMD process, but formal technical changes would be submitted as edits to a shared protocol specification.

Each amendment would describe precisely how the upgrade affects transactions, network communications, consensus and other relevant functions.

The changes would undergo technical review and any required governance approvals before becoming part of the official specification when activated on Solana's Mainnet.

This would allow teams developing Agave, Firedancer and future validator software to work from the same documented requirements rather than relying on another implementation's code as their primary reference.

The trusted state already includes the epoch boundary transition, so it must not be applied again.

A common rulebook makes the target explicit. Starting-state trust and the next epoch remain separate requirements.

This is a draft specification, not an adopted protocol change. It does not establish starting-state correctness, historical replay or continuous operation into E+1. No client reliability gain is quantified.

This is a draft specification, not an adopted protocol change. It does not establish starting-state correctness, historical replay or continuous operation into E+1. No client reliability gain is quantified.

It could also make software testing more effective. When independent validators produce different results, developers would have a common standard against which to examine the disagreement and identify which implementation needs correction.

Watt has already published a draft specification covering 18 areas of Solana's operations, including transaction processing, network communications, consensus, fees, and validator rewards.

However, the draft remains limited to a defined period of Solana's blockchain history and assumes developers begin from an already trusted network state. Further work would be needed to cover subsequent upgrades and establish that implementations remain compatible over time.

5 Airlines Flying The Airbus A321XLR With Lie-Flat Business Class Seats

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Airbus A321XLR is an aircraft designed for thin long-haul routes. By bringing narrowbody economics to intercontinental services, the aircraft promises to open up new city pairs that couldn't be served economically by widebodies. The aircraft is small, and it sips fuel in comparison to even a modern Boeing 787 or Airbus A350, let alone an A330 or 777.

It has already proven reasonably popular for its niche, but even though the economics are great for airlines, passengers still want widebody comfort. A321XLR operators, as well as operators of the older A321LR, are now widely installing lie-flat business class seats on these planes.

While these are narrowbody-optimized models with significant differences from those used on widebodies, they still provide passengers with a lie-flat seat, while newer models offer direct aisle access by using either a herringbone or reverse herringbone layout. As the A321XLR is introduced by more airlines, these are the five carriers with the largest lie-flat business class cabins on this aircraft type.

Iberia was the global launch operator of the A321XLR, having operated the type's first commercial flight on November 6, 2024, from Madrid to Paris, as well as its first long-haul flight from Madrid to Boston on November 14. The airline now operates eight A321XLRs, with no more currently remaining on order.

Iberia uses the type to serve destinations such as Toronto, Boston, New York, Washington-Dulles, Santo Domingo, San Juan, Recife, and Fortaleza. Iberia installs 182 seats on the A321XLR, which is a relatively high number for this aircraft. 14 of these are business class seats, which are based on the Thompson VantageSOLO.

This is a herringbone seat model optimized for narrowbodies, and was first introduced by JetBlue in 2021 on the A321neo as its new Mint Suites. Unlike JetBlue, however, Iberia did not option sliding doors, nor did it opt for the front row 'business class plus' option that JetBlue did. Air Canada also operates the A321XLR with 14 business class seats, and its examples also feature 182 seats in total.

However, Iberia gets the bump over Air Canada in this list because it operates more examples, with Air Canada currently flying five A321XLRs to Iberia's eight. With that being said, Air Canada holds orders for 30 XLRs in total, which means that it will eventually operate substantially more A321XLRs. It will, in fact, be one of the larger A321XLR operators in the world.

Aer Lingus was originally set to be the worldwide launch operator for the A321XLR, but a dispute with parent company IAG over pilot pay led the holding company to allocate the first A321XLR to Iberia instead. However, Aer Lingus would soon begin receiving its own XLRs, and the Irish flag carrier now operates six examples.

These planes complement the airline's existing fleet of eight A321LRs, giving it a total fleet of 14 long-range A321neos with lie-flat seats. Aer Lingus installs 184 seats on the A321XLR, including 16 business class seats. Unlike Iberia, the carrier uses the Thompson Vantage, a staggered seat model featuring three rows configured four-abreast and two rows containing 'throne seats'.

This seat is ultimately far from cutting-edge, as it debuted in the late 2000s and it doesn't even offer direct aisle access. However, the seat remains quite space-efficient, and Aer Lingus also uses it on its A330 fleet in addition to the A321s. The A321XLR is ultimately an improved, more capable A321neo, and Aer Lingus already operates eight A321LRs.

Aer Lingus's A321LRs have the same configuration as the XLRs, while also using the same exact business class seats. The main way to tell the two apart on the inside is the fact that the XLRs feature the Airbus Airspace cabins, while also featuring newer, larger screens. In addition, Aer Lingus installs newer Recaro seats on the A321XLR, while the A321LRs are equipped with the Collins Aerospace Meridian.

Air Canada's Airbus A321XLR skips Premium Economy entirely, opting for 14 business suites and 36 extra-legroom seats on thinner transatlantic routes.

United Airlines is the newest operator on this list, having taken delivery of its first A321XLR in May 2026. The carrier now operates three examples, and it has another 47 on the way. These planes are currently only flying from Washington-Dulles to Los Angeles and San Francisco.

However, they will eventually fully replace United's Boeing 757 operation across the Atlantic, serving thin destinations in Europe with far greater efficiency than what the 757 could do, while also featuring all-new interiors. These aircraft are equipped with 20 Polaris suites, based on the Elevate Altitude. This is a herringbone seat installed at a notably high 49-degree angle.

However, the row pitch of 32 inches (81.3 centimeters) is standard for a narrowbody herringbone seat. In addition, these seats are equipped with privacy doors, whereas the A321neo Coastliner subfleet that will also feature these seats will not be equipped with doors.

This represents the first time that an Elevate seat has been put into service on an Airbus aircraft. United will use the A321XLR to replace its 757s on transatlantic services, but the two aircraft are configured very differently. United's 757s feature 16 business class seats and 160 economy seats.

Meanwhile, the A321XLRs have four additional Polaris suites, 42 fewer economy seats, and a Premium Plus cabin consisting of 12 seats. While the A321XLR is currently only operating domestic services for United Airlines, it will begin flying the aircraft transatlantic in December, initially deploying it from Washington-Dulles to Amsterdam and Dublin.

American Airlines was the US launch operator of the A321XLR, having taken its first XLR in July 2025. For American, the A321XLR serves a similar role as it does for United, but the airline is using its current fleet of 11 A321XLRs to serve both transatlantic and transcontinental routes. United's deployment of the A321XLR on transcontinental services is temporary until the type begins long-haul flights.

However, this is American's long-term strategy for its 40 A321XLRs. Just like United, American Airlines installs 20 Flagship suites on the A321XLR, although American gets the bump in this list as it has more examples. American selected the Collins Aerospace Aurora, a herringbone seat model very similar to the Thompson VantageSOLO and Elevate Altitude.

American Airlines Future Lie-Flat Narrowbody Fleet

United Airlines Future Lie-Flat Narrowbody Fleet

Just like the Elevate Altitude on United's examples, American's version of the Collins Aurora is pitched at 32 inches (81.3 centimeters), and it comes equipped with privacy doors, as is increasingly becoming the standard. Since the A321XLR will be serving thin European routes for both airlines, the configurations that American and United have selected are remarkably similar.

Both airlines have opted to install 20 business class seats, along with 12 premium economy seats. The main difference is that American sells five extra economy seats on its A321XLRs, as United has installed a snack bar at the rear of the cabin, which takes up a set of three seats, while it has also blocked two exit-row seats to bring the total seat count down to 150.

The next-generation narrowbody has an exciting future ahead of it.

Saudia has ordered 15 A321XLRs in total, and took delivery of its first example in May 2026. The airline now already has five A321XLRs in its fleet, and they're currently being used to serve thinner routes to the Maldives, Mauritius, and Europe out of the carrier's hubs in Jeddah and Riyadh.

Saudia was the first customer in the Middle East to take delivery of the A321XLR, and it expects to have all 15 aircraft on property by the end of 2027. The airline installs the largest business class cabin on the A321XLR of any operator, equipping these planes with 24 business class seats. It uses the Thompson VantageSOLO, similar to Iberia.

Current Narrowbody Lie-Flat Seat Models Being Used By Airlines

Reverse herringbone direct aisle access lie-flat

​​​​​​​However, Saudia has opted for privacy doors as well as the front-row business class plus seat, similar to JetBlue. The configuration is also strikingly similar to JetBlue's layout for the A321LR, as both aircraft feature 24 business class seats and no premium economy, while JetBlue's aircraft have six fewer economy seats. Saudia has been growing, and it has an active order book of nearly 200 aircraft.

Saudi Arabia is looking to attract more visitors, which it hopes to accomplish in part by expanding Saudia's operations while also starting a new full-service airline named Riyadh Air. The A321XLR plays a unique but important role in connecting the country to destinations such as Barcelona, where demand from Jeddah Airport (JED) could be low. The A321XLR enables the airline to expand its reach and relevance without committing to an expensive widebody.

Nintendo Hints the Switch 2 Will Keep Getting Classic Remakes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo's Nate Bihldorff says that new games and remakes are of 'equal importance'

This time last year, Nintendo’s 2026 plans were a bit of a mystery. We knew that games like Pokémon Pokopia and Mario Tennis Fever would round out the Switch 2’s first year, but we still had few clues about what would be 2026’s big game for the platform. Now, we know that Nintendo was hiding a big secret during that time. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time would be the Switch 2’s tentpole 2026 game, giving players (and Nintendo’ holiday sales) a blockbuster remake. Nate Bihldorff, Nintendo’s SVP of product development and publishing, knew that would be a very big deal.

“I often have the best time anticipating which games are going to send the fandom into as big of a fervor as possible, and certainly the first time we heard about Ocarina of Time, we expected that to be the case,” Bihldorff told Polygon at the game’s launch event at Nintendo’s flagship New York store.

During our conversation about Ocarina of Time, Bihldorff explained how the critically acclaimed Zelda game fits into the Switch 2’s growing software lineup. The Switch 2’s first year of games was especially eclectic, giving players left-field swings like Kirby Air Riders and Tomodachi Life: Living the Dream. While the lineup was strong, some Nintendo players wondered when the publisher would roll out its biggest guns. But Nintendo was confident about holding on to a system-selling announcement for a little while, because it had plenty of faith in the Switch 2’s initial software lineup.

“We always have confidence in our developers coming out with great games,” Bihldorff said. “There’s one constant that has been consistent through my entire career at Nintendo, and that’s that you can pretty much set your watch by the idea that we’re going to be coming out with stuff. And the beauty of having so many different IPs that appeal to different people is that you can have a cycle of different games that come out. It doesn’t matter how excited I am about Donkey Kong Bananza at launch; there’s always going to be more games that are coming down the pipeline. This felt no different than that.”

While Ocarina of Time was likely to be a slam dunk, it’s a creatively risky project on paper. Players have strong feelings about the original game, which has long been hailed as the greatest video game of all time. According to Bihldorff, the developers were aware of the stakes, but they were more excited than nervous about working on the remake.

“They knew the challenge that was before them when they decided to take on this project, but you can also read the enthusiasm in all of their comments about how excited they got, once they actually got in the mix, to bring this to a new generation of people,” Bihldorff said. “I think that outweighs any concern about ‘I hope we don’t screw this up!’ No, they have passion about this. Let’s get this out. The final product really speaks for itself.”

Part of that excitement comes from the hardware the game was built on. In an interview with Polygon earlier in the day, Zelda producer Hidemaro Fujibayashi stressed that the Switch 2 granted developers the opportunity to realize Ocarina of Time in a way that mid-’90s gaming technology simply wouldn’t allow.

“Now with the new hardware, Nintendo Switch 2, we're able to leverage all of the capabilities and try to do things that we weren't able to do with Ocarina of Time,” Fujibayashi told Polygon. “So I think this gave us an opportunity to really explore thoroughly what that kind of gameplay and game design is."

Ocarina of Time would have been an eye-catching move for Nintendo in its own right, but it’s even more interesting in context of the Switch 2’s 2026. It’s the second major remake of the year for the company, following June’s Star Fox. (Though not a literal remake, 2025’s Kirby Air Riders was also, in many ways, a revisiting of the GameCube’s Kirby Air Ride.) Was it a coincidence of timing, or a signal of a strategic shift for Nintendo as it looks to bring back its most beloved games? Bihldorff emphasized that while original games are still as important as ever for Nintendo, remakes are a big part of Nintendo’s software plans right now.

“I do think that new games and remakes both have a place in the Nintendo stable, of equal importance to be honest,” Bihldorff said. “There are fans of every single IP that we have out there. Of course, having new games in those series is wonderful, and I look forward to them as much as anyone else does, but there’s also a deep satisfaction in revisiting games that were very, very special, like Ocarina of Time… But it’s also important to think about how many people had never played the original, and for them, the remake of Ocarina of Time is going to be an absolutely brand-new, 2026 experience, and one that I just cannot wait for them to get their hands on.”

Bihldorff’s comments paint a picture of Nintendo's strategy in the second year of the Switch 2. The publisher is living in an interesting creative space, where it’s balancing niche games in smaller IP with high-profile remakes that feel like safer bets. That tightrope act seems to be working, especially considering that left-field releases like Tomodachi Life: Living the Dream are turning out to be financial winners for Switch 2. If any move — whether it be reviving a critically panned GameCube game or giving Ocarina of Time a radical facelift — seems surprisingly risky, you can chalk it up to the kind of bravado you’re bound to earn when those risks keep paying off.

Andy Weir says his new sci-fi audio drama 'Exoplanet' is a way to 'revive the old art of radio plays' (interview)

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

"Earlier on in my life, even before 'The Martian,' I wanted to bring back audio-only entertainment."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hot off the tremendous theatrical success of this year's "Project Hail Mary," adapted from Andy Weir's 2021 novel of the same name, the New York Times bestselling author is rocketing back into deep space with a brand new Audible Original release landing on Nov. 5, 2026, titled "Exoplanet."

Weir ("The Martian") is executive producing this ambitious full-cast audiobook drama with author and ace comic writer Benjamin Percy ("Wolverine") for the first in a series of curated sci-fi projects for Amazon's Audible.

The seven-episode sci-fi thriller stars the vocal talents of Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose, and John Leguizamo in a wild time-travel space disaster odyssey that also benefits from Lucasfilm’s Skywalker Sound, which provided the show’s immersive acoustic elements.

"One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone," states the official synopsis. "When a quantum anomaly tears apart the starship Wild Blue Yonder, each surviving crew member crash-lands in a different era on the same deadly exoplanet—hunted, alone, and running out of time."

Ahead of the audiodrama's launch, we sat down with Weir and Percy to find out more about this exciting sci-fi adventure.

When Percy was first approached by Audible about this challenging endeavor, it was part of a bake-off situation where he was competing with several other writers, all jockeying for position.

"I think the thing that set me apart is my understanding of how to channel legacy properties," Percy tells Space. "Having written for DC and for Marvel, I can take 'Star Wars' or 'Predator' or Wolverine or Batman and honor that legacy but also put my own unique spin on it.

"When it came to the banner of 'Andy Weir Presents,' my job was to write my best version of an awesome Andy Weir story," explains Percy. "So I thought about space disasters, big-hearted hopefulness, quippy humor, and a roller coaster plot, and then I nested into all of that a time-travel story."

Writing an audiodrama is unlike the comic work that Percy is known for, but as he explains, it's also quite different from working on conventional shows and films too.

"If you’re watching TV or film, you’re passive and letting it wash over you. You’re taking in the story as a passenger," notes Percy. "But when you're listening to audio, you're invited in with the dialogue, the narration, the sound cues. You’re invited to fill in the vacancies. As a result of that, you become like a co-author in the experience. I was super excited to jump into this project."

The genesis of "Exoplanet" started with Audible approaching Weir with the basic idea, but what they didn't know was that he had already been thinking about resurrecting great radio dramas of the past.

"Earlier on in my life, even before 'The Martian,' I wanted to bring back audio-only entertainment with full-cast productions and sound effects," shares Weir. "I got into listening to things like 'The Lone Ranger' and 'The Shadow' and, of course, the Basil Rathbone Sherlock Holmes adaptations. Also, 'Zorro' was a radio show, and there were a lot of good westerns."

"At the time, I wasn't yet famous or a writer, but I thought I could do this myself. I could write something, narrate it, throw in some sound effects, and put it up online," recalls Weir. "But then I didn't because I’m really lazy. Then many moons later, Audible comes up to me, and I have an opportunity to participate in that exact thing. I especially like the part where all the hard work is done by Ben. That’s my favorite part."

As Weir points out, he's not the one wielding the pen this time around, but he did select Percy to be his champion.

"We got a bunch of different story concepts submitted to us from lots of talented authors, and Ben's was the one that stood out head and shoulders above the rest. It’s a really interesting concept," says Weir.

"It's not just trope sci-fi with an evil empire of bad guys and some plucky rebels. Ben came up with a unique story with a unique angle. Audio is quickly becoming a larger and larger medium. Everyone's on the go, and now for the first time in history we all have computers in our pocket capable of storing multiple audiobooks to last us years. It’s a good time to strike."

"It's a great time to revive the old art of radio plays, and I think this is a great way to do it.”

Weir's enthusiasm for the project is clear, and we can't wait to check it out. And we won't have to be, because it releases today.

"Exoplanet" is available starting Nov. 5 exclusively from Audible for members of its subscription tiers or as an a la carte option.

One crew. One planet. Three timelines. No one survives alone. A sci-fi thriller executive produced by Andy Weir (#1 bestselling author of Project Hail Mary, The Martian), with an all-star cast led by Anthony Mackie, Ariana DeBose and John Leguizamo.

Exoplanet is free for Audible members, but also available for purchase separately.

Jeff lives in beautiful Bend, Oregon amid the ponderosa pines, classic muscle cars, a crypt of collector horror comics, and two loyal English Setters.

Everyone Has AI Now. Your Next Competitive Advantage Is the People You’re Ignoring.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

AI is no longer a differentiator in the workplace. Human computing power (in collaboration with AI) is the new focus.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Everyone is in an unavoidable race to develop, deploy and integrate AI solutions that give their business operations an edge. The difficulty, at this point, is that AI is available to everyone. Yes, one person can use it more efficiently than the next, but as a new generation of AI-native workers enters the workforce, the “we use AI better than the next guy” advantage is shrinking.

Thoughtful leaders should be using AI, but while it can automate tasks, it can’t eliminate distractions, improve conversations or change how people experience the spaces where they work. As this new digital transformation charges ahead, many teams are overlooking the physical environments that quietly shape their human performance each day.

When you step back, it’s easy to see that AI adoption is no longer the game-changing factor it once was. Instead, workplace design is the next competitive differentiator that will help human teams amplify their AI outputs the best.

AI has many functions and the list of its capabilities is growing all the time. It’s hyper-efficient. It’s good at synthesizing data. It’s cost-effective and can scale without much trouble.

But it is still software. It’s controlled by algorithms. It’s trapped in a machine (even the “Cloud” has to run off physical data centers). That means it can’t address many of the physical human needs in the workplace like when it comes to how people operate and collaborate.

As AI advances, the computing power of the human brain is the part of the equation that is starting to become a deciding factor. The speed (or lack thereof) at which humans can use AI can quickly become a limiting factor in its impact.

The struggle is that it isn’t digital computing power but physical environments that have the most influence on cognitive performance. For instance, Harvard School of Public Health found that simple things like better indoor air quality can affect cognition and, by extension, productivity.

Something like office air pollution isn’t a factor that Claude can compute, or Gemini can jerry-rig. It requires physical solutions and a human mind to design, create and deploy.

It’s important to shift mindsets here when it comes to office environments. Buying that air purifier, for example, isn’t just a health benefit carried over from the past few years of safe distancing. It’s a chance to improve the thinking and performance of your team.

That’s your first step. Stop seeing basic office environmental things like better lighting or cleaner air and water as fundamental requirements. Look at them as investments that give you an edge.

It’s essential that leaders realize how the advantages are shifting. Three years ago, using AI when a competitor wasn’t doing so was a clear advantage. Now, everyone has AI, but those with the best human work environments have the best chance at creating a synergistic advantage through the combination of their machine and human elements.

Investing in overlooked competitive advantages like clean air, low sound, better lighting and other workplace design elements can help:

All of these are more than employee benefits. They are competitive advantages that increase performance through environments that are not just AI-powered but are also human-aligned.

The key is finding ways to incorporate human advantages in a workspace that are as low-lift as possible. Take sound as an example. How loud or “noisy” a space is will impact people’s concentration. What makes a space “noisy” is something called reverberation time, explains RPG Acoustical System, which is the amount of time it takes for sound waves to get below certain decibels. The longer it takes, the noisier a room sounds.

Leaders can take this into consideration when designing or arranging workspaces. They can make sound waves decrease more quickly through the use of sound-absorbing elements, such as rugs or fabrics hung on the wall, wood furniture pieces and drop ceilings.

AI in the workplace is about more than productivity. Artificial intelligence is hyper-productive in a way that humans are just starting to comprehend. It can produce at such a prodigious level that, in most areas of work, the need to have more output won’t be the problem moving forward.

Instead, employers should be designing workplaces that support better work. As machines crank up the output in nearly every work environment, the offices with the highest-performing human workers will have the best chance of producing the highest-quality work. This makes optimizing human cognition and collaboration a priority.

It is an advantage that few leaders are thinking about as everyone races to invest more in the latest AI solutions. Don’t stop investing in AI, but start thinking about your human workers again, too. Investing in overlooked areas like workplace design can be the quiet way to give yourself an advantage in the AI/human world that is unfolding.

Everyone is in an unavoidable race to develop, deploy and integrate AI solutions that give their business operations an edge. The difficulty, at this point, is that AI is available to everyone. Yes, one person can use it more efficiently than the next, but as a new generation of AI-native workers enters the workforce, the “we use AI better than the next guy” advantage is shrinking.

Thoughtful leaders should be using AI, but while it can automate tasks, it can’t eliminate distractions, improve conversations or change how people experience the spaces where they work. As this new digital transformation charges ahead, many teams are overlooking the physical environments that quietly shape their human performance each day.

When you step back, it’s easy to see that AI adoption is no longer the game-changing factor it once was. Instead, workplace design is the next competitive differentiator that will help human teams amplify their AI outputs the best.
