25% to 10%: how 18 tech companies cut their 2026 workforces

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Groupon, Canva, Oracle, Meta, and 14 more: a selection of 2026 tech layoffs sorted by the share of staff each company cut.

Picture opening an early-morning email and reading that today is your last working day. Tech workers across the industry have absorbed news like that throughout 2026 as companies reorganize to keep up with the swelling capabilities of artificial intelligence. Chief executives write memos about flatter structures, fewer management layers, and freeing cash for AI investment. Each of those decisions lands on people who depend on a paycheck to cover their bills. Nothing in the record suggests the wave has crested, and more reductions look likely before the year ends.

TrueUp finds that this year's job cuts appear to be arriving faster than last year's. Its count tops 200,000 tech layoffs so far in 2026, within reach of the more than 245,000 it tallied across all of 2025. Many reports place the U.S. at the center of the losses. Workers have started to push back. More than 4,500 Google $GOOGL employees signed a petition demanding buyouts before any mandatory layoffs, along with guaranteed severance. State government has taken notice too. In June, California Gov. Gavin Newsom unveiled a tool to track how AI affects the workforce and said the state was preparing for AI's advance through governance and fresh policy. Layoffs on this scale have turned private staffing decisions into a public concern.

Yahoo Tech keeps a running tracker of 2026 tech layoffs that moves month by month from March through November, with its latest update on Nov. 4. Its entries draw on news reports, company statements, and filings, and they cover far more companies than this list includes. The tracker itself does not sort companies by the share of staff they cut. This list pulls 18 entries that state a company-wide percentage and orders them from highest to lowest, giving the edge to the larger stated headcount when two companies post the same figure. Several of those percentages carry hedges such as "about" or "roughly," and some describe plans that will unfold over months or years. A share of workforce measures how far a cut reaches into a company, something a raw job count can obscure. A small firm and a global giant can report the same percentage while shedding headcounts that differ by thousands.

Groupon announced plans in May to eliminate roughly 25% of its workforce, about 400 jobs, the deepest proportional cut in this selection of 2026 layoffs. The company expects most of those positions to disappear by the end of the third quarter, based on a Securities and Exchange Commission filing, according to the Tribune. The schedule spreads the exits over several months instead of a single day of departures. Anyone staying through that window would work beside colleagues whose positions were about to vanish.

Financial filings put Groupon's worldwide headcount at 1,734 at the end of 2025, according to the Tribune. Measured against that base, the planned exits come to about 23% of staff, or nearly one in four employees. Many employers on the tracker have disclosed larger headcount losses, yet few have proposed removing so large a slice of their own payroll. The company has been shrinking for years. Earlier rounds of downsizing took it from more than 11,000 employees to fewer than 2,000 over the past decade, the Tribune reports. Cutting this deep into an already slimmed-down company reshapes the whole organization, unlike trims confined to one division or a thin layer of management.

Groupon tied the restructuring to one goal, rebuilding the business as an AI-native company. Its stated aim names AI as the destination, a direct connection that several other firms in the tracker sidestepped. Money figures in the plan as well. The Tribune reports that Groupon projects annualized savings of about $25 million and intends to put as much as half of this year's projected savings into AI infrastructure. The company also argued that the margins of scaling local commerce cannot support a "human-bandwidth solution." Read together, those statements make the layoffs both a cost cut and a funding source for the AI buildout Groupon now treats as its growth engine. The word "rebuild" signals an intent to remake Groupon from its foundations.

Canva is eliminating 22% of its staff, a reduction of just over 1,200 employees at the design software company. A cut of that size implies a starting headcount of roughly 5,500. The November announcement makes Canva one of the most recent names in a year crowded with tech job losses. A reduction this deep forces a company to decide which work continues and which stops, and Canva has signaled its answer through how it is reshaping those who remain.

Canva is folding its product, growth, and platform teams into one another as part of the same restructuring, according to reports. The merger redraws the company's internal map while Canva trims payroll. Three teams that once operated as separate units will run as a combined organization. Pairing that consolidation with the layoffs means employees confront new team boundaries and a thinner bench at the same moment.

Canva also aims to pare back "subspecialisation," the same coverage indicates. Fewer narrow specialties could hand those who stay a heavier load of responsibilities. Each worker left may cover ground that a bigger, more specialized roster once divided. The shift favors breadth over depth, so someone who once owned a single niche may end up juggling several.

No AI rationale surfaces in the descriptions of Canva's downsizing, which sets it apart from several 2026 entries that pin the exits on artificial intelligence or on rising AI spending. Canva's example shows that one of the steepest proportional downsizings in this selection came with no AI explanation attached. The changes described so far concern how Canva arranges the work itself. Combined divisions and broader roles point to a company that expects to run leaner, relying on generalists after this round. The accounts leave open which roles Canva will keep, but the direction tilts toward wider job descriptions spread across fewer hands.

ClickUp eliminated 22% of its workforce in May 2026 as the company turned toward AI to raise productivity. CEO Zeb Evans announced the layoffs himself, in a post on X $TWTR.

Evans denied that savings drove the decision. He said the layoff "wasn't about cutting costs," and he described the money it frees as flowing "directly back into the people who stay." His account treats the job losses as a reallocation, with payroll leaving one group of employees and landing with another. Read that way, ClickUp's total spending on people need not fall at all. The same dollars change hands.

Million-dollar salary bands give the remaining staff a concrete target. ClickUp introduced the bands for employees who "create outsized impact using AI," a standard that ties the company's highest pay to a single kind of contribution. Workers who stay now have a stated reason to measure their output against what AI lets them accomplish. The structure turns skill with AI tools from a useful asset into the clearest path toward the top of ClickUp's pay scale. It signals to job seekers what the company prizes before they apply.

Evans' reported remarks center on the people who remain and put no number on the departures. The 22% share stands as the sole gauge of how deep the reduction reached. More than one in five positions disappeared. Without a headcount, outsiders cannot tell how many people lost their jobs or how large a pool of money now flows toward the workers left behind. The gap leaves Evans' reinvestment pledge without a figure anyone can check against later results.

The disavowal of cost motives and the new compensation tier point to a company betting that a smaller staff, rewarded for what each person achieves with AI, can deliver more than the larger one it replaced.

PayPal $PYPL intends to eliminate around 20% of its staff, or nearly 4,800 employees, under a plan the Wall Street Journal reported in May. The reductions will roll out over the next few years. PayPal is the only company in this selection whose reported timeline stretches that far, a schedule that treats downsizing as a long project.

CEO Enrique Lores laid out the strategy for investors in two steps. The first strips duplication and extra layers from PayPal's organizational structure. The second speeds up the adoption of AI and automation across the company's operations.

Lores' ordering puts the cleanup ahead of the technology push. Several companies in this selection name AI as the main force behind their layoffs. PayPal's chief executive began with redundant roles and stacked hierarchy and turned to automation in his second step. His remarks cast AI as a tool to apply after the company sheds its excess management.

The multiyear horizon turns the 4,800 figure into a target. No milestones accompany it. Nothing in the reporting reveals how many positions disappear in any given year or which divisions absorb the first rounds.

Among the four companies in this selection trimming about one-fifth of their workforce, PayPal's number dwarfs the rest. Wix, Monday.com, and Patreon account for fewer than 1,800 roles combined, and PayPal's total runs nearly triple that sum. A cut of nearly 4,800 at around 20% implies a payroll of roughly 24,000 people, the largest base in that group.

Oracle $ORCL and Meta $META are the lone companies in this selection with larger job totals, and both are shedding a smaller slice of their staff. Oracle has carried out its cuts and Meta has begun its round, while PayPal's exits lie ahead. PayPal pairs a roughly 20% share with the third-largest headcount here, a match no other company in the selection can claim.

Wix, the website builder, is cutting around 20% of its staff, a reduction expected to touch roughly 1,000 roles. The 1,000 figure is an estimate based on the company's headcount. Working backward from those two numbers puts Wix's workforce near 5,000 people. The plan surfaced in May 2026, a month that also brought layoffs at Groupon, ClickUp, Intuit $INTU, Meta $META, PayPal $PYPL, and Coinbase.

Avishai Abrahami, Wix's chief executive, laid out the goal in a post on X $TWTR and boiled it down to three adjectives: "we need to become a faster, leaner, and flatter organization." Each word aims at a different target. Speed governs the pace of work, leanness governs size, and flatness points to fewer management tiers between employees and leadership. The job losses themselves deliver leanness, and the push for flatness suggests management layers will thin as well.

Wix also blames part of the decision on a force outside its software business. The company, based in Israel, points to the exchange rate between the Israeli shekel and the U.S. dollar. No other company among the 18 in this list names currency as a reason to downsize. The other companies' explanations range across AI, team structure, profitability, and market conditions.

Webflow, another company that builds and hosts websites, let people go in May in a round of unclear size. A developer told the San Francisco Chronicle the round outstripped Webflow's last major one, a 2024 reduction that affected 8% of its staff. One worker called the cuts "a bloodbath" in comments to the same paper. The tracker points to a shift toward AI as the likely cause. Webflow CEO Linda Tong argued that AI is redrawing the rules for how marketing teams build, test, and refine digital experiences. She added that "the companies that move decisively through moments like this are the ones that come out ahead."

Two website builders moved to shed staff in the same month, a pattern that hints at strain across the category. Wix carries a burden Webflow's chief did not cite, a home currency working against it.

Monday.com is laying off 20% of its workforce, about 630 people, as it pushes to turn its AI platform into a core offering. The two figures put the company's headcount before the July cuts near 3,150, with roughly 2,520 employees staying on. The reduction extends a product bet Monday.com placed several months earlier.

Monday.com built its main product earlier in 2026 around the belief that a growing number of business customers want their employees working alongside AI agents. The July round carries that belief past the software and into the organization that builds it. A company that sells agent-assisted work to clients will operate with a staff one-fifth smaller once the layoffs take effect.

Company leaders sounded confident about AI competition roughly five months before the job cuts. CNBC reported in February that co-CEO and co-founder Eran Zinman said Monday.com had felt no effect from AI companies. Zinman added that the company had reoriented its marketing and advertising around AI. On an earnings call, management told analysts that Monday.com held a strong market position. Executives cited new AI features, among them agents and a vibe feature, built to raise conversion and engagement.

Monday.com's 20% matches the share at PayPal $PYPL, Wix, and Patreon, yet its pace diverges from PayPal's. PayPal plans to spread nearly 4,800 job cuts across the next few years, per the Wall Street Journal. Monday.com's July layoffs come with no comparable multiyear timeline. In raw numbers, PayPal's plan reaches more than seven times as many jobs as Monday.com's. Wix's estimated 1,000 roles and Patreon's 93 jobs fall on either side of Monday.com's total.

By midyear, the AI push had reached Monday.com's marketing, its flagship software, and its payroll. Whatever pressure AI companies did or did not apply, Monday.com has chosen to compete on their ground, staking both its software and its staffing on AI.

Patreon cut 20% of its workforce in July, eliminating 93 jobs as it reworked what the company called "our organizational structure and how we work." Ninety-three positions at that share imply a staff of roughly 465 before the cuts. No other company in this selection that disclosed both a share and a headcount comes out smaller. Patreon shares the 20% tier with three far larger employers on this list, yet its job count amounts to a sliver of theirs. A layoff of fewer than 100 people still removed one worker in five at a company of that size.

Patreon's email announcing the cuts rejected AI as the motive, stating that "we are not making the above changes because we believe AI replaces humans." The same message conceded ground with a second line: "AI has fundamentally transformed the tech industry, though, including how we work." Side by side, the two statements deny that AI can stand in for people while admitting it has changed how the company functions day to day.

Jack Conte laid out the practical shape of the overhaul. He described a flatter hierarchy, with teams refocused on the company's top priorities. Conte also wrote that AI has altered how Patreon builds products and communicates, and that this change bears on how the business operates and arranges itself.

The disclaimer and the concession clash on the surface alone. Patreon rejected the notion that software could take over the work its people did. It named AI as a force behind the way it works, and Conte's account links that force to the company's internal design. The leaner hierarchy he described leaves surviving teams with fewer managerial layers above them and a narrower set of goals in front of them.

Patreon's explanation keeps AI in the background and the reshaped hierarchy in the foreground. The record suggests the technology changed how Patreon works first, and the 93 exits followed from the slimmer structure that emerged.

Squarespace is cutting 19% of its workforce, about 300 people, in a November move that the website-building company frames as a reset of how it operates.

Jeff Seedman, a Squarespace representative, told TechCrunch the changes aim to simplify the company's operations, sharpen its priorities, and let it move faster. He presented the overhaul as a way to ready Squarespace for the next stage of its growth. His explanation emphasizes speed and focus. It specifies no teams, offices, or product lines, and it never refers to AI.

AI-driven "vibe-coding" site builders have shaken Squarespace's industry as they gain popularity, according to the tracker. Seedman offered no such link himself. The connection between that new competition and the job cuts reflects the tracker's view of the market, and no company statement confirms it.

Webflow, a rival that builds and hosts websites, ran its own layoff round in May without disclosing the size. A developer told the San Francisco Chronicle the round outstripped Webflow's last major reduction, which hit 8% of its staff in 2024. An employee called the cuts "a bloodbath" in comments to the paper. The tracker identifies a shift toward AI as the apparent cause.

Webflow CEO Linda Tong argued that AI is rewriting how marketing teams build, test, and refine digital experiences. She added that companies that seize such moments come out ahead. Her framing puts AI at the center, a contrast with Squarespace's statement, which leaves the technology unmentioned.

Squarespace's 19% cutback runs more than twice as deep as Webflow's 2024 cut. Six months separate the Webflow round from Squarespace's November announcement. Across that stretch, two companies selling tools for building websites both shrank, yet only Webflow's chief executive pointed to AI as a force reshaping the work. In this record, Squarespace's silence on AI leaves the tracker's market reading as the only link between its cuts and AI-powered rivals.

Lucid $LCID, the electric vehicle maker, is cutting about 18% of its workforce in a June round that extends to the assembly line. Full-time employees, contract workers, and hourly manufacturing staff all fall within its scope. Including contract workers carries the impact past Lucid's own employees. Lucid is the lone automaker among the companies on this list, so its reduction affects people who build cars as well as those who work at desks.

Lucid's production side absorbs a concrete share of the loss. IBTimes UK reports that the company eliminated the second production shift at its Casa Grande, Arizona, factory. Dropping a shift removes a whole rotation of plant labor, which matches the hourly roles the layoffs include. An operation that runs one shift in place of two has less room to raise output if demand climbs.

Chief Operating Officer Marc Winterhoff is leaving the company alongside the layoffs. His departure takes away the executive whose title covers operations at the moment the company pares its factory staff. Leadership turnover and a shrinking payroll converge on the same part of the business.

Lucid ties the layoffs to money and markets, saying they aim to "boost profitability amid growing competition." The explanation centers on earnings and rivals and leaves AI out. Groupon, Monday.com, Intuit $INTU, and other companies on this list linked their job losses to AI, which sets the carmaker's rationale apart. Lucid's case shows that a company can arrive at a cut this steep through competitive and financial pressure, with technology absent from its stated motives.

Lucid's round landed in a June that brought 14,000 job cuts across companies such as game developer Bungie and trading platform Robinhood. A vanished shift and a departing COO point to a carmaker surrendering capacity and labor costs to secure the gains its statement seeks.

Intuit $INTU plans to lay off 3,000 employees, about 17% of its workforce, Reuters reported in May. No later report confirms the cuts as complete, and the figure still describes an intention.

CEO Sasan Goodarzi told employees in a memo that the layoffs would help the company focus on infusing AI across its services. His explanation links the job losses to the products Intuit offers its customers, with AI woven through them. The reduction becomes a way to redirect the company toward that goal.

Read together, the headcount and the percentage imply a company of roughly 18,000 employees before the reduction. A cut at this depth would remove about one in every six jobs at Intuit.

Intuit's planned total outstrips the raw job counts at several companies that sit higher in this list. Groupon, Wix, Monday.com, and Squarespace each post a steeper share while shedding fewer people. Groupon and Monday.com also framed their layoffs around AI, yet their combined 1,030 jobs amount to about a third of Intuit's plan.

The tracker's entry names no teams, offices, or regions, and it gives no completion date. Employees in every Intuit division face the same uncertainty until the company specifies where the cuts will land. Goodarzi's memo points to the destination, AI across Intuit's services, but leaves open which jobs the company will trade to get there.

Snap $SNAP is eliminating 1,000 workers, or 16% of its staff, according to Variety, and the company presents the smaller workforce as a bet on compact teams equipped with AI. The April 2026 announcement removes roughly one in six positions at Snap.

Investor pressure shaped the timing. Snap announced the layoffs weeks after Irenic Capital Management called on the company to make changes that would lift its performance and revenue. The short gap between Irenic's request and Snap's decision places the job losses inside a larger argument over how the company should improve its results. An outside shareholder's demand and an internal technology strategy point in the same direction, toward fewer people producing more.

CEO Evan Spiegel grounded his case in what he had observed inside Snap. "We have already witnessed small squads leveraging AI tools to drive meaningful progress across several important initiatives," he said. His remark treats existing teams as proof that the approach works. In his telling, the gains came before the cuts, which recasts the downsizing as an expansion of a method he believes Snap has tested. The quote names none of those initiatives and offers no measure of the progress. Outsiders have only his word for how much the squads achieved.

Snap is using AI to streamline its operations and to support the employees who remain. The math reveals the load they carry. A 1,000-person reduction equal to 16% implies a pre-layoff headcount near 6,250, leaving roughly 5,250 employees to absorb the duties of departed colleagues.

Snap's leadership is wagering that a staff that lean, armed with AI tools, can deliver the turnaround Irenic Capital sought. The bet ties an investor's financial demand to an AI thesis Snap has never proven at its new size. Snap controls its payroll. It cannot dictate revenue, which hinges on whether Spiegel's squads keep up the pace he described.

Rackspace cut 750 workers in June, around 15% of its staff, as the cloud computing services company turned its attention toward AI. Read together, the headcount and the percentage imply a workforce of roughly 5,000 before the layoffs. About one in seven employees lost a job.

Rackspace's filing names a strategic decision to give less weight to some older service delivery operations as the chief driver of the restructuring. Most of those operations belong to its Public Cloud unit. The filing describes steering the freed resources into building out the company's enterprise AI offerings.

The company frames the layoffs as a decision about where to spend. Rackspace chose which work to downgrade and which to grow, and the restructuring centers on older public cloud delivery. The jobs it eliminated and the products it wants to build sit in different corners of the business. Staff whose roles supported legacy operations carry the cost of a bet on a newer line of work.

Roughly 4,250 employees remain to carry out the shift. Rackspace now relies on a smaller workforce to keep the operations it retained running while it builds an enterprise AI business that its own filing presents as unfinished.

The cryptocurrency platform Coinbase is laying off 700 employees in a May round that removes around 14% of its staff. A reduction of 700 at that proportion implies a pre-layoff headcount of roughly 5,000. About one worker in seven is leaving the company.

CEO Brian Armstrong pinned the decision on two forces, the current market and AI changing the way Coinbase works. His account pairs an outside pressure with an internal one. Market conditions sit beyond the reach of any single firm. A shift in how work gets done reflects a choice about the shape Armstrong wants his organization to take.

The dual explanation separates the crypto firm from employers in this selection that tied their layoffs to AI alone. Armstrong did not present AI as the sole driver. By naming external conditions alongside it, he linked Coinbase's payroll to forces the company cannot steer. His framing leaves room for two readings: some lost roles track how the market behaves, and others disappear because the work itself has changed. Armstrong's public statement does not say how many positions each force claims.

Robinhood, another trading platform, said through its communications account on X $TWTR in June that it planned to let go of 10% of its workers, around 290 jobs. Whether AI shaped that move remains unclear. CEO Vlad Tenev said the company would be "utilizing frontier technologies to push our execution even further." His language points toward technology without any reference to external pressure. Two companies in adjacent corners of finance reached for different vocabulary to explain shrinking payrolls.

Coinbase's cut runs 4 percentage points deeper than Robinhood's and reaches more than twice as many people. Armstrong also offered the fuller public rationale. Of the two, the company asking more of its workforce is the one that named more reasons for doing so.

Oracle $ORCL eliminated 21,000 jobs over the past year, roughly 13% of its workforce, according to the filing it submitted June 23. The tracker credits Oracle with the heaviest layoff impact of 2026 so far. The filing's one-year window reaches back into 2025, so the figure does not line up with the calendar year. Even so, the headcount sits in a different category from most companies here, several of which shed a few hundred people or fewer.

Artificial intelligence appears in the document as a cause. Oracle wrote that adopting and deploying AI across its operations has already led to reductions in its workforce and may keep doing so. The second half of that admission carries the warning. By putting that possibility on the record, the company signaled that 21,000 might not be the final tally.

Spending on AI infrastructure drove the downsizing, according to reports that describe the goal as stopping a cash drain from Oracle's buildout. The company has reportedly sought to offset the money flowing into AI chips and data centers. In that trade-off, payroll gave way to hardware.

The purge began in March, when early projections put the coming cuts in the thousands without settling on a total. On the last day of the month, more than a dozen employees announced on LinkedIn that they had lost their jobs, according to Business Insider. The notices those workers received pointed to Oracle's current business needs and told them, "we have made the decision to eliminate your role as part of a broader organizational change." The wording framed each departure as one piece of a larger reorganization.

Another round followed in September, and its size remains unknown. Employees reportedly received early-morning emails on Monday, Sept. 14, telling them the day was their last on the job. The September round arrived less than three months after the June filing raised the prospect of further reductions. Oracle's cuts now run past the 21,000 it disclosed, by a margin no one outside the company has counted.

Etsy laid off about 220 people, or 12% of its workforce, in August to simplify its organizational structure. Most of the losses fell on product and engineering teams. Staff in those groups design and build the company's technology, and the layoffs struck that technical core harder than any other part of Etsy.

CEO Kruti Patel Goyal called the layoffs necessary to build "the organization we believe Etsy needs for the future." Her rationale looks ahead and treats the move as a redesign of how Etsy arranges its people. The reported reasoning names no revenue shortfall, cost target, or investor demand. The overhaul itself serves as the stated motive. Several other tech employers tied their 2026 reductions to freeing money for AI, among them Meta $META and Atlassian $TEAM.

An Etsy spokesperson told CNBC that AI adoption did not drive the cuts. A handful of other companies have issued similar denials this year. Epic Games said its March dismissal of more than 1,000 people had no connection to AI. LinkedIn's plan to let go around 875 employees drew a comparable disclaimer from one source, who said the decision was "not for artificial intelligence to replace jobs at LinkedIn." Etsy's denial carries the company's own voice, which gives it more weight than a single source's account.

Dropbox's October layoff of roughly 200 workers, about 9% of its staff, targeted many of the same functions, with engineering, product, design, and general and administrative teams bearing the bulk. Etsy removed a similar number of people at a higher share, a sign that Etsy started from a smaller headcount. Dropbox co-CEO Ashraf Alkarmi tied his company's layoffs to choices about where to invest and how to organize, a rationale close to Goyal's focus on structure. Engineers and product staff carried the heaviest load at both companies. Etsy's case shows that a double-digit cut to those ranks can proceed while the company itself rejects AI as the cause.

Meta $META began cutting 10% of its workforce in May, around 8,000 employees, and plans to close 6,000 open roles on top of those exits. The company tied the reduction to one goal: freeing more room for AI spending. Eliminating vacancies trims tomorrow's payroll in the same way dismissals shrink today's. Combined, the two moves would remove roughly 14,000 positions from Meta's staffing plans, counting openings never filled alongside people already on the job.

A far smaller March round came first. Meta started letting go of about 700 people on March 25, The New York Times reported, and the losses landed on Reality Labs, social media, and recruiting teams. The May layoffs reached more than 11 times that headcount. Shedding recruiters in March and scrapping thousands of openings two months later point the same way, toward a company that intends to hire less.

Reality Labs, the unit that runs Horizon Worlds for the Quest VR headset, absorbed part of the March blow as Meta steps back from building the metaverse. Thinning that division signals a company redirecting its people away from virtual worlds. The same wave reached social media teams too, which carried the reductions beyond the metaverse effort.

A Reuters report in March raised the prospect of a much deeper reduction, suggesting Meta could cut 20% of its staff because of rising AI investment. The March round fell well short of that estimate. The May layoffs reached half of it. Even at the lower share, Meta's stated reason for the spring cuts, making space for AI outlays, matched the motive the news service had described in March. The Reuters report overshot on scale but identified the same cause.

Between March and May, Meta moved staff away from its metaverse projects and toward AI, and closed vacancies would make up more than 40% of the positions it is removing from its books.

Uber $UBER announced in September that it would eliminate 3,300 jobs, about 10% of its workforce, in a restructuring built around the distance between rank-and-file staff and the top of the company. CEO Dara Khosrowshahi cast the overhaul as an effort to strip out management tiers and steer resources toward growth. "We are removing layers, simplifying team structures, refining our global location strategy, and focusing our people and investments against the biggest opportunities ahead of us," he said.

Uber pinned numeric targets to the reorganization. The company said it would shrink the number of employees who sit seven or more levels below the CEO by 20%. It intends to cut its count of micro-teams by almost 50%. The two goals measure the plan by organizational depth and fragmentation, and the job losses serve those aims.

The cuts reach managers and regular employees alike. Each management tier adds a rung between the CEO and the people doing the work. Sparing supervisors would leave the ladder as tall as before. By including both groups, Uber matched its layoffs to its stated goal of a flatter chain of command.

Khosrowshahi's memo left AI off its list of reasons, which sets Uber apart from several companies in this selection that blamed rising AI spending for their cuts. The memo instead invoked "the autonomous future" as the horizon Uber is working toward.

Uber's July round, a narrower cut, carried a different message. Bloomberg reported that month that Uber laid off 10% of its customer service division as it sought to "embrace artificial intelligence" and streamline its staffing. In May, the company had said it would slow hiring as it leaned more on AI inside its own operations. The two rounds point to a split in how Uber explains its cuts. Bloomberg's account tied the support-staff trims to AI, while the September memo grounds the companywide reset in management depth.

Atlassian $TEAM let go of roughly 1,600 employees on March 16, around 10% of its workers, and CEO Mike Cannon-Brookes presented the move as a way to bankroll the company's next bets. He said the reductions would "self-fund further investment in AI and enterprise sales, while strengthening our financial profile."

Cannon-Brookes's wording ties each eliminated job to spending that comes after it. He named two destinations for the freed money, AI and enterprise sales, and attached no dollar figure to either. His reference to the company's financial profile adds a third aim. Atlassian wanted the savings to firm up its books as well as pay for new work.

Cannon-Brookes made no attempt to separate the layoffs from money. His statement names savings, new investment, and the balance sheet in a single line, a plain admission that cost discipline drove the decision alongside strategy. Other chief executives in this selection have distanced their job losses from budget pressure, but Atlassian's leader built his explanation on it.

A new internal blueprint accompanied the layoffs. Cannon-Brookes said Atlassian is "changing the way we work and reorganising around our System of Work to move faster." His remark pairs a structural overhaul with a push for speed. The headcount cut arrived bundled with a redesign of how the remaining teams operate. Fewer people have to deliver faster results inside a reworked system, which raises the stakes for every employee who stayed.

Atlassian moved early in the year. Its mid-March round landed about two months ahead of the May wave that brought cuts at seven other companies on this list, from Groupon and ClickUp to Meta $META and PayPal $PYPL. Atlassian also matched the share of staff cut at Meta and Uber $UBER while losing fewer people than either. Under the self-funding approach, Atlassian's AI budget grows out of its payroll savings. Its technology push and its headcount move in opposite directions.

CleanMyMac now helps you clean up Gmail and manage Chrome tabs

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

MacPaw today announced an expansion of CleanMyMac’s capabilities beyond the app’s traditional Mac maintenance and optimization tools. Here are the details.

CleanMyMac got two new features today, Email Cleanup and Browser Care, which are “designed to help users tackle digital clutter throughout their workday,” MacPaw explains.

Interestingly, both features focus on Google services, with Email Cleanup targeting Gmail and Browser Care designed specifically for Chrome.

Email Cleanup also helps users identify emails taking up storage space shared across Gmail, Google Drive, and Google Photos, allowing them to reclaim it once those messages are permanently deleted.

As for Browser Care, it “helps users reclaim Mac memory and stay focused by identifying Google Chrome tabs that consume valuable resources or create unnecessary clutter,” according to the company’s press release.

It works via a Chrome browser extension that connects directly to CleanMyMac, and lets users access “near real-time,” as MacPaw put it, “memory usage, pause resource-intensive tabs, find duplicates across browser windows and identify pages that have been left open for hours or days.”

In practice, MacPaw says, Browser Care makes it easier to identify which background tabs are affecting the Mac’s performance, helping users address slowdowns and reduce distractions.

MacPaw also emphasizes that Email Cleanup requires confirmation before making changes, while Browser Care only closes or suspends tabs when users choose to do so. The company adds that page content, titles, and browsing history remain stored locally on the Mac.

Both features are rolling out today to users with an active CleanMyMac subscription. To learn more about today’s announcement, follow this link.

IRS unveils higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The IRS has unveiled higher capital gains tax brackets for 2027.

In its announcement Thursday, the agency boosted the taxable income limits for the long-term capital gains brackets, which apply to assets owned for more than one year.

It also increased figures for dozens of other provisions, including federal income tax brackets, the estate and gift tax exemption, and eligibility for the earned income tax credit, among others.

Despite post-election stock market volatility, many long-term investors hold profitable assets after years of market growth.

The new capital gains brackets could impact investors' 2027 plans for sales or rebalancing, which can trigger taxes for brokerage account investments.

Here are the long-term capital gains tax brackets for 2027:

The latest round of IRS inflation adjustments for 2027 come as many Americans wrestle with affordability amid the rising cost of housing, groceries, gasoline and other living expenses.

The capital gains rate you pay — 0%, 15% or 20% — is based on which bracket you fall into based on taxable income.

You calculate taxable income by subtracting the greater of the standard or itemized deductions from your adjusted gross income.

For 2027, the standard deduction will rise to $16,600 for single filers, up from $16,100, and $33,200 for married couples filing jointly, up from $32,200.

Starting in 2027, single filers with taxable income of $51,050 or less and married couples filing jointly with $102,100 or less, will qualify for the 0% long-term capital gains rate.

A Definitive Guide to the Best Black Boots

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Out of all the fall fashion purchases you make this season, there’s one item that is guaranteed to give you maximum mileage in the months ahead: a pair of black boots.

For those who favor a sleek silhouette to elevate and anchor your outfits, a classic knee-high boot complements dresses and skirts across the board. If you’d like your boots to serve as more of a personality piece, why not test drive a Western-inspired pair, or one detailed with moto hardware? Of course, timeless shapes like square-toe booties and Chelsea boots will always be reliable go-tos; trendier styles like the slouchy boot still feel just as versatile as they did decades ago.

Ahead, we break down nine different black boot styles, from equestrian-inspired riding boots to all-weather lug sole boots. Find the right one for you in our guide to the best black boots of the season.

A quintessential knee-high black boot can be worn layered beneath dresses and skirts or styled over slim trousers and denim. Let your personal preference inform the height—flat or block heels are best for commuters, while a higher heel can transition from the workday to evening seamlessly.

If you’re not yet a Western boot convert, the easiest way to style these silhouettes is to contrast them with wardrobe staples that don’t feel cowboy adjacent. Whether you’re letting your boots peek out from beneath a pair of wool trousers, or opting for a pair with subtler motifs, there’s always a way to make them feel your own.

Saint Laurent Dylan 45 Patent Leather Boots

A traditional Chelsea boot silhouette is the workhorse of many wardrobes. While a classic take on this style makes a great investment, don’t miss modern interpretations, like the Row’s, which are cut extra low.

Manolo Blahnik Chelsa Leather Chelsea Ankle Booties

Whether you’re going for moto-inspired hardware or something with a bit of downtown edge (Coach and Vince, as examples), a silver buckle is the kind of detail that adds dimension while staying versatile.

Manolo Blahnik Calica Buckled Biker Boots

For instant polish, slip into a pair of black riding boots. This equestrian-inspired silhouette is both classic and distinct, ideal with preppy separates like pleated skirts and boxy blazers.

The Row Nobilis Leather Knee-High Riding Boots

Khaite, Chloé, and several other designers included the slouchy boot in their fall 2026 collections, highlighting how this style has shifted away from its ’80s associations and in a much more compelling direction (particularly when rendered in classic black).

Preparing for the winter means being ready for the storms ahead, and a pair of lug-sole boots is a must. The season’s best styles offer all-weather protection (and are a stylish snow-boot alternative), while still feeling versatile enough to wear on glorious days when the weather is agreeable.

Not quite an ankle boot or a knee-high either—the mid-calf boot is shaping up to be one of the season’s most interesting shapes. Need proof? Just look to the designers embracing it—from cool-girl favorite Maryam Nassir Zadeh to buzzy newcomer Jude.

There’s a time and place for a pointed boot, but a sleek square toe always manages to feel a touch cooler. Look for styles with a slimmer, subtler square toe, so you can style them for any occasion.

Tottenham chief reveals ultimate aim to win the Premier League as he insists club is on 'right track'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Tottenham have won their last three matches after failing to win any of their previous six

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Tottenham chief executive Vinai Venkatesham believes the club is heading in the right direction again and has outlined an ultimate objective of winning the Premier League.

Spurs narrowly avoided a disastrous drop to the Championship during a tumultuous 2025-26 campaign that saw long-standing chairman Daniel Levy leave, while Thomas Frank and Igor Tudor were both sacked before Roberto De Zerbi secured survival on the final day.

A summer of heavy spending followed, bringing 10 new signings and several high-profile departures. After a sluggish opening to the campaign, Tottenham travel to Leeds on Saturday on the back of three straight victories.

Speaking on The Sports Agents Podcast, Venkatesham said: "We’ve had three better results in a row, but there is nothing here where we think, ‘oh we’ve cracked it’ and we’re right at the start of this journey of where we are going to.

"There will be good moments this season and bad moments this season, but I firmly believe we’re on the right track to where we want to get to.

"Ultimately, I guess the best way to answer your question (about targets) is what are we here for? We’re here to win, right? The people that we’ve attracted to the football club, the long list of people, everyone is here to win.

"If you work for Tottenham, when we talk about being here to win, that means we’re here to win the Premier League. That’s what this football club wants to do. It wants to win the Premier League and it wants to be consistent in the Champions League and ultimately win the Champions League.

"Can I tell you what year that’s going to happen in? Obviously I can’t but that’s what we’re aiming for and that’s what I want every single person that works for this club to think about every single day."

Venkatesham also disclosed that preparing for relegation last season was necessary, stating: "To not make that plan would have been irresponsible."

Meanwhile, playmaker Xavi Simons is progressing in his recovery from a severe knee injury and should return to training in late November.

The Netherlands international suffered anterior cruciate ligament damage during April's 1-0 victory at Wolves, missing the conclusion of Tottenham's survival battle.

ACL injuries typically require a nine-month recovery period, but Simons remains ahead of schedule and is set for first-team contact training later this month after the international break. This opens up the possibility of a match return weeks before the end of the year, delivering a timely boost to De Zerbi.

Stare Straight Into Your Subject’s Soul With This Strange New Camera

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Stare Straight Into Your Subject’s Soul With This Strange New Camera

South Korean consumer electronics startup Lutra Inc. has launched a camera brand, Humans of Authenticity (HOA). HOA’s first camera, the HOA A1, looks weird as hell.

Like the widely-panned Godox C100, the HOA A1 says “no, thank you” to a viewfinder and, for that matter, a screen. The HOA A1’s rectangular frame itself is the viewfinder. If that sounds strange, it’s probably because it is.

"Frame is designed to be a viewfinder," HOA says. "Look through it with both eyes open, and feel the air. You can capture the scene just as you see."

As the company's few in-situ product photos show, the user winds up looking rather unusual, staring just over the top of the built-in lens at their subject.

Speaking of the built-in lens, HOA says it is a 26mm equivalent f/1.75 prime lens. It is paired with a 16-megapixel Sony IMX image sensor. The output images, presumably JPEG only since RAW isn't mentioned, are 4656 x 3104, which is about 14.5 megapixels.

Gratefully, HOA shares the sensor's size, which is a rather unusual move in the broader "odd digital camera" segment. It is a Type 1/2.53 image sensor, which delivers around a six-times crop factor. So while an f/1.75 aperture sounds fast, users should not expect a shallow depth of field by any stretch of the imagination.

That said, it's impossible to say anything about the HOA A1's image quality right now because HOA isn't sharing any sample photos, citing the need to "fine-tune the camera's color and tone in collaboration with photographers."

Time is running out, given that the company says the camera launches on December 21, "just in time for Christmas." Whether it's a fantastic present or a camera-shaped (?) piece of coal remains to be seen.

The camera's built-in battery offers up to eight hours of standby or 90 minutes of continuous shooting, per HOA.

Now is a good time to mention that Lutra Inc. also has extensive experience with blockchain and digital asset authentication. A core tenet of HOA's debut camera is celebrating authenticity. Subjects can see the photographer's face while they shoot; after all, this is about building connection to reality.

The camera authenticates every photo with "ShootProof," which creates a certificate that binds "image and data" at the time of capture. The certificate is recorded on a public blockchain. It's also anonymous, unlike some similar authentication technologies.

"ShootProof proves a photo is real," says HOA.

"Photography is endangered. Generated images are everywhere, making it hard to trust what you see," the company says. Fair point.

"That’s where ShootProof matters. It proves images are captured, not generated -- protecting photography’s credibility."

Photographers can also sign their photos through a "signature mode." Basically, photographers create a signature in an accompanying app; whether it's a legitimate one or a goofy little illustration is up to them, and the camera then slaps that signature onto the corner of every shot.

There's no way to know what this will look like yet, but it's an interesting idea. The smartphone integration goes far beyond this mode. The user's phone is the only way to view photos, although exactly how it works hasn't been detailed yet. Photographers will tap their phone on the back of the camera, and then some wireless transfer will occur. Photos can be captured without any connectivity, but Wi-Fi is required for viewing. HOA adds that the network is required to "safely authenticate" photos.

The HOA A1 First Edition launches on December 21. The first batch, comprising just 100 units, will be engraved with numbers from 001-100. Customers can reserve a specific number on a first-come, first-served basis. The HOA A1 is 360,000 KRW, or $289 USD. Preorders haven't opened yet, and interested buyers are advised to follow the company on Instagram to learn more.

Canva, Block and Meta layoffs in 2026 came with different severance terms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Tech layoff severance packages from Canva, Block and Meta show how far some employers go beyond base pay, from equity and laptops to extended healthcare

Sixteen weeks of base pay is a common reference point for severance at major tech companies. Outplacement figures from Challenger, Gray & Christmas and tracking by Simple Severance both put typical tech severance pay at around that level. Several layoffs in 2026 produced terms that differed from that baseline in the amount of pay, in what came with the pay, or in both.

A severance offer is rarely a single number. The packages in this list combine several parts: base pay, extra weeks tied to tenure, continued healthcare coverage, treatment of unvested equity, cash stipends or grants, company equipment and career support. Each employer assembled those parts differently.

Layoffs at Canva, Block $SQ and Meta $META in 2026 show that range. One company let departing staff keep their company-issued laptops. Another extended healthcare to employees' families. Block cut jobs twice in the same year, and the terms of its second round differed from the first.

Comparing tech layoff severance packages means looking past the headline count of weeks. Tenure formulas raise the total for long-serving staff. One company in this list adds two weeks for each continuous year of service, while Block adds one. Healthcare terms vary in length and in who is covered. Equity rules can decide whether a recent hire keeps shares or forfeits them. Some packages also include money aimed at what comes next, such as a transition stipend or a grant to start a venture.

The five entries that follow are a selection, not a ranking. Four describe individual layoff rounds: Canva in November, Block in February and November, and Meta in May. The fifth looks at the 16-week baseline and how the reported terms sit beside it. Each entry sets out the terms as reported. Where reporting on a round left out a detail, such as a headcount, the entry says so.

Reporting on these rounds does not say whether the base pay was delivered as a lump sum or as continued salary. The entries describe pay in weeks or months only, without assuming either method.

Canva promised every employee affected by its November 2026 layoffs at least six months of base pay, wherever in the world they worked. The cuts reached 22% of the company's workforce, which HR Brew puts at 1,271 employees.

The six-month figure is a floor, not a fixed amount. Each departing worker was promised no less than that much base pay.

Canva also changed how it treated equity for its newest employees. Vesting is the process by which granted stock becomes the employee's to keep. Many tech stock grants vest on a schedule that starts with a cliff. Under a one-year cliff, an employee earns no shares at all until the first anniversary of their start date. After that date, the remaining shares usually vest in smaller portions over the rest of the schedule. Someone laid off before reaching the cliff would normally walk away with nothing from the grant.

Canva removed that one-year vesting cliff for affected employees. Recent hires who had not yet reached their first anniversary keep their shares instead of forfeiting them.

The package extended to hardware. Departing employees could keep their company-issued MacBooks rather than return them.

Canva also put money toward what departing staff do next. It offered $10,000 grants to employees starting either a business or a community venture.

Healthcare was part of the offer. Canva provided one year of coverage "where applicable," a qualifier the reporting does not define further.

Job-search support rounded out the package. Affected workers received 12 months of LinkedIn Premium, along with career coaching and help with their résumés. LinkedIn Premium is the paid subscription tier of the professional networking site. It adds tools such as direct messages to members outside a user's network.

Canva announced other changes alongside the cuts. The company said Sydney would become the main base for more of its product teams. It also said some employees would be asked to move to centralized hubs.

Block $SQ's February 2026 layoffs came with 20 weeks of salary for departing employees, plus one additional week for every year of tenure. The package also included six months of healthcare coverage and a $5,000 transition stipend.

The salary portion has two parts. The 20 weeks act as a floor that applied to departing staff whatever their length of service.

The tenure component sits on top of that floor. Each year at Block added one more week of salary. A longer-serving employee therefore left with more weeks of pay than a recent hire.

Healthcare coverage ran for six months as part of the package. Reporting on the round does not say whether that coverage extended to employees' families. It also does not name the program or mechanism used to provide it.

The $5,000 transition stipend is a separate element. It was reported as a single cash amount, not as a figure calculated from salary or tenure.

Reporting on this round covers three elements: pay, healthcare and the stipend. It does not describe how unvested equity was treated. It also does not say whether staff kept company equipment or received career coaching or similar support. That limits any comparison of this severance package with others to the three elements that were reported.

Reporting on this round gives no headcount and no share of Block's workforce.

Block $SQ's November 2026 layoff package started at seven weeks of base pay. Affected employees also received one more week of pay for each year they had worked at Block.

The package had a fixed starting amount and a variable one. Every departing employee began from the same seven weeks. The tenure weeks then added to that figure. A worker with longer service at Block received more than a recent hire.

Block also gave departing staff a 30-day pay-without-working period. During that window, employees remained on pay without being expected to do their jobs. With the 30 days added, the November package had three separate pay elements: the seven-week base, the tenure weeks and the 30-day period.

Reporting on the round does not give a combined total that reconciles with these parts. This entry lists each element separately for that reason.

Healthcare for the November round ran through COBRA. COBRA is a U.S. federal law that allows eligible workers to keep their employer's group health coverage for a limited time after leaving a job. The reported terms name COBRA but do not say how long coverage lasted under Block's package. They also do not say whether Block paid any part of the premiums. That gap matters for departing staff. COBRA premiums are generally paid by the former employee unless an employer agrees to cover part of them.

The November terms differ from those of Block's February 2026 round. February's package started at 20 weeks of salary. November's started at seven weeks of base pay. Both rounds added one week for each year of service. With a smaller starting amount, the tenure weeks made up a larger part of a long-serving employee's package in November than in February.

The reported November terms do not mention a transition stipend.

No headcount or share of the workforce has been reported for Block's November cuts.

Meta $META's May 2026 layoff round affected roughly 10% of its workforce. Departing employees received 16 weeks of base pay, plus two additional weeks for each continuous year of service.

Reporting on the round gives the cut only as a share of staff. It does not say how many people lost their jobs.

The 16-week base equals four months of pay before any tenure is counted. Every employee in the round started from that same floor. The tenure weeks then build on it for each continuous year an employee spent at the company.

The formula ties those extra weeks to continuous service. That makes the length of an unbroken stretch at Meta the deciding factor in the final payment. A longer uninterrupted run brings more weeks of pay. A shorter one brings fewer. Reporting on the round does not explain how Meta treated a break in service.

Healthcare continuation came alongside the pay. Meta continued health coverage for 18 months. That coverage reached employees' families as well as the employees themselves.

HR Brew reports that the 18-month term tripled the healthcare coverage Meta offered in previous layoff cycles. The publication does not give the length of that earlier coverage.

The 18-month period runs longer than the stated healthcare terms in two other 2026 packages. Canva offered one year of coverage where applicable. Block $SQ's February round included six months. Block's November round named COBRA but gave no duration. Its length cannot be set beside Meta's.

Severance at major tech companies has typically landed around a 16-week baseline, according to outplacement figures from Challenger, Gray & Christmas and tracking by Simple Severance. HR Brew draws on both sources to set recent packages against that common reference point. It does not name a specific report or date from either source.

A baseline describes what is typical, not what is required. Each employer still sets its own formula for base pay, tenure credit and benefits. That makes the 16-week mark a yardstick for reading individual offers.

Against that yardstick, HR Brew treats three parts of Canva's November 2026 package as outliers. The first is the floor of at least six months of base pay for every affected employee worldwide. The others are the decision to let staff keep their company-issued MacBooks and the $10,000 grants for startup or community ventures.

Block $SQ's February 2026 round started above the benchmark. It paid 20 weeks of salary before any tenure credit. It then added one week for each year an employee had worked at the company.

Meta $META's May 2026 round started at the benchmark itself. Its formula paid 16 weeks of base pay. It then added two weeks for each continuous year of service. That formula lifts the total above the 16-week mark for anyone with at least one continuous year.

Block's November 2026 round set its base pay starting point below the benchmark. That package opened with seven weeks of base pay plus one week per year of tenure. It also included a separate 30-day pay-without-working period.

Weeks of pay are only one part of a layoff severance package. Comparing offers on that measure alone leaves out other terms. Healthcare terms vary by company. Meta continued coverage for 18 months for employees and their families. Block's February round gave six months of healthcare, and its November round named COBRA without a stated duration. Canva offered one year of coverage "where applicable." Block's February round also added a $5,000 transition stipend.

Ex-husband of Brent Sikkema sentenced to life in prison over murder-for-hire killing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Brent Sikkema at Art Basel Miami Beach Photo by Joe Schildhorn / BFA.com

The former husband of the late New York art dealer Brent Sikkema, Daniel Sikkema, aged 56, has been sentenced to life imprisonment for organising Sikkema’s murder in 2024. A federal court in Manhattan handed down the sentence on Wednesday (4 November).

In May 2026, after a two-week trial, Daniel Sikkema was convicted on charges of murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder-for-hire and conspiracy to commit murder in a foreign country. In addition to life imprisonment, he was sentenced to five years of supervised release.

Brent Sikkema, who was aged 75 at the time of his murder, was found stabbed to death at his home in the Jardim Botânico neighbourhood of Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, on 15 January 2024. According to prosecutors, Daniel Sikkema began planning the murder in 2023 in the midst of his divorce from the dealer, with whom he had been married for around 15 years and shared a son.

As the couple’s divorce proceedings became more acrimonious, Daniel Sikkema, who holds dual US and Cuban citizenship, recruited the Cuban national, Alejandro Triana Prevez, as the hitman. On the night of the murder, Prevez entered the late Sikkema’s residence and stabbed him 18 times. Prevez subsequently received $5,000 from Daniel Sikkema and was promised further payments.

Prosecutors said Daniel Sikkema made 11 payments to Prevez and Prevez’s partner in Cuba, using the identities of unwitting friends, employees and family members to conceal the transactions. Prevez was arrested by Brazilian authorities four days after the murder at a gas station on the side of the highway in the state of Minas Gerais, around 600 miles from Rio de Janeiro. He had stolen cash and jewellery in his possession. His case is being handled separately by a Brazilian court.

Brent Sikkema was born in 1948 in Morrison, Illinois, and studied at the San Francisco Art Institute before beginning his career. In the early 1990s, he founded Wooster Gardens in SoHo, moving the gallery to Chelsea in 1999. The business became Sikkema Jenkins & Co in 2003 following the involvement of Michael Jenkins and Meg Malloy.

The gallery is now known as Sikkema Malloy Jenkins and continues to operate in Chelsea. It has championed the work of artists including Kara Walker, Vik Muniz, Sheila Hicks, Jeffrey Gibson and Arturo Herrera. A representative for Sikkema Malloy Jenkins told The Art Newspaper that the gallery will not be providing any statement regarding the conviction or sentencing.

The founder of Chelsea's Sikkema Jenkins & Co was 75 years old

Daniel Sikkema has been accused of planning and paying for his ex-husband’s murder in Rio de Janeiro

Prediction: Valencia vs Real Madrid

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Real Madrid continue their pursuit of the La Liga summit on Saturday evening when they travel to Mestalla to take on Valencia.

Jose Mourinho’s side arrive in excellent form, having won seven consecutive matches in all competitions, while Valencia are hoping recent signs of improvement can help them climb away from the relegation zone.

Valencia’s season has been a struggle from the outset.

Los Che sit 19th in the standings with just nine points from 11 league matches and boast one of the weakest attacking records in the division, having scored only eight goals all season. Those numbers underline the scale of the challenge facing Javier Aguirre as he attempts to steer the club away from danger.

There was at least a welcome boost before the international break, as Umar Sadiq’s goal secured a vital 1-0 victory away to Elche. The success ended a difficult run and offered supporters hope that results may finally be turning in the right direction.

Now, Valencia have the opportunity to record back-to-back league victories for the first time since last season, although few opponents provide a sterner test than Real Madrid.

Sergi Canos, Diego Lopez and David Otorbi are unavailable, while Jose Manuel Copete faces a late fitness assessment.

The return of Sadiq, however, comes at an important time. After scoring the winner against Elche, the striker is expected to return to the starting lineup, while Harvey Elliott is available again following suspension.

Real Madrid head to the east coast as one of Europe’s form teams.

Mourinho’s side made it seven straight victories in all competitions with a dramatic 3-2 Champions League success away to AEK Athens in midweek. That result continued a remarkable run that has also included victories over Barcelona, Sevilla, Roma and RB Leipzig.

The Clasico triumph at the end of October was particularly significant, cutting Barcelona’s lead at the top of the table and re-energising Madrid’s title challenge.

Los Blancos currently sit third with nine wins from 11 league fixtures and know that another victory would draw them level on points with the Catalan leaders before Barcelona’s next outing.

Rodrygo, Raul Asencio and Ferland Mendy remain unavailable, while Vinicius Junior serves the final match of his domestic suspension.

Even so, Madrid’s squad depth remains formidable. Kylian Mbappe continues to spearhead the attack, Jude Bellingham remains a decisive influence between the lines, and Federico Valverde arrives buoyed by his winning goal against AEK Athens.

Arda Guler and Aurelien Tchouameni are also expected to return to the starting lineup after being rested in Europe.

Valencia should provide a competitive test at Mestalla, particularly after gaining confidence from their recent victory over Elche. The hosts have enough quality to trouble Madrid at times, and home support could help them produce one of their better displays of the season.

However, Real Madrid are operating at a different level right now. Mourinho’s side continue to find ways to win, possess significantly more quality throughout the squad, and are carrying considerable momentum into the fixture.

Valencia may find the net, but Madrid’s attacking strength should ultimately prove decisive.

Econ Review: A Look at October’s Key Market Data

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Editor’s note: Econ Review is a roundup of the housing and economic market data reports released during the month.

As the winter slowdown draws closer, the housing market remains in a sticky spot as the economy continues to face challenges trickling down from the Iran War. Now moving toward the end of 2026, the question remains whether we’ll begin to see a housing market recovery next year.

Let’s take a look at what the housing and economic data reports released in October can show us:

Existing-home sales saw a slight boost as the summer season finally came to a close, up 1.5% month-over-month in the latest report. Some regions picked up as well, with the South and West recording month-over-month gains.

Despite a slight monthly uptick, National Association of Realtors® Chief Economist Lawrence Yun noted in the report that the end of summer—August and September—marked “the two slowest months for home sales this year.”

As such, existing-home sales remained depleted year-over-year by 2.2%. All four regions also saw losses year-over-year.

Ali Wolf, chief economist at NewHomeSource and Zonda, said that the uptick was not an indicator of marker recovery as “supply is still pulling most of the weight here.”

“Today’s report shows a market clearing accumulated listings, not a wave of buyers coming in,” she continued. “And, this is the last clean read before mortgage rates shot up to over 7%, meaning next month we’ll start to see the impact on buyer activity.”

A positive note in the report was a slightly increased share of first-time buyers, as Realtor.com® Chief Economist Danielle Hale said that the market is “past the worst of the starter-home supply crunch nationwide, which may help entry-level buyers gain a foothold.”

In addition, existing-home inventory remains on the rise, growing 1.9% month-over-month and 4.6% year-over-year.

Construction challenges kept new-home sales muted for another month, falling 0.6% month-over-month and 5.3% year-over-year. Additionally, last month’s sales numbers were also revised downward by about 0.6%, dimming activity further.

“Builders continue to offer incentives and adjust pricing to attract buyers, but elevated mortgage rates and high home prices are limiting the number of households that can afford to purchase a home,” said National Association of Home Builders (NAHB) Chairman Bill Owens.

Wolf also predicted a potential increase in new-home sales in the fall and winter due to the incentives being offered by builders to “meet their year-end goals,” making it the “prime season for new home shopping.”

Looking ahead at potential activity, mortgage rates back up in the 7% range are starting to weigh on the market as pending home sales dropped 7.3% month-over-month and 11.3% year-over-year. All four regions also experienced declines on both a monthly and annual basis.

Realtor.com Senior Economist Hannah Jones noted that elevated rates are definitely “deteriorating” the market, and “this month’s deep freeze in buyer activity could persist until affordability improves.”

“The local data confirms that higher rates are impacting buyers throughout the country, even in markets that have shown consistent strength,” she continued.

However, Jones added that even though rates are straining buyer budgets, “the market is becoming more hospitable for those who can afford to participate.”

“Lower prices, a slower pace and more price reductions may help offset some of the impact of higher rates,” she concluded.

The latest Case-Shiller Index saw real home values decline for the 15th straight month as inflation continues to outpace home-price growth.

Home-price growth did see a slight uplift this month, advancing from 1.9% year-over-over growth last month to 2.1% in the latest report. The 10- and 20-City Composites were also both up 0.2% in their annual gains to 3.6% and 2.7%, respectively.

Rebecca Kaufman, associate director of Commodities at S&P Dow Jones Indices, did note a discrepancy in seasonally adjusted and non-seasonally adjusted prices (declines vs. rises, respectively), which she explained is indicative of a “post-summer slowdown” that “is obscuring some underlying strength in home prices.”

Kaufman also cautioned that affordability will still face challenges as inflation rises due to gas, oil and energy prices rising.

“Households continue to face elevated costs alongside persistently high home prices,” she said.

As for existing homes, the median existing-home price clocked in at $417,500, which is a 1.3% annual increase. This is a reversal from last month’s 1.2% annual fall, but still a lower rate of growth than historical peaks.

As previously mentioned, housing construction’s challenges have kept the industry segment fairly muted throughout the year, and this month was no expectation.

The latest New Residential Construction report saw housing starts drop 5.2% month-over-month and 7.4% year-over-year, led by 7.2% month-over-month and a whopping 25.9% year-over-year fall in multifamily starts (which had previously been performing well). Single-family starts also remained diminished with a 4.6% fall month-over-month, but did see a slight year-over-year improvement of +2.6%.

Danushka Nanayakkara-Skillington—NAHB’s assistant vice president for forecasting and analysis—noted that this month’s data “align(s) with our forecast of lower production in 2026, with further declines expected in 2027 as rates stay elevated and material costs rise.”

Despite starts remaining diminished, housing completions did see a slight month-over-month uptick by 2.2%. The slight monthly boost is likely due to a 14.9% month-over-month boost in multifamily completions. However, overall completions were still well down year-over-year by 14%, as single-family completions were down across the board and multifamily completions were down year-over-year.

In addition, the fall in multifamily starts may not last for long, as multifamily permits were up 5.9% month-over-month and 1% year-over-year. Overall, permits had the smallest fall in the report, down only 0.2% month-over-month and 3% year-over-year.

“The market is not conducive to building right now, and the diminished starts and completions figures make that clear,” added Realtor.com Senior Economist Joel Berner. “However, the relative resiliency of new home permitting shows that builders see these conditions as temporary.”

With construction reports still coming in well below historical norms, builder confidence remains fairly shaken. The latest Housing Market Index actually saw builder confidence tank to a 14-year low, clocking in at 29 (out of 100, with 50 being the baseline).

The “Trump administration’s efforts to advance regulatory reform” are, according to NAHB Chairman Owens, “helping to ease some of the cost pressures (builders) face.” Nevertheless, the administration’s immigration crackdown is “causing construction delays and discouraging legal workers from reporting to work.”

Price cuts and incentives are still being heavily used by builders to sell new homes, with the share of builders enacting price cuts actually peaking to a six-year high at 43%.

The labor market has seen a lot of resilience in recent months, but this month’s jobs report unfortunately weakened and lost some ground.

The unemployment rate—which has been unchanged at 4.1% for the past two months—saw a slight uptick to 4.2%. Only 29,000 jobs were added this month, which is below economists’ expectations in the range of 80,000.

Realtor.com Senior Economist Jake Krimmel called the report a “clear miss,” but noted that it probably wouldn’t change that much when it came time for the Federal Reserve’s Open Market Committee (FOMC) meeting.

“Despite tepid September job growth, unemployment remains low, so in short, this is no labor disaster that would pull the FOMC’s focus off inflation,” he continued.

Krimmel also pointed toward the downward revisions in added jobs over the past two months, explaining that the unemployment rate’s increase is not as severe as people may believe.

“Layoffs haven’t picked up, and jobless claims have been falling, so the low-hire, low-fire market is likely to stay entrenched as we head into 2027,” he said.

Speaking of inflation, the latest Consumer Price Index saw an increase as inflation remains elevated, now clocking in at 3.7% and core inflation at 2.6%. Krimmel noted that these figures “came in slightly hotter than expected,” and potentially point to a Fed rate hike by December.

The Personal Consumption Expenditures price index also recorded an increase this month, hitting 3.6% annual inflation and 3.1% core annual inflation. Navy Federal Chief Economist Heather Long said that “inflation is back near a 3-year high,” which is “largely due to war in Iran.”

Long also identified other major reasons for inflation—a “(b)ig rise in goods prices,” “service sector inflation” and “sideways” housing inflation as indicative of rising core inflation in general.

The Fed ended up not making any changes to rates, despite many predictions leaning in that direction. However, further rate hikes remain on the table.

Notably, Realtor.com Chief Economist Hale anticipated another two to three hikes over the next six to eight months, which would mean a high-end rate range of 4.5% to 4.75%.

Consumer opinions were slightly mixed this month, as sentiment remained flat while confidence saw an increase.

Sentiment’s lack of movement was largely attributed to the prolonged conflict with Iran. Also, despite little movement this month, sentiment has honed in on a 74-year low in the survey’s history.

Confidence, on the other hand, grew due to growth in positive perceptions of current and expected business conditions. This was attributed to the brief recovery seen in gas prices from September to October. Confidence does, however, remain on the downward trend it has been on over recent years, the report noted.

Looking ahead, the Leading Economic Index registered flat this month as the economy remains challenged due to the aforementioned elevation in inflation. The report was also not indicative of any recession in the coming months.

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.
