10 sectors where 2026 tech layoffs landed, from Oracle to Nike

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Tech has logged more than 200,000 layoffs in 2026. Here are 10 sectors, from cloud software to streaming, where cuts landed.

Picture opening your inbox before sunrise to learn that today is your last day at the company. Across the technology business in 2026, thousands of people have lived some version of that morning, and the pink slips have reached beyond the engineers who write code. Recruiters, lawyers, product managers, and human resources staff have joined the casualties. Many reports place the U.S. at the center of the downsizing. Anyone who works in tech, hires tech talent, or relies on a company that does has reason to watch where the next round falls.

Spending priorities explain much of the upheaval. Several companies have tied job reductions to heavier AI investment, and payroll has become the place where they find the money. Pushback has come from government and from workers. California Gov. Gavin Newsom unveiled a tool in June to follow how AI affects the labor force, pitching it as a way to ready the state through governance and policy. More than 4,500 Google $GOOGL staffers signed a petition seeking protections, including buyouts ahead of any mandatory layoffs and guaranteed severance. The calendar adds urgency. TrueUp counts more than 200,000 tech layoffs in 2026 with a couple of months to go, against more than 245,000 across all of 2025. By TrueUp's reading, this year's tally appears to be outpacing last year's.

Yahoo Tech maintains a rolling tracker of 2026 tech job losses, logging them company by company and month by month from March through its Nov. 4 update. Writers Katie Teague and Valentina Palladino assemble each entry from company statements, executive memos, social media posts, and outside news reports. Many entries describe announced plans or moves that other outlets reported, and several companies did not disclose how many people they let go. The tracker does not total or rank industries. This article sorts its company entries into 10 sectors, an editorial selection running from enterprise software to telecom and consumer brands. The order makes no claim about which sector shed the most jobs.

Oracle $ORCL shed 21,000 jobs over the past year, about 13% of its workforce, according to a June 23 filing. Another round followed in September, and the company never disclosed how many people it hit. Recipients reportedly opened early-morning emails on Monday, Sept. 14, telling them the day would be their last. Reports tie the September round to Oracle's effort to offset the money pouring into AI chips and data centers.

Intuit $INTU plans to lay off 3,000 employees, about 17% of its headcount. CEO Sasan Goodarzi wrote in a memo that the reductions would help the company concentrate on weaving AI throughout its services. Atlassian $TEAM dismissed some 1,600 people on March 16, around 10% of its workers. CEO Mike Cannon-Brookes told the team the cuts would let Atlassian pay for more investment in AI and enterprise sales out of its own savings while shoring up its finances. His framing casts payroll savings as the bankroll for AI, the clearest statement of the trade in this group.

Cloudflare is eliminating more than 1,100 positions in a restructuring tied to AI. Rackspace, a cloud computing services provider, let go of 750 workers, around 15% of its employees, as it pivoted toward AI. Cisco $CSCO is cutting just under 4,000 jobs, including 471 in the Bay Area.

Monday.com is dropping 20% of its people, about 630 in all, as it works to make its AI platform a core offering. Earlier in the year, the company built its main product around the idea that business customers want their teams working alongside AI agents. ClickUp CEO Zeb Evans announced a 22% cut as the company leans on AI to raise productivity. Evans insisted the move "wasn't about cutting costs" and pledged the savings would flow "directly back into the people who stay." ClickUp introduced million-dollar salary bands for employees who "create outsized impact using AI."

Salesforce $CRM axed at least 86 jobs in California. Workday $WDAY is laying off around 140 Bay Area employees to slim its tech team, part of a broader plan to pare its worldwide ranks by 2.5%. Seven of the 10 companies here tied their job losses to AI, through executive statements or press reports. In this corner of tech, AI spending competes with salaries for the same dollars.

Canva is eliminating 22% of its staff, just over 1,200 employees, as the design software company merges its product, growth, and platform teams. Reports place the overhaul in November and describe a push to reduce "subspecialisation," the habit of carving work into narrow roles. Fewer niche jobs could leave the employees who stay with a wider spread of duties.

Wix set a parallel course in May. Cofounder and CEO Avishai Abrahami announced on X $TWTR that the website builder would trim around 20% of its workforce, a reduction that should reach about 1,000 roles based on headcount. Abrahami pointed to the changes AI has set in motion and said the company needs to "become a faster, leaner, and flatter organization." He pinned part of the pressure on the exchange rate between the Israeli shekel and the U.S. dollar. Wix has its headquarters in Israel, which explains why the currency entered his reasoning.

Canva and Wix are each shedding about a fifth of their people, and both describe the goal in structural terms. Canva wants to thin out narrow specialties, while Wix wants a shallower hierarchy. Each plan redraws how the remaining staff split their work.

Webflow's reduction comes with no public headcount, though one employee called the round "a bloodbath." The word conveys how severe the layoffs felt to at least one person inside the company. A move toward AI appears to sit behind the cuts. CEO Linda Tong has said AI is rewriting how marketing teams build, test, and fine-tune digital experiences, which is the work Webflow's tools exist to support. Among the four companies here, Webflow's account alone comes from a rank-and-file worker.

Airtable let go of an undisclosed number of workers under its owner, Bending Spoons, the company that acquired Vimeo. Federico Simionato, a product leader at Bending Spoons, laid out the thinking on X as a "deep belief that small teams ship more and better." His wording lines up with Abrahami's call for a leaner company and with Canva's merger of separate teams.

Wix and Webflow both point to AI in this group, and Wix's CEO paired it with a currency squeeze. Canva and Airtable explain their cuts through team structure alone, with no mention of the technology. Half of these companies tie their layoffs to AI, and the other half treat compact teams as the goal in itself.

Epic Games announced on March 24 that it was laying off more than 1,000 workers, and CEO Tim Sweeney traced the move to a "downturn in Fortnite engagement" for its free-to-play shooter. His note to staff framed the problem as plain arithmetic. Epic was "spending significantly more than we're making," Sweeney wrote, and needed major reductions to keep the company funded. He added that AI played no part in the decision. One fading hit can destabilize a payroll built around it.

Electronic Arts $EA plans to eliminate 400 jobs, about 3% of its workforce, and the move includes closing BioWare, the studio behind the Mass Effect games. Shuttering BioWare will also cancel the next Mass Effect title. Earlier in the year, EA told investors it would rein in research and development spending as part of a push to save upwards of $700 million. A modest headcount trim can carry a franchise's next chapter out the door.

Xbox is shedding 3,200 roles through fiscal year 2027, Xbox chief Asha Sharma wrote in a post on X $TWTR. The first 1,600 people left on July 6, and the company said at the time that another 1,600 terminations would follow. In September, Xbox announced 268 more eliminations across Halo Studios, other first-party studios, and additional corners of Xbox Game Studios, all under a "reset" unveiled over the summer. The July and September rounds account for 1,868 roles, which leaves 1,332 of Sharma's total still to come. Soon after the July departures, Sharma joined a U.S. Federal Reserve task force advising on "productivity and jobs."

Sony $SONY Interactive Entertainment said in June that it is removing "a significant number of employees" from Bungie, but it gave no count. The losses reach most of the Destiny team, some members of the Marathon $MPC team, and SIE groups that support Bungie. Stripping out most of Destiny's developers leaves open how the series continues.

Ubisoft dismissed 105 employees at Red Storm Entertainment to reduce costs. Development has ended at the studio, which makes the downsizing permanent. Red Storm employed 180 people in 2022 and has weathered several earlier waves of job losses. The latest round alone equals more than half of that 2022 headcount.

Eidos-Montréal announced on March 30 that it would let go of 124 people, and studio head David Anfossi chose to depart. The studio's LinkedIn post pointed to "changing project needs" across production and support teams and said talent and performance played no role.

None of the six game companies in the tracker names AI as the trigger. The reasons they do give run to player engagement, spending, shifting projects, and a corporate reset, which ties the industry's losses to its own business pressures.

Meta $META began removing 10% of its workforce in May, about 8,000 employees, and it plans to shut 6,000 open roles to free up money for AI. The planned vacancy closures equal three-quarters of the dismissals. Meta is shrinking the payroll it carries today and the one it once meant to build.

Smaller rounds at Meta preceded the big one. The company cut about 700 people starting March 25 across Reality Labs, social media, and recruiting teams, according to reports. Reality Labs operates Horizon Worlds for the Quest VR headset, and Meta is stepping back from metaverse creation. Meta also reportedly set the end of May as the exit date for roughly 200 Bay Area workers, 124 in Burlingame and 74 in Sunnyvale. Trimming recruiters fits the move to close open positions. Both steps point to slower hiring ahead.

Microsoft $MSFT's July round hit 4,800 jobs, around 2.1% of its staff, with most of the losses in its gaming division. Buyouts form the other half of its approach. In April, the company extended its first-ever buyout to as many as 7% of employees. The one-time retirement program covered workers at the senior director level and below whose age and years of employment add up to 70 or more. A later reported offer reached 60,000 employees. Buyouts let the company lower headcount while leaving each worker to decide whether to go.

Amazon $AMZN's reductions arrived in scattered pieces. Reports put white-collar losses in its stores business at fewer than 1,000, with about 250 more in devices and an undisclosed number in its AGI group. A warehouse in Homestead, Florida, shed more than 600 positions. Amazon also eliminated roughly 30,000 people in rounds spanning October and January. The entries stretch from retail and devices to AI research and a warehouse floor, reaching corporate offices and frontline operations alike.

Apple $AAPL laid off at least 60 people in August in its Vision Group, the team behind the Vision Pro headset, and estimates put the companywide total above 200. The Intelligent Systems Experience group, which handles some AI features on Apple devices, also lost employees, reportedly because of architecture changes for the company's AI-infused Siri. An undisclosed number of Fitness+ workers departed too. The Vision Pro launched in 2024 at a starting price of $3,699, and incoming CEO John Ternus would reportedly put the category "on ice." Whether Apple exits AR and VR for good remains an open question.

Headset and metaverse teams surface in the rounds at both Meta and Apple, a sign that the two companies are cooling on mixed reality in the same year.

Visa $V is cutting about 2,600 jobs, around 7% of its workforce, and technology and product teams bear most of the losses. Efficiency drives part of the effort. In a memo, CEO Ryan McInerney wrote that Visa must "continue evolving how we work" and that "AI is also helping to accelerate this evolution and shape the way work gets done at Visa." Reports indicate that AI was one factor among several.

The card network intends to reinvest in consumer payments and value-added services such as stablecoin, according to reports. Visa is trimming technical staff while steering money toward newer payment products. The move shifts resources inside the company as much as it lowers expenses.

Coinbase is laying off 700 employees, about 14% of its headcount. CEO Brian Armstrong pointed to two pressures: the current market and AI's effect on how the company works. Coinbase stands as the lone firm in this group to name market conditions, which ties its downsizing to outside forces as well as internal strategy.

Visa's plan removes almost four times as many people as Coinbase's, yet Coinbase's share runs twice as high. The percentages put Coinbase near 5,000 people and Visa at about 37,000. At Coinbase, the cut reaches about one in seven workers.

Robinhood plans to trim its ranks by 10%, around 290 positions, as part of a restructuring it announced through the Robinhood Comms account on X $TWTR. The announcement leaves open whether AI shaped the decision. CEO Vlad Tenev said the company will turn to frontier technologies to drive its execution further. His phrasing points toward new tools without naming any.

The three companies acted in consecutive months, with Coinbase in May, Robinhood in June, and Visa in July. AI runs through the explanations from Visa and Coinbase, yet each executive paired the technology with a second motive, efficiency at Visa and the market at Coinbase. Payments companies appear to fold AI into cost calculations that predate it.

Uber $UBER announced 3,300 job cuts, about 10% of its workforce, and pointed the steepest reductions at the distance between employees and the top of the company. The plan trims staff sitting seven or more layers below the CEO by 20% and shrinks micro-teams by close to 50%. Together, the two targets describe an org chart losing its middle rungs and its smallest units at once. CEO Dara Khosrowshahi's memo on the changes made no mention of AI. Uber also reportedly cut 10% of its customer service staff in July.

Etsy followed a similar path with about 220 layoffs, 12% of its employees, to simplify its setup. Product and engineering teams took most of the losses. CEO Kruti Patel Goyal said the changes would help build "the organization we believe Etsy needs for the future." A company spokesperson said AI adoption did not drive the decision. At 12%, Etsy's reduction ran two percentage points deeper than Uber's.

Patreon let go of 93 people, 20% of its headcount, and tied the move to "changing our organizational structure and how we work." Its email to creators and team members stated that the company was "not making the above changes because we believe AI replaces humans." The same message conceded that AI has reshaped the tech industry and Patreon's own working methods. The email held both positions at once, crediting AI with changing daily work while denying it any role in who lost a job.

Groupon planned the deepest proportional downsizing among these companies, roughly 25% of its people or 400 positions, with the cuts expected by the end of the third quarter. The company set out to rebuild itself as an AI-native business. Of the six employers here, Groupon stands alone in making AI the stated purpose of its overhaul.

eBay is trimming 9% of its workforce, following a February round that eliminated about 800 positions. A company spokesperson described the move as a way to line up eBay's teams and resources behind its top strategic priorities. Zillow is laying off just over 500 people, and its CEO pointed to "a disciplined cost structure and getting more efficient" as the goal.

All six of these companies gave reasons for their cuts, and several, including Uber, Etsy, Patreon, and eBay, pinned them on how they arrange their teams while leaving AI aside. Across consumer marketplaces, reshaping the org chart drove more of the downsizing than automation did.

Netflix $NFLX plans to shed about 1,000 people, around 6% of its global workforce, in its largest round of job losses since 2022. Product, tech, legal, and human-resources teams will carry the bulk of the reduction, according to reports. The streamer has struggled to raise customer engagement and broader growth, and the downsizing lands amid that struggle. Legal and human-resources staff work far from the screen, so the reductions reach past the engineers who build the service.

Disney $DIS started its retrenchment in April, when Josh D'Amaro announced 1,000 layoffs two months after becoming CEO. Marvel followed by releasing 8% of its employees. In his memo, D'Amaro described the cuts as part of a search for ways to streamline operations across parts of the company. He argued that the fast pace of change in Disney's industries forces it to keep reassessing how to build a nimbler workforce that leans more heavily on technology.

Disney's next move could reach thousands of jobs, according to November reports describing a plan to lower annual costs by 20%. The company has not confirmed the plan. People familiar with the matter say the savings could pair staff departures with other spending trims. If Disney carries out the plan, the April layoffs would mark the first stage of a broader contraction.

NPR offered voluntary buyouts to 300 newsroom employees after losing federal funding, yet it will accept 30 of them, one in 10 of those offered. The news organization plans targeted dismissals if volunteers fall short of the target. Its approach resembles the buyouts Microsoft $MSFT extended this year. How many staffers step forward will decide whether NPR moves to forced exits.

Vimeo parted with more than 120 people, about 25% of its city staff, in its third wave of exits since Bending Spoons acquired it. Bending Spoons also owns Airtable, so one parent has pared headcount at two separate tech brands this year. Three waves under the same owner show the shrinking did not stop once the purchase closed.

LinkedIn plans to drop about 875 roles, or 5% of its personnel, to reorganize teams and steer attention toward growing areas, an anonymous person told reporters. A source said the move was "not for artificial intelligence to replace jobs at LinkedIn."

None of the five employers here named AI as the main reason for shrinking, and LinkedIn's source ruled it out. The reasons on record at Netflix, Disney, NPR, and LinkedIn run to engagement and growth struggles, cost targets, lost public money, and team reorganizations. Across streaming and media, audience and funding pressures account for the losses more than automation does.

General Motors $GM is letting go of around 500 to 600 employees in information technology, with reports describing the goal as a way to "trim costs and clear the way to bring in staff with skills in other technology areas." The automaker's rationale pairs savings with a change in expertise. GM wants room on its payroll for people who know different technical fields. The cutback shrinks the current IT group while it opens seats for new hires, making the move a swap of skills as much as a cut in headcount.

Redwood Materials, a battery recycling company, laid off 10% of its employees, or 135 people, in April, a month before the GM news broke. The two figures imply a staff of about 1,350 before the cuts. Redwood's chief HR officer wrote in an email that the company acted "to sharpen our focus, our work and the size of our teams to support the direction Redwood is going in the future." The message names no new direction and sets no savings target. It puts its weight on team size and focus, casting the layoffs as a reset of priorities.

Lucid $LCID, an EV automaker, belonged to a June wave of job losses that reached 14,000 positions across companies such as game developer Bungie and trading platform Robinhood. Lucid tied its own downsizing to an effort to "boost profitability amid growing competition." The phrase points to pressure on earnings, a different motive from GM's skills swap or Redwood's tighter priorities. No count of Lucid's departures accompanies the report. Lucid's slice of the June total remains unknown.

Each explanation in this group turns on cost, profit, or team size, and none of the three mentions AI. The timing runs in sequence, with Redwood in April, GM in May, and Lucid in June. Vehicle and battery companies kept shedding staff across three straight months without citing the AI spending that pushed cuts at several other firms.

Samsung is eliminating 179 jobs as it moves its New Jersey headquarters to Texas later this year, and estimates put the number of affected workers at around 1,000. The gap between those two figures shows how an office move spreads beyond the people it puts out of work. Many more employees face disruption than face dismissal. The plan surfaced in July, as Samsung joined the companies that cut staff after Microsoft $MSFT's round that month.

Reuters put the New Jersey count at 739 roles affected in some way. A portion of those employees received relocation offers, and others lost their jobs. Another 100 roles disappeared in Texas, most of them in the mobile division. The destination office, in other words, shed people of its own. The move falls short of a clean transfer of headcount from one state to another. The three counts track different groups, and no single total ties them together.

Intel $INTC is shrinking its Data Center Group and has disclosed no tally of the positions involved. The company described the overhaul as a way to become more focused and efficient, and it said it remained committed to its product roadmap. The roadmap pledge signals that Intel intends to keep its product plans on course while the group gets smaller. Without a number, outsiders cannot gauge how deep the unit's cuts run.

The Wall Street Journal reported in April that GoPro, the wearable camera maker, will lay off 145 employees this year under a cost-cutting restructuring plan. The reductions continue through the end of the year, which spreads the job losses across months instead of a single day. Among the three firms, GoPro states the plainest motive. Its plan aims to lower expenses.

None of the three hardware entries links its staffing moves to AI. The reasons on record are a headquarters move, a leaner data center operation, and budget control. The pattern sets this corner of tech apart from the companies that trimmed payroll to make room for AI spending.

Nike $NKE is cutting about 1,400 roles in its global operations, and most of those positions sit in technology, according to Chief Operating Officer Venkatesh Alagirisamy.

Technology staff carry the larger share of this reduction at a consumer brand. Nike's appearance in a tracker of tech job losses shows the pullback spreading past companies whose business is software. Alagirisamy's account does not say how many of the roughly 1,400 positions fall outside technology. It does establish that tech jobs make up the majority.

T-Mobile $TMUS is reportedly shedding more IT roles after a January round of layoffs. No firm count has surfaced, and reports suggest the total could run into the hundreds. The size of this second round remains an open question. Its target is not. The newer reductions land on the same function Nike singled out.

Both companies aimed their cuts at technology work. Together, they suggest internal IT teams have become a target even at telecom and consumer firms, far from the software makers that fill most of this list.

Ruben Amorim rips into his AC Milan players after embarrassing Europa League defeat: Ex-Man United boss hits out at team's attitude and criticises 'awful' performance

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published: 16:34 EDT, 5 November 2026 | Updated: 17:01 EDT, 5 November 2026

Ruben Amorim savaged his AC Milan side for their 'awful' attitude and performance in losing to Hungarian minnows Ferencvaros.

The former Man United boss presided over a 2-0 defeat in the Europa League on Thursday night in front of furious home supporters at the San Siro.

To make matters worse, the visitors even played the last 15 minutes with ten men but Milan were still unable to break through.

Amorim, 41, who was sacked from his post at Old Trafford last season and took over the Italian club in the summer, gave a brutal assessment of his team after the loss.

'When you play for Milan, you have a responsibility; you can't go out there like this,' he said at his post-match press conference.

'We're not ready to compete on both fronts in Serie A and Europe; we need to work on our approach to matches.

Ruben Amorim could hardly believe what he was seeing on a dreadful night for AC Milan

The home side suffered an embarrassing defeat by Ferencvaros at the San Siro

'There's nothing I can say tonight; we had to win; it was an important match. I can't say anything to defend the team; we need to understand what happened.'

He went on: 'Maybe I did something wrong in the last few days. Last year, the team had similar problems, and today we weren't ready; we didn't have the attitude to try to come back at all costs when we were behind.

'Tonight, we have no excuses. Whoever was on the pitch could have won this match. We have to resolve these ups and downs; we should have done many things much better, even just on second balls: that alone would have made things go differently.

'The first half, in particular, was awful; it's about ambition, attitude, and concentration. We weren't ready to move from the derby to another competition.'

Amorim's side are fifth in Serie A, seven points off top spot, and struggling massively in Europe down in 28th place – outside of the qualification spots for the knockout stages.

Even though Amorim made changes to the side that beat Inter in the derby at the weekend, he insisted the team should have had enough to beat Ferencvaros.

He went on: 'We're not mentally ready. The technical aspect has nothing to do with it. The rotation has nothing to do with it. You can't tell me that the starting line-up wasn't up to the task of winning the game.

'We gave ourselves all the chances we had, especially at the start. I take full responsibility for this, because the team is my responsibility, but we can't afford to do something like this, especially in the first half, in front of our fans, at our home. We can't do this; we have to improve, otherwise it's over.

'We want to do well in the Europa League too, we need to rotate and win like that too. We're not ready to play every three days now, but I certainly won't give up on the Europa League. We'll fight until it's over. It's a promise.'

Ferencvaros fans in the away end enjoyed one of the most memorable nights in their history

Amorim's results at AC Milan have been mixed after a positive start at his new club. He opted to take charge of the Italian giants having been out of work since his dismissal at United.

The Portuguese's reign at Old Trafford was disastrous. He oversaw the club's lowest Premier League finish (15th) after joining in November 2024.

And he only managed to win 38 per cent of United's games, taking 58 points from 47 games in the Premier League in total.

Amorim will hope the Ferencvaros result is a blip and AC Milan will have the chance to bounce back against Genoa on Sunday.

Sweden Returns Benin Bronze Trove to Nigeria, Indigenous Remains to Bolivia

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Swedish government is set to return 40 sacred artifacts from the Kingdom of Benin to Nigeria, marking one of the largest repatriations of the so-called Benin Bronzes.

Nigerian media report that the artworks are expected to be received by a delegation from the Benin Palace in Lagos today, a development that Oba Ewuare II, the monarch of Benin, described as years in the making. Since ascending to the throne, Ewuare has advocated fiercely for the return of bronze sculptures, ceremonial objects, and ivory carvings—collectively known as the Benin Bronzes—from museum and private collections worldwide, with considerable success. Related Articles Buddhas and Dinosaur Bones Among Dozens of Smuggled Artifacts Repatriated to China Met Museum Repatriates Five Pieces of Looted Cretan Armor to Greece

“We have been trying, through my grandfather’s efforts, to bring back our artifacts,” Ewuare said in a statement. “My grandfather succeeded, my father succeeded more, and I think now history is obvious to everybody. We are doing our best to triple and triple all those efforts of our forefathers.”

The Kingdom of Benin was looted of roughly 5,000 royal and religious objects in 1897 by British forces on a punitive expedition through what is now Nigeria. Dispersed through illicit channels among museums and private collections worldwide, the artifacts have become emblematic of efforts by formerly colonized countries to reclaim their cultural heritage, as well as the broader—and often contentious—debate over the ethics of ethnographic collections. In recent years, public sympathies have seemingly shifted toward formerly colonized countries, prompting a wave of repatriations. Museums across the United States, as well as institutions in the Netherlands, Germany, and Switzerland, have returned Benin Bronzes and other ethnographic objects to their countries of origin.

The Swedish government also announced today that it will return 77 objects belonging to the Kallawaya people, including human remains, to the Archaeological Museum in La Paz, Bolivia. The remains were taken out of Bolivia illegally and remained in the collection of the World Culture Museum until 2023, when the Swedish government announced their transfer of ownership.

Maresca not concerned by Donnarumma’s recent mistakes

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Enzo Maresca insists he has no concerns over Gianluigi Donnarumma's recent form, giving the goalkeeper his full backing ahead of their Premier League trip to Nottingham Forest.

Donnarumma was at fault for one of the goals City conceded in their 3-2 win over RB Leipzig in the Champions League on Wednesday, as he was beaten at the near post by Ridle Baku.

Across all competitions this season, Donnarumma has made three errors leading to goals – with one of those coming against Aston Villa in a loss that ended City's perfect start to the Premier League campaign.

City have lost their last two league matches, slipping to second in the table behind Arsenal, but Maresca has no doubt that Donnarumma will get back to his best soon.

"It's not a concern, but it's a mistake," Maresca said when asked about Donnarumma.

"We recognise that; he knows that it's a mistake, but now it's Nottingham Forest away, we need to be focused on that."

Asked if he was thinking of reviewing the position during the international break, Maresca told a press conference: "I'm not, to be honest. Seriously, at the moment I'm not thinking about the international break and goalkeeper situation because we are talking about a mistake.

"A few minutes before the mistake, he has made a save that any keeper in the world is not going to do. So, it's always a balance.

"Gigio knows that the first goal was not good, but, again, he's going to do better."

What a stop, Gigio! https://t.co/wBAIFIrooD

— Manchester City (@ManCity) November 5, 2026

Forest are struggling at the other end of the table, sitting in 19th coming into the weekend, with only one win to their name in the league so far (D3 L5).

Their winless streak stretched to five games after going down 2-0 to Brentford last time out, a result which prompted an impassioned rant from Oliver Glasner, who said the team needed to make changes.

He even went as far as to say, "If you don't want to follow, then I guess the sixth manager will come in", hinting at Forest's many changes in the dugout since the start of last season.

But Glasner believes that warning has had the desired impact on his players.

"I didn't attack anyone directly. We share that we want to be successful and win games. It was not a problem," he said.

"It was much louder outside than I felt it was inside. This is just how I see a relationship has to be. Let's talk about things if we don't like things.

"I can't blame somebody because if they don't know, how can you expect things to change?

"We are now in a society where criticism or the way I talk is negative, but the intention was positive and to change to get better results so that we can all have a better life."

Toronto playing for pride after missing out on playoffs

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Despite missing out on an MLS playoff spot, Robin Fraser says Toronto FC are playing for pride when they travel to the Philadelphia Union on Decision Day.

Toronto sit 12th in the Eastern Conference with 39 points, four behind ninth-place Orlando City SC, with their 3-1 loss to Atlanta United last weekend ending their outside hopes of reaching the playoffs.

The Reds had given themselves hope, losing just one of their five games prior to the Atlanta defeat, but they now know their season will be coming to an end on Saturday.

Fraser was disappointed with the outcome of their campaign, but he is still determined to make sure they finish the regular season on a high.

"Obviously, people are let down," said Fraser. "But I do think that from what we've seen, this pride factor is an important thing.

"There are guys who have different things to play for – whether it's contracts or longevity or starting positions – there's always something to play for, I think, as a professional.

"Of course, we want to finish on a good note. First and foremost is to win the game. We want to do all things the right way.

"If you're a professional, I feel like that should be your obligation. There's no other thought."

— Toronto FC (@TorontoFC) November 4, 2026

Philadelphia's postseason place is already confirmed as they sit sixth in the East, but a poor result this weekend could see them drop out of the automatic places into one of the wildcard spots.

After storming back into the playoff picture with stunning form after the World Cup break, the Union have slipped at the wrong time, failing to win any of their last four league games (D2 L2), including a 1-0 loss to FC Cincinnati last time out.

And ahead of Decision Day, club captain Alejandro Bedoya has revealed he will be retiring at the end of the 2026 season, after 11 seasons at the club.

"When I look back on my time in Philadelphia, so many moments come to mind, from the big wins and incredible matches to the challenges we faced along the way," said Bedoya.

"However, what stands out most is everything beyond the game. I came here because I believed in what the club was building and wanted to be part of that vision. Over time, Philadelphia became so much more than the place I came to play soccer.

"I'll miss this city and this club tremendously, but I take comfort in knowing I've given everything I had on the pitch.

"Philadelphia and this club have given me more than I ever could have imagined when I first arrived, and I'll always be grateful for that."

11 seasons for # 11 Alejandro Bedoya has announced his retirement at the conclusion of the 2026 season. https://t.co/RIWuWveQpZ

— Philadelphia Union (@PhilaUnion) November 2, 2026

Four of Neil Pierre's five goals this season have been headed goals, tied for the most headed goals in a single regular season by a Union player.

Tai Baribo netted four headed goals in both 2024 and 2025, while Cory Burke notched four in 2018.

Lewis Morgan scored his third goal of the season, and his second goal as a substitute for Toronto in 2026 in Sunday's defeat.

Prior to this season, just one of Morgan's 34 career MLS regular-season goals had come in matches in which he did not start.

With a 1-1 draw in August 2025 and a 3-3 draw in April, Philadelphia and Toronto have drawn two or more consecutive matches across all competitions for the second time. The clubs previously drew three straight meetings spanning 2012 and 2013.

The Union have scored more goals than any team in MLS (44) since returning from the World Cup break but have scored no more than one goal in any of their last three matches. They had scored multiple goals in nine straight matches prior to their current drought.

Toronto suffered their seventh multi-goal road loss of the season against Atlanta, with only Sporting Kansas City (nine) and Orlando (eight) having more.

Toronto's 33 away losses by multiple goals over the last five seasons are tied with the Colorado Rapids for the most in MLS.

Liverpool’s defensive errors ‘not sustainable’, says Van Dijk

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Virgil van Dijk insisted Liverpool's defence must improve, saying the errors they made in their Champions League defeat to Fenerbahce are "not sustainable".

Liverpool were beaten 3-2 at the Chobani Stadyumu Fenerbahce Sukru Saracoglu Spor Kompleksi, despite taking the lead twice on Tuesday.

Trey Nyoni's 25th-minute opener was swiftly cancelled out by Mason Greenwood, whose free-kick deflected in off Jeremy Jacquet after turning away from the ball in the wall.

Alexander Isak restored Liverpool's advantage three minutes after replacing Victor Munoz, but Fenerbahce responded once again when confusion from a set-piece saw Cody Gakpo flick the ball to the back post for Archie Brown to tuck home.

Fenerbahce completed the comeback in the 79th minute through Vedat Muriqi, who met Brown's looping cross with a towering header, despite being outnumbered by Jacquet, Nyoni and Alexis Mac Allister inside the penalty area.

The result saw Liverpool concede three goals from set pieces in a Champions League game for just the second time, along with their 3-3 draw with Sevilla in November 2017, though against Fenerbahce was the first time ever they have let in three non-penalty set-piece goals in one game.

Liverpool have also conceded 10 goals in four Champions League games this season, shipping three goals in each of their last three (6-3 vs LASK, 4-3 vs Villarreal, 2-3 vs Fenerbahce), the first time they have ever conceded 3+ goals in three major European games in a row.

"It's definitely not sustainable," said Van Dijk said of Liverpool's defensive woes.

"That's why it's very disappointing for many reasons, but definitely because of the goals we conceded and the manner we conceded them as well. There is work to do.

"Three set-pieces as well. We have been very good at them so far this season, but we switched off and weren't ready for the first and second balls. It's a tough one to take."

69 – Tonight was the latest Liverpool have ever taken the lead (69th minute – Alexander Isak to make it 2-1) in a major European match and then lost. Scuppered. https://t.co/OUAm9vxoyE

The defeat to Fenerbahce was only the third time in Liverpool's major European history they took the lead twice in a game and then lost, along with a 4-2 defeat to Roma in May 2018 and 3-2 defeat to PSV in January 2025.

And having gone ahead in the 69th minute, it was the latest Liverpool have ever taken the lead in a major European match and then lost.

Liverpool's defeat comes after they were beaten 1-0 by Arsenal in the Premier League, having won each of their last five matches across all competitions beforehand.

Andoni Iraola's side make the trip to Selhurst Park to face Crystal Palace on Sunday, though Van Dijk believes their midweek experience will only be beneficial for the Liverpool players as they look to sign off for the international break with a positive result.

"I mentioned after the last game [against Arsenal] that we as a group have to go through good moments but also less good moments," Van Dijk added.

"That will only form a bigger bond and it will only be beneficial. No-one wants to lose games, especially as we did, but it should only help us in the near future.

"We should not accept losing, especially in the manner we did."

Trump Convenes Final Review for Triumphal Arch, Seeks Immediate Start on Construction

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump’s planned 250-foot-tall triumphal arch could win approval as soon as Thursday, after he bulldozed a mountain of opposition to secure a final review by a federal commission. Approval would clear the way for construction to begin immediately, though a federal judge could still block the project, as some on Capitol Hill hope.

Washington, D.C., media reported that the Trump-allied National Capital Planning Commission began reviewing plans for the arch on Thursday afternoon, just two days after Republicans lost control of the Senate and the House of Representatives in seismic midterm elections. The commission is expected to approve the plans following lengthy public testimony. Meanwhile, the project’s opponents have called on the federal courts to halt it before construction can begin. Related Articles Arlington County Board Files Lawsuit to Stop Construction of Trump's Triumphal Arch After Resignation of Advisory Council, American Institute of Architects Weigh in on Trump's Arch

“The Court should not allow this lawless project to evade scrutiny,” Brian Schwalb and Jay Jones, the attorneys general of D.C. and Virginia, respectively, wrote in a joint court filing on Wednesday, as reported by the Washington Post.

The structure would rise at Memorial Circle, between the Lincoln Memorial and Arlington National Cemetery, to a height that critics say would cast shadows over the surrounding memorial landscape at various times of day. In recent weeks, the president has reimagined the project as a “military complex,” complete with a sniper’s nest.

Trump first announced plans for the triumphal arch more than a year ago, presenting it as a necessary tribute to the nation’s 250th anniversary. A project of this magnitude would traditionally require congressional approval, which the president has not sought. D.C. lawmakers, historic preservationists, and veterans’ groups argue that the project has been illegally advanced and threatens the site’s solemn character.

In a lawsuit filed last month, the Arlington County Board argued that the arch would “directly obstruct[ing] the line of sight between the Lincoln Memorial and Arlington National Cemetery that was deliberately created to symbolize the nation’s post-Civil War reunification.”

The Trump administration maintains that the arch is authorized by century-old plans approved by Congress to commemorate the nation’s reunification after the Civil War. Critics, however, have pointed out that the lawmakers’ plans called for a pair of columns, not a triumphal arch of the kind the president has likened to France’s Arc de Triomphe, “but better, more beautiful actually, much more beautiful.”

Mark Paoletta, a White House official who was appointed commission chairman in August, reportedly said on Thursday that the empty space at Memorial Circle “dishonors our veterans and leaves a key monumental gateway to Arlington that Congress authorized uncompleted.” He added that it was a “national disgrace” that such a landmark was not already standing, praising the president’s “historic vision.”

The Arlington County Board’s lawsuit, which names the National Park Service, as a defendant, accuses the administration and its official of failing to consider the arch’s potential risks to regional flight paths and its impact on emergency services.

“Using the Arch to store large stockpiles of ammunition and as a staging point for drones and snipers, so close to commuters and tourists, will dramatically worsen these public-safety harms and the risk of accidents,” the lawsuit warns.

United Airlines is connecting its two West Coast hubs to northern Michigan.

Next summer, the carrier will launch nonstop service to Traverse City, Michigan, from both Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) and San Francisco International Airport (SFO).

The two routes will launch on June 19 and run through the peak summer season, according to details shared Thursday by Cherry Capital Airport (TVC). The airport touted the service as something that would give TVC its "first-ever coast-to-coast nonstop footprint."

Traverse City is a popular summer destination, situated on the banks of Grand Traverse Bay, an arm of Lake Michigan.

The region is also home to the famous National Cherry Festival each summer. That century-old extravaganza — which includes an air show — will feature the U.S. Navy's Blue Angels in 2027.

United will operate its California service to Traverse City on Saturdays through the summer.

However, the carrier will also offer flights on select Wednesdays — including before and after the cherry festival.

United will operate a Boeing 737-800 on the route, according to the airport.

That aircraft features 16 domestic first-class recliners across four rows up front, and 150 seats in the main cabin (the number of extra-legroom Economy Plus seats vary from one jet to another)

While United’s Los Angeles and San Francisco routes to TVC are new, the carrier is no stranger to the region.

United already offers service to the airport from each of its other mainland U.S. hubs — including Chicago, Denver, Houston, and Washington Dulles International Airport (IAD).

On top of the two Traverse City routes, United announced additional new service from San Francisco.

The carrier will launch flights to Jacksonville International Airport (JAX) on March 29. That route will operate twice per week, year-round.

Traverse City and Jacksonville give United a total of seven new routes from its Bay Area hub in just the last couple of weeks.

Several of those flights are new transcontinental service, including routes to Hartford, Conn.; Richmond and Norfolk, Va; and Charleston, S.C.

Bobby Flay and Brooke Williamson Reveal What It’s Like To Have 2 Celebrity Chefs in a Home Kitchen—and How They’re Prepping for Thanksgiving

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Food Network chefs Bobby Flay and Brooke Williamson are getting ready for another Thanksgiving celebration at their beautiful Hamptons home, and they've shared their best tips and tricks ahead of the holiday.

In an interview with People magazine, the power couple said that their best advice is to start dinner prep early.

“Prep as much as you can ahead of time,” said Williamson, 48. “I think stuffing tastes better the next day, once it’s had a chance to meld together.”

Flay, 61, dropped another helpful piece of advice: “Have chicken stock on your stove. It reheats the sliced turkey, which ultimately gets dried out no matter what you do. It can also thin out gravy that gets too thick.”

While one might assume that having two celebrity chefs in a household can feel like a case of too many cooks in the kitchen, the duo said it’s never a problem for them.

“We definitely split up the work,” said Williamson. “It never feels like we’re in each other’s way. It feels like it makes sense.”

Flay and Williamson invited the publication into the kitchen of their New York City home, showing off the gorgeous cabinets and generously sized island.

After a recent trip to Italy, the couple told the magazine that they’re planning a Tuscan-inspired feast this year, joking that it’ll be a “mini ‘Brady Bunch’” scenario with their blended families. Flay is dad to 30-year-old Sophie, while Williamson has a son, Hudson, 18.

In September, Flay spoke about the home kitchens he has in his two residences—his Tribeca pad and an estate in the Hamptons.

“The kitchen is spacious, and I make three meals a day in there,” he told The Wall Street Journal. ”The space relaxes me and allows me to cook for family and friends.”

In his home out East, the kitchen is not just a place to whip up delicious meals. The room functions more like a full-fledged restaurant.

“On the other side of my sizable island are three French cafe tables for guests,” he explained. The setup is even equipped with a professional fryer, which he described as a “recent kitchen splurge.”

Among his latest kitchen tools is a utensil he’s relied on for years: “I still have the chef’s knife I used at [the French Culinary Institute],” he shared. “When [I] prepare food with it, the feel reminds me that I’m still learning every day."

The kitchen of the Amagansett home is his favorite place to cook and where he has typically hosted Thanksgiving.

Flay built the dwelling from the ground up during his marriage to “Law and Order: SVU” actress Stephanie March. The former couple, who divorced in 2015, hosted a turkey dinner for a staggering 50 guests at their first Thanksgiving in the home.

“I prepared two 30-pound turkeys and cooked sides for days,” he recalled in a 2013 interview with Elle Decor. “I stand at my kitchen island from the minute I get out here to the minute I leave.”

Although Flay has publicly discussed his desire to steer clear of matrimony after three divorces, he and Williamson have agreed on an alternative way to show their commitment to each other.

“She’s like, ‘I just want a big ring and a big party,’ and I was like, ‘I love that idea because my marriages have not lasted, unfortunately,’” he told People.

Explaining that the “most important thing” is to “make sure that Brooke feels like she’s committed to,” he recalled of the moment he gifted her a “giant diamond ring” during a trip to London.

Williamson describes the flashy piece of jewelry as “the perfect ring,” adding that “it’s very sparkly.”

Although she was initially thrown off by the gesture, she now sports the rock on her right hand.

“I thought it was incredibly thoughtful,” said Williamson. “He said just because I don’t feel the need to get married or want to get married again doesn’t mean that you don’t deserve the big, beautiful ring that comes along with it.”

While Flay has been private about his past relationships, he admits he’s been more open this time around, and cites all they have in common for his “confidence” about their partnership.

“I feel very confident about this,” he said of the pair’s connection. “There’s so many wonderful things about it.”

The October jobs report is out Friday. Here's everything to expect

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The U.S. economy isn't expected to have produced much job growth in October, with employers reluctant to hire workers but also not cutting staff amid a shrinking labor force and a growing problem of long-term unemployment.

Economists are looking for a meager nonfarm payrolls gain of 59,000, a slight uptick from the 29,000 jobs produced in September, according to the Dow Jones consensus. That result would continue a pattern of sparse growth.

But that's kind of OK: The forecast also is that the unemployment rate will hold at a very low 4.2% rate, the product of a climate where a stagnant labor force reduces the need for outsized job increases to keep the jobless level steady.

"Long-term, we're not going to need as many jobs added to the economy to have stable employment because we have fewer people who are available to work," said Nicole Bachaud, labor economist at jobs site ZipRecruiter. "An aging workforce is pushing a lot of people into retirement. We have had decades of low birth rates, meaning we don't have a lot of people who are coming in to replace those retiring workers. And immigration is now down pretty significantly, which is reducing some of that prime-age employment."

Evaluating the health of the labor market in such a climate becomes a moving target, Bachaud said.

More clues will come Friday when the Bureau of Labor Statistics releases the October nonfarm payrolls report at 8:30 a.m. ET.

"Do we have an economy where we have a lot of productivity but not as many jobs? Do we start focusing on things like wage growth? Is that more of a signal, especially for lower-wage workers?" she said. "I think that's the situation we're in right now because we're not going to go back to 200,000-plus jobs being added a month. That's just not the the scenario that that we're sitting in."

Indeed, payroll growth from January 2025 through September 2026 averaged just 35,000. There has been just one month during that period when gains topped 200,000 and seven months have seen losses.

Yet the unemployment rate has barely budged, up just 0.2 percentage point despite the marked slowdown.

During the period, though, the labor force has contracted by 434,000 and is down by just shy of a million since September 2025. The share of the labor force either working or looking for employment, known as the labor force participation rate, also has pulled back sharply, down to 61.8%, or a 0.8 percentage point slide.

But while hiring has been weak, other aspects show strength.

The level of layoffs has been meager, with initial jobless claims sliding to 189,000 last week, the lowest since July and hovering around the lowest level since 1969. Job openings also picked up by 333,000 in September, while the level of workers quitting their jobs — a proxy for confidence in being able to find new work — hit 3.36 million, its highest level in a year and a half.

Conversely, long-term unemployment has been a problem. The total number of workers out of a job for more than 25 weeks is up by 495,000 since January 2025, while the average duration of unemployment is at 24.8 weeks, an increase of 2.8 weeks, according to BLS data through September.

Federal Reserve officials generally consider the labor market as solid, but questions remain.

"If you have a job, you're fine, right?" ZipRecruiter's Bachaud said. "There's not a lot of movement happening, but at least you're employed. For people who are out of work because of the slowdown in turnover, people aren't vacating their jobs, employers aren't expanding and adding new jobs. That makes it very difficult to kind of break back in."

Bonfire of the Neocons: The MAGA Finger-Pointing Begins

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Good afternoon from Washington, where foie gras is now illegal and the local Stasi is going door-to-door to check that every citizen has downloaded Bluesky.

The town, one of the bluest in the country, is in a state of stunned ecstasy as it struggles to process the mind-bending midterm election results. Democrats won the House, the Senate, and a majority of governorships, penetrating deep into historically red strongholds like Texas, Kansas, Mississippi, and South Carolina. During a book party for Sophia Bush at The Jefferson hotel on Wednesday night, the actor nearly cried as she delivered a toast to the results—and to her new bestseller.

On the right, the finger-pointing has already begun. In this edition of Party Animals, you can read my dispatch from a night out with the DC Republicans in mourning and the Democrats celebrating in cocktail-fueled delirium. Plus, a MAGA civil war reignites over Iran, with Vice President JD Vance already wading into the factional fight that could determine the future of the party.

Also mentioned in this issue: Marc Thiessen, Curt Mills, Darline Graham, Ken Paxton, Greg Abbott, James Talarico, Annie Andrews, Mark Mitchell, Matt Walsh, and more.

Got tips? Hit the Party Animals hotline: aidan_mclaughlin@condenast.com. Or text me—my number is 917-817-8266. Complaints will be drowned in cocktail-fueled delirium. Praise will be run as a Democrat south of the Mason-Dixon Line.

The morning after the generational Republican midterm collapse that Party Animals is dubbing Blue Tuesday, JD Vance put on his political-analyst cap. “The reason we lost is the war,” he wrote in a lengthy X post. “The president himself said so last night.”

Never mind that Vance’s referenced war is one that his own administration started and is currently presiding over. Vance has often tried to cast himself as an outside observer of the Trump administration, its preeminent reply guy on social media and podcasts—a clever, if often ludicrous, pose for the vice president.

The Vance post was the highest-level opening salvo in a civil war that had been brewing for some time. It erupted into the open on Tuesday night as the results began to spell doom for Republicans and the blame started flying with velocity. The internecine battle is between the “America First” faction of the Trump movement and those who have supported the war in Iran, the latter labeled by detractors—with a sneer—as neocons.

The day before the blue tsunami, hopeful MAGA devotees attended a campaign rally in Ohio.

“The Iran War is the greatest political blunder in the last two decades at least,” wrote Matt Walsh in a post on X. “A catastrophically stupid decision. An unforgivable betrayal of America First principles.” As the dire results came down, even some on the right who had cheered on the war came to see, in the cold glow of their screens, the political devastation it had wrought. “Iran War is clearly catastrophically unpopular and has to end,” posted Will Chamberlain, who had spent the last few months loudly dismissing the war’s critics as subliterate simpletons.

On social media, MAGA luminaries shared a screenshot of an infamous op-ed written by Iran war booster Marc Thiessen, whose headline confidently claimed earlier this year: “There is no US downside to striking the Iranian regime.” Fox host Mark Levin lashed out at critics blasting his support for the war, insisting it had nothing to do with the results. Reality would seem to differ. One NBC News exit poll found that 74% of voters said Iran was a “crucial or major factor in how they voted.”

“It was the Iran war, stupid,” said Curt Mills, the executive director of The American Conservative, in an interview with Party Animals. Speaking about Thiessen and Levin, Mills was unsparing: “Professional liars. Professional liars. Not analysts, not honest people, and, honestly, not very invested in the future of the country.”

I asked Mills, who praised Vance’s statement, if it was not strange for the vice president to speak out against the administration while serving in it. “What option does he really have if he wants to keep his job, stop the war, and maybe run for president? I mean, it is strange. It is unprecedented in some ways,” he said. “Is it working? Maybe.”

In restaurants and barrooms across the capital, the ruling party’s apparatchiks watched and agonized as Democrats mounted a historic midterms rout.

But in the MAGA midterm wreckage, the idea of the manosphere as a political force has shattered.”

A Few Good Streamers: VF’s Abigail Sylvor Greenberg spoke with Hasan Piker and his lawyer, who promised that Piker’s $75 million defamation lawsuit against Scott Jennings is only the beginning.

BioWare are gone, but at least you'll be able to watch their final days in a documentary next year and oh wait that's really sad actually

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It feels like every single week for the past few years has been the infinitely quotable 30 Rock quote on repeat. That feeling struck particularly last month when EA decided to shut down the iconic Mass Effect and Dragon Age developer BioWare. That obviously resulted in the cancellation of Mass Effect 5, of which a leaked trailer and some, now former, devs made sound pretty interesting. But hey! At least there's a documentary coming next year all about the studio that managed to capture the studio's last days! Yeah, I'm not so convinced by this upside either.

As reported by Deadline, Shane Fennessey and Adam Scorgie will be helming a documentary about the beloved RPG studio for Canada's Super Channel. It's called, punnily enough, Massive Effect: The BioWare Story, and will apparently take a look at its origins in 1995 all the way up to its closure and a bit beyond, planned for release in 2027. Filming began two years ago, so clearly this was meant to be much more of a celebratory project than the funeral procession it's going to be.

Some big names from BioWare's history will be appearing in the doc, including its founders Ray Muzyka, Greg Zeschuk and Augustine Yip, alongside the likes of longtime devs like Mark Darrah, David Gaider, Drew Karpyshyn, Aaryn Flynn, Mac Walters, Trent Oster, James Ohlen and Caroline Livingstone. You can also expect appearances from Jennifer Hale, Casey Hudson and, as if his sole goal is to haunt this industry everywhere, Geoff Keighley.

In a statement, co-founders Muzyka and Zeschuk said, "When the production team asked for our blessing to tell the BioWare story, we were honored and deeply moved. BioWare was never just about the logo or the building. It was about the stories brought to life by the amazing people on our dev teams, and the fans who defended those worlds, championed them and carried them forward. We’re proud this film honors those who made it all possible. The best stories never really end. They live on in our memories and wait for someone to pick them up again."

Obviously the documentary won't only be about the studio's closure, it sounds like a much bigger effort than that, so hopefully it will be a way to recognise how much of an influence BioWare has had on RPGs. It's just a ridiculous shame that it's something of a posthumous release. Continued solidarity with all those at BioWare.

Keanu Reeves' Constantine 2 Could Be Dead After A Major DC Studios Development

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

John Constantine is about to enter James Gunn's DC Universe. Deadline reports that DC Studios has recruited filmmaker Francis Galluppi, writer/director of the upcoming "Evil Dead Wrath," to write a movie starring Constantine. But didn't Warner Bros. already have a Constantine movie in development?

Keanu Reeves played Constantine in the eponymous 2005 "Constantine" film. It's been criticized for its inaccuracies to the original "Hellblazer" comics (Reeves is often ranked as the worst onscreen John Constantine), but as a film unto itself, "Constantine" has found an audience who love it in spite of its infidelity.

Reeves himself has been pushing for a "Constantine" sequel, but "Constantine 2" has kept facing delays. With DC Studios moving forward with a new Constantine, it's unclear if Reeves' sequel will continue; Deadline's reporting does not mention the project one way or another. Compare how DC Studios has held off bringing in Batman to its new DC Universe, even when making a movie like "Clayface" that's set in Gotham City. That's clearly because Matt Reeves' "The Batman" franchise is existing in parallel with the DCU. While a DCU-set Batman movie, "The Brave and the Bold," was announced in 2023, there's been little movement on that project since then.

Two versions of Batman simultaneously existing already seemed like a risky move. Two competing John Constantine movies? Forget about it.

Co-created in 1985 by Alan Moore, Stephen R. Bissette, and John Totleben, John Constantine first debuted as a supporting character in "Swamp Thing." Just a few years later, he earned a spin-off comic, "Hellblazer," that ran for 25 years and 300 issues.

As for Constantine, he's a warlock, an occult detective, an exorcist, and a con man known for his acerbic cynicism and chain-smoking. (Noted "Hellblazer" arc "Dangerous Habits" by Garth Ennis and Will Simpson is all about John discovering he has lung cancer from his constant smoking and racing to save himself.) While initially presented as part of the main DC Universe, "Hellblazer," published by DC's Vertigo imprint, gradually drifted into its own separate world.

But then, DC Comics' 2011 reboot "The New 52" broke down those walls again. Suddenly, John was joining the Justice League Dark, a team for DC's supernatural superheroes. That integration carried over outside the comics. Welsh actor Matt Ryan played Constantine in a short-lived NBC series in 2014 — but that was only the beginning. Ryan guest-starred as John in Season 4 of "Arrow" and then became a regular on "Legends of Tomorrow." He also became the character's official voice actor in animation, beginning with a 2017 "Justice League Dark" animated movie.

This early, it's unclear who DC Studios could have in mind to play John Constantine, though actor Bill Skarsgård has put himself in contention. It's worth noting that DC Studios currently has a "Swamp Thing" movie, directed by James Mangold, in the works. This is pure speculation, but it wouldn't be out of place for John to show up in that.

Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

Google Find Hub rolls out ‘Notify when left behind’ tracker alerts on Android

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

As previewed in October, Google has added “Notify when left behind” alerts for Find Hub tags and trackers on Android.

To enable, open the Find Hub app and choose a tag. In the top-right corner of the bottom sheet, select the settings gear icon.

There is a new “Notify when left behind” section to “Receive a notification when this tag is separated from your phone.” By default, there’s an exception for the Home address you’ve stored in your Google Account (Maps).

We’re seeing this notification option with Chipolo trackers and the Moto Tag today with version 3.1.787-06 of Find Hub for Android for both US and European users. It does not appear for other device types, like headphones.

This is a basic capability that Find Hub should have really offered for devices on the network since the beginning.

Google first mentioned this “Left-Behind Reminders“ capability in the release notes for Google Play services. This should also be surfaced as part of a “new registration flow” for devices, along with setting category icons (Bike, Luggage, Wallet, etc.) and renaming tags. Google also mentioned upcoming NFC support that lets you “quickly access Find Hub tag details and settings or identify lost tags when you tap the tag with your phone.”
